Arts and Music

Viola

Needs review

Original description: A bowed string instrument tuned C–G–D–A, pitched between the violin and cello, and commonly played in alto clef as an inner or alto voice in ensembles.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-011b939c1f8cfb0bd703 · Estimated cost: $0.0247

Overview

The viola is a bowed string instrument in the violin family, held against the shoulder and played with a bow or by plucking its strings. Larger and lower in sound than the violin, it fills the family’s middle, or alto, register between violin and cello. Its four strings are tuned C–G–D–A, each a perfect fifth apart; the lower strings give it a darker, richer tone. In orchestras and chamber groups, violas support the harmony and connect higher and lower parts, but they can also play melodies and solo passages. Viola music is usually written in alto clef. Historically, “viola” named different instruments, so viola da braccio and viola da gamba are not interchangeable terms.

Sources: https://philharmonia.co.uk/resources/instruments/viola/, https://omeka-s.grinnell.edu/s/MusicalInstruments/item/655, https://www.vsl.co.at/academy/strings/viola

Key facts

  1. The viola’s standard tuning, from lowest to highest, is C–G–D–A.Sources: https://philharmonia.co.uk/resources/instruments/viola/, https://omeka-s.grinnell.edu/s/MusicalInstruments/item/655
  2. Viola parts are typically written in alto clef; treble clef may be used for higher passages.Sources: https://omeka-s.grinnell.edu/s/MusicalInstruments/item/655, https://www.vsl.co.at/academy/strings/viola
  3. Its middle-register role makes the viola a regular part of both orchestras and chamber ensembles.Sources: https://omeka-s.grinnell.edu/s/MusicalInstruments/item/655, https://www.vsl.co.at/academy/strings/viola
  4. The viola’s tone is generally darker and less brilliant than the violin’s, while still allowing agile playing and solo passages.Sources: https://philharmonia.co.uk/resources/instruments/viola/, https://omeka-s.grinnell.edu/s/MusicalInstruments/item/655
  5. In historical usage, viola da braccio and viola da gamba referred to distinct instrument traditions; the modern viola belongs to the violin-family line associated with viola da braccio.Sources: https://www.vsl.co.at/academy/strings/viola, https://omeka-s.grinnell.edu/s/MusicalInstruments/item/655

Harpsichord

Needs review

Original description: A plucked-string keyboard instrument in which keys activate jacks to pluck strings; it was prominent in Renaissance and Baroque music and preceded the modern piano.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-dcd50fab90f2afc6b9b8 · Estimated cost: $0.0247

Overview

The harpsichord is a keyboard instrument whose keys lift vertical jacks; a plectrum on each jack plucks a string, producing a bright, crisp sound. In its grand form, its strings run front to back, unlike the horizontally strung virginal or spinet. Pressing a key harder does not make a harpsichord note louder, so it differs from the piano, which uses hammers to strike strings and allows changes in loudness through touch. Harpsichords were played in Renaissance households and became important in Baroque music, both as solo instruments and in basso continuo, where they supplied chords beneath a melody.

Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/es/essays/renaissance-keyboards, https://www.metmuseum.org/fr/perspectives/inventing-cristofori-piano, https://www.vam.ac.uk/articles/renaissance-music, https://visitmuseum.gencat.cat/en/museum/museu-de-la-musica-de-barcelona/tour/la-nova-musica-el-sentit-harmonic-del-barroc

Key facts

  1. When a key is pressed, its jack rises and a plectrum plucks the string; as the jack falls, the plectrum pivots aside and a damper silences the string.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/es/essays/renaissance-keyboards
  2. The harpsichord’s sound is often described as light, bright, and crisp.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/es/essays/renaissance-keyboards
  3. Players cannot vary a note’s loudness just by pressing its key harder; the piano instead strikes strings with hammers.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/es/essays/renaissance-keyboards, https://www.metmuseum.org/fr/perspectives/inventing-cristofori-piano
  4. Harpsichords and spinets were among the keyboard instruments found in Renaissance households.Sources: https://www.vam.ac.uk/articles/renaissance-music
  5. In Baroque music, the harpsichord could serve as a solo instrument or provide basso continuo: chords accompanying a melody and reinforced by a bass instrument.Sources: https://visitmuseum.gencat.cat/en/museum/museu-de-la-musica-de-barcelona/tour/la-nova-musica-el-sentit-harmonic-del-barroc
Source reference details
  • Renaissance Keyboards — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    Jack-and-plectrum action, sound, string orientation in the grand harpsichord form, and the inability to change loudness by key pressure.
  • Spotlight: Inventing the Piano — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    Contrast between the harpsichord’s plucking mechanism and the piano’s hammer mechanism, developed for greater expressive control.
  • Renaissance music — Victoria and Albert Museum
    Harpsichords and spinets as household instruments during the Renaissance.
  • The new music: the harmonic sense of the Baroque — Visitmuseum, Government of Catalonia; Barcelona Music Museum
    The harpsichord’s Baroque role as a solo instrument and basso continuo instrument.

Surrealism

Needs review

Original description: A 20th-century avant-garde art and literary movement exploring dreams, the unconscious, and unexpected or fantastical juxtapositions.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-bc6ae6fbc034800ffecd · Estimated cost: $0.0357

Overview

Surrealism is an international literary and arts movement that took shape in 1920s Paris, with writer André Breton as a leading organizer; Breton’s 1924 manifesto helped define its aims. Influenced by Sigmund Freud’s ideas about dreams and the unconscious, Surrealists sought to loosen the control of reason and social convention so hidden thoughts and imaginative associations could surface. They worked in poetry, painting, drawing, photography, film, and design, using methods such as automatic writing or drawing, chance, collage, and startling combinations of familiar things. There was no single Surrealist look: some artists painted precise, dreamlike scenes, while others made spontaneous or abstract forms. René Magritte and Salvador Dalí became prominent visual members.

Sources: https://www.nga.gov/surrealism, https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/surrealism, https://www.vam.ac.uk/articles/surrealism-and-design

Key facts

  1. Breton’s Manifesto of Surrealism appeared in Paris in 1924, helping establish the movement as an international literary, artistic, and political force.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/surrealism
  2. Surrealist automatism meant creating writing or art with reduced conscious control; artists also used free association and chance.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/surrealism, https://www.vam.ac.uk/articles/surrealism-and-design
  3. Surrealism drew on Freud’s ideas about dreams and the unconscious, but its artists used them as creative inspiration—not as a single required subject or style.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/surrealism, https://www.vam.ac.uk/articles/surrealism
  4. Surrealists made work in many forms, including literature, painting, photography, film, design, and objects assembled from existing things.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/surrealism, https://www.vam.ac.uk/articles/surrealism
  5. René Magritte and Salvador Dalí were prominent visual Surrealists; their work illustrates the movement’s range, from carefully rendered, uncanny scenes to hallucinatory imagery.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/surrealism
Source reference details

    Surrealism’s development in 1920s Paris, André Breton’s role, its inspiration from dreams and the unconscious, and its international reach.
  • Surrealism — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    The movement’s literary origins, Breton’s 1924 manifesto, influence of Freud and free association, varied techniques and media, and artists including Magritte and Dalí.
  • Surrealism and design — Victoria and Albert Museum
    Surrealism’s exploration of dreams, the unconscious, and the irrational; automatic techniques; and its use in collage, objects, design, theatre, and film.

Human voice

Needs review

Original description: The human voice is the sound-producing instrument used for speech, singing, choral music, and other vocal performances.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-91450303f98f5928f72d · Estimated cost: $0.0132

Overview

The human voice is the body’s sound-making instrument: air from the lungs passes through the larynx, where paired vocal folds vibrate to create sound. People use that sound for speech, singing, humming, cries, and other vocal expression. Voice is not identical to speech: it is the sound source, while coordinated movements of the tongue and lips shape it into recognizable speech sounds. The throat, mouth, and nose also modify the sound through resonance. The vocal folds and resonating spaces help determine pitch, volume, and tone, contributing to why voices differ. Because it can communicate words and carry melody, the voice is central to communication and music.

Sources: https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/taking-care-your-voice, https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/news/multimedia/voice-speech-language-what-are-they-text-version, https://www.asha.org/practice-portal/clinical-topics/resonance-disorders/

Key facts

  1. The vocal folds are a pair of tissue bands in the larynx; they are open during quiet breathing and vibrate as air from the lungs passes them during voice production.Sources: https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/taking-care-your-voice
  2. Voice is the sound source; speech forms recognizable sounds by coordinating movements of the tongue, lips, and other parts of the vocal tract.Sources: https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/news/multimedia/voice-speech-language-what-are-they-text-version, https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/news/multimedia/how-does-human-body-produce-voice-and-speech-text-version
  3. The throat, mouth, and nose act as resonating spaces that modify vocal sound; their size and shape help influence voice quality.Sources: https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/taking-care-your-voice, https://www.asha.org/practice-portal/clinical-topics/resonance-disorders/
  4. Pitch, volume, and tone are among the qualities that help make voices sound different.Sources: https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/taking-care-your-voice
  5. People use the voice for activities including speaking and singing, as well as vocal sounds such as humming and crying.Sources: https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/health/taking-care-your-voice, https://www.nidcd.nih.gov/news/multimedia/voice-speech-language-what-are-they-text-version
Source reference details
  • Taking Care of Your Voice — National Institute on Deafness and Other Communication Disorders (NIDCD), National Institutes of Health
    Explains vocal-fold vibration, airflow, resonance, voice qualities, and voice use including singing.
  • Voice, Speech, and Language: What are they?: Text Version — National Institute on Deafness and Other Communication Disorders (NIDCD), National Institutes of Health
    Distinguishes voice from speech and describes voice production and examples of vocalization, including humming and crying.
  • Resonance Disorders — American Speech-Language-Hearing Association (ASHA)
    Describes how the vocal tract and its oral, nasal, and pharyngeal spaces modify sound through resonance.

Francisco Goya

Needs review

Original description: Spanish painter and printmaker whose works include the Black Paintings, Los Caprichos, The Disasters of War, and The Third of May 1808.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-b648bf27885bad80a79e · Estimated cost: $0.0242

Overview

Francisco José de Goya y Lucientes (1746–1828) was a Spanish painter and printmaker whose long career ranged from royal portraits and lively scenes to stark, unsettling images. He became a court painter, but is especially remembered for works confronting superstition and the human cost of war. His 1799 etching series Los Caprichos used satire and fantasy; The Disasters of War depicted atrocities during Spain’s struggle against French occupation; and The Third of May 1808 shows captured Spaniards facing a French firing squad. Later, at his country house, Quinta del Sordo, he painted the private murals now known as the Black Paintings.

Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/pt/essays/francisco-de-goya-1746-1828-and-the-spanish-enlightenment, https://www.nga.gov/artists/1353-francisco-goya

Key facts

  1. Goya became completely deaf after an illness in the early 1790s.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/pt/essays/francisco-de-goya-1746-1828-and-the-spanish-enlightenment
  2. He completed and published Los Caprichos in 1799; the series contains 80 allegorical etchings.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/pt/essays/francisco-de-goya-1746-1828-and-the-spanish-enlightenment
  3. The Disasters of War prints were made between 1810 and 1820 and were not published until after Goya’s death.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/pt/essays/francisco-de-goya-1746-1828-and-the-spanish-enlightenment
  4. The Black Paintings were private works made at Quinta del Sordo between 1820 and 1823.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/pt/essays/francisco-de-goya-1746-1828-and-the-spanish-enlightenment
  5. The National Gallery of Art lists Goya as Spanish and gives his lifespan as 1746–1828.Sources: https://www.nga.gov/artists/1353-francisco-goya
Source reference details
  • Francisco de Goya (1746–1828) and the Spanish Enlightenment — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    Goya’s career, court role, deafness, Los Caprichos, The Third of May 1808, The Disasters of War, and the Black Paintings, including the dates, subject matter, and setting of these works.
  • Francisco Goya — National Gallery of Art
    Goya’s identity, nationality, lifespan, and examples of his portraits and prints in the Gallery’s collection.

The Anatomy Lesson of Dr. Nicolaes Tulp

Needs review

Original description: Rembrandt’s 1632 group portrait depicting Nicolaes Tulp conducting an anatomical dissection before fellow surgeons.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-c182eb7b74ada6642f86 · Estimated cost: $0.0251

Overview

Rembrandt van Rijn’s *The Anatomy Lesson of Dr Nicolaes Tulp* is a 1632 group portrait commissioned to show Amsterdam surgeons with Dr Tulp teaching anatomy. Tulp demonstrates the muscles of a dissected forearm while the other surgeons look in different directions, making the gathering feel active rather than like a stiff row of sitters. Strong light-and-dark contrasts help focus attention on the figures. The body is identified as Adriaen Adriaensz, known as Aris Kindt, who was hanged before the dissection. Although the scene depicts an anatomy lesson, medical historians note it should not be treated as an exact record of the dissection: the painting also arranges the surgeons into a commemorative group portrait.

Sources: https://www.mauritshuis.nl/en/our-collection/artworks/146-the-anatomy-lesson-of-dr-nicolaes-tulp, https://pure.rug.nl/ws/files/121034472/The_Anatomy_Lessonof_Dr._Nicolaes_TulpbyRembrandt_1632_.pdf

Key facts

  1. The painting is dated 1632 and is an oil painting on canvas.Sources: https://www.mauritshuis.nl/en/our-collection/artworks/146-the-anatomy-lesson-of-dr-nicolaes-tulp
  2. The Amsterdam surgeons’ guild commissioned Rembrandt to portray its members with Dr Nicolaes Tulp.Sources: https://www.mauritshuis.nl/en/our-collection/artworks/146-the-anatomy-lesson-of-dr-nicolaes-tulp
  3. Tulp demonstrates the forearm’s muscles; the corpse is identified as Aris Kindt, who was hanged on January 31, 1632.Sources: https://www.mauritshuis.nl/en/our-collection/artworks/146-the-anatomy-lesson-of-dr-nicolaes-tulp, https://pure.rug.nl/ws/files/121034472/The_Anatomy_Lessonof_Dr._Nicolaes_TulpbyRembrandt_1632_.pdf
  4. The surgeons’ varied gazes, dynamic arrangement, and strong light-and-dark contrasts distinguish the composition.Sources: https://www.mauritshuis.nl/en/our-collection/artworks/146-the-anatomy-lesson-of-dr-nicolaes-tulp
  5. The painting presents a group portrait in the form of an anatomy lesson, not necessarily an exact depiction of the dissection itself.Sources: https://pure.rug.nl/ws/files/121034472/The_Anatomy_Lessonof_Dr._Nicolaes_TulpbyRembrandt_1632_.pdf
Source reference details

Dale Chihuly

Needs review

Original description: American artist known for large-scale, brightly colored blown-glass sculptures and installations inspired by natural forms and gardens.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-2b73ddc25b66eb116910 · Estimated cost: $0.0128

Overview

Dale Chihuly (born 1941 in Tacoma, Washington) is an American artist who made glass the focus of large-scale sculpture and installation. After studying interior design and glass, he co-founded Pilchuck Glass School in 1971, helping build a center for glass artists. Chihuly develops work with teams of glassblowers, rather than making every piece himself. His well-known series include Baskets, Seaforms, Persians, and Chandeliers. He also places glass in architectural and garden settings, extending sculpture onto walls and ceilings and into outdoor spaces. Projects such as Chihuly Over Venice and his Garden Cycle show how he uses settings as part of the experience of his glass art.

Sources: https://americanart.si.edu/artist/dale-chihuly-859, https://www.tacomaartmuseum.org/exhibit/dale-chihuly/artists/, https://www.chihulygardenandglass.com/about/dale-chihuly

  2. He received a Fulbright Fellowship in 1968 and worked at Venice’s Venini glass factory, where he observed a team approach to glassblowing.Sources: https://www.chihulygardenandglass.com/about/dale-chihuly
  3. Chihuly co-founded Pilchuck Glass School in Washington State in 1971.Sources: https://americanart.si.edu/artist/dale-chihuly-859, https://www.chihulygardenandglass.com/about/dale-chihuly
  4. After an automobile accident in 1976 left him without sight in his left eye, Chihuly shifted his way of making art; he has worked with a team of glassblowers.Sources: https://www.tacomaartmuseum.org/exhibit/dale-chihuly/artists/, https://americanart.si.edu/artist/dale-chihuly-859
  5. His series include Cylinders, Baskets, Seaforms, Macchia, Venetians, Persians, Niijima Floats, Chandeliers, and Fiori.Sources: https://www.chihulygardenandglass.com/about/dale-chihuly

Hector Berlioz

Needs review

Original description: French Romantic composer known for Symphonie fantastique, innovative orchestration, programmatic music, and The Damnation of Faust.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-95b750ae2bc4eea365b7 · Estimated cost: $0.0243

Overview

Hector Berlioz (1803–1869) was a French Romantic composer and conductor who left medical studies to pursue music. His 1830 Symphonie fantastique tells a dramatic story through five movements: a recurring melody, the idée fixe, represents the beloved of an imagined young artist, while changing orchestral colors help shape the narrative. Berlioz’s fascination with Shakespearean actress Harriet Smithson inspired the work in part, but its plot is a musical program, not a literal account of events. He also composed The Damnation of Faust and wrote a widely influential treatise on instruments and orchestration, treating tone color as an important part of musical expression.

Sources: https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/hector-berlioz, https://www.berliner-philharmoniker.de/en/programme-notes/hector-berlioz-symphonie-fantastique-op-14/, https://harvardonline.harvard.edu/course/berliozs-symphonie-fantastique-program-music-19th-century, https://dicteco.huma-num.fr/en/book/2007

Key facts

  1. Berlioz was born in La Côte-Saint-André, France, in 1803; after beginning medical studies in Paris, he chose a career in music.Sources: https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/hector-berlioz
  2. Symphonie fantastique was composed in 1830 and premiered in Paris on December 5 that year; it has five movements.Sources: https://www.berliner-philharmoniker.de/en/programme-notes/hector-berlioz-symphonie-fantastique-op-14/
  3. In the symphony, the idée fixe—a recurring theme associated with the beloved—returns in different forms across all five movements.Sources: https://www.berliner-philharmoniker.de/en/programme-notes/hector-berlioz-symphonie-fantastique-op-14/
  4. His orchestration treatise describes instruments’ ranges, mechanics, timbres, and expressive possibilities; its first edition appeared in late 1843, though the work is dated 1844.Sources: https://dicteco.huma-num.fr/en/book/2007
  5. The Damnation of Faust premiered in Paris in 1846, where its first performance was unsuccessful.Sources: https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/hector-berlioz

The Creation of Adam

Needs review

Original description: A celebrated fresco panel by Michelangelo on the ceiling of the Sistine Chapel depicting God giving life to Adam through nearly touching hands.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-98f3c3d904da6944ddc7 · Estimated cost: $0.0241

Overview

The Creation of Adam is a scene in Michelangelo’s frescoed ceiling of the Sistine Chapel in Vatican City, painted between 1508 and 1512. It presents the biblical account in Genesis of God creating the first human: Adam reclines on the left while God, borne by angels and wrapped in a mantle, reaches toward him. Their meeting fingers form the scene’s focal point, a vivid image of life passing from Creator to human. The panel belongs to a larger sequence of Genesis stories, placed between scenes of creation and the later story of Eve and humanity’s fall. Its striking, simple gesture has made it one of the best-known images of Renaissance art.

Sources: https://www.museivaticani.va/content/museivaticani/en/collezioni/musei/cappella-sistina/volta/storie-centrali/creazione-di-adamo.html, https://www.museivaticani.va/content/museivaticani/en/collezioni/musei/cappella-sistina/volta/storie-centrali.html, https://www.metmuseum.org/ja/essays/the-papacy-and-the-vatican-palace

Key facts

  1. The Vatican Museums identify the scene with Genesis 1:26–27 and describe the life-giving encounter between the Creator and Adam.Sources: https://www.museivaticani.va/content/museivaticani/en/collezioni/musei/cappella-sistina/volta/storie-centrali/creazione-di-adamo.html
  2. The Vatican Museums describe Adam as a resting athlete and God as leaning toward him, supported by angels and wrapped in a mantle.Sources: https://www.museivaticani.va/content/museivaticani/en/collezioni/musei/cappella-sistina/volta/storie-centrali/creazione-di-adamo.html
  3. The panel is part of the ceiling’s nine Genesis scenes, in the group about humanity’s creation, before the scenes of original sin and expulsion.Sources: https://www.museivaticani.va/content/museivaticani/en/collezioni/musei/cappella-sistina/volta/storie-centrali.html
  4. The Metropolitan Museum of Art dates Michelangelo’s painting of the Sistine Chapel ceiling to 1508–1512.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/ja/essays/the-papacy-and-the-vatican-palace
  5. The Metropolitan Museum of Art notes that the ceiling’s larger Genesis program includes the creation of the world and of Adam and Eve, the Fall and Expulsion, and the Flood.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/ja/essays/the-papacy-and-the-vatican-palace
Source reference details
  • Creation of Adam — Vatican Museums
    Identifies the scene with Genesis 1:26–27; describes the focal encounter between the fingers, the transmission of life in the biblical scene, Adam’s pose, and God with angels and mantle.
  • Central Stories — Vatican Museums
    Explains that the ceiling’s central sequence contains nine Genesis stories and places the Creation of Adam in the humanity group before Original Sin and the expulsion.
  • The Papacy and the Vatican Palace — The Metropolitan Museum of Art, Department of European Paintings
    Dates Michelangelo’s Sistine Chapel ceiling to 1508–1512 and summarizes its broader Genesis subjects.

Rembrandt

Needs review

Original description: Rembrandt was a Dutch Golden Age painter and printmaker known for works including The Night Watch, The Anatomy Lesson of Dr. Nicolaes Tulp, and Storm on the Sea of Galilee.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-d8fae10bf539039f367d · Estimated cost: $0.0129

Overview

Rembrandt van Rijn (1606–1669) was a Dutch painter and printmaker whose portraits, history paintings, and prints helped define seventeenth-century Dutch art. Born in Leiden, he trained there and in Amsterdam before moving to Amsterdam around 1632, where he built a successful workshop. Viewers often recognize his work by its dramatic contrasts of light and dark, expressive faces and gestures, and willingness to vary composition and brushwork. His 1642 group portrait, The Night Watch, turns a civic militia portrait into an energetic scene, with figures caught in different actions rather than arranged as a still lineup. He also made technically inventive etchings and drypoints, revising plates to create different effects.

Sources: https://www.nga.gov/artists/1822-rembrandt-van-rijn, https://www.rijksmuseum.nl/en/collection/node/Rembrandt-van-Rijn--ba2dc062acd137ffeac364321b7b10df, https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/rembrandt-van-rijn-1606-1669-prints

Key facts

  1. Rembrandt was born in Leiden in 1606 and died in Amsterdam in 1669.Sources: https://www.nga.gov/artists/1822-rembrandt-van-rijn
  2. He trained with Jacob van Swanenburgh in Leiden and Pieter Lastman in Amsterdam; by 1628 he was teaching pupils.Sources: https://www.nga.gov/artists/1822-rembrandt-van-rijn

  4. The Rijksmuseum describes The Night Watch as a lively group portrait: instead of posing the militia in a static lineup, Rembrandt shows its members in action.Sources: https://www.rijksmuseum.nl/en/collection/node/Rembrandt-van-Rijn--ba2dc062acd137ffeac364321b7b10df
  5. Rembrandt made about 300 etchings and drypoints between roughly 1626 and 1665, experimenting with printing methods and sometimes substantially reworking plates.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/rembrandt-van-rijn-1606-1669-prints

Gioachino Rossini

Needs review

Original description: Italian composer of comic and serious operas including The Barber of Seville, La Cenerentola, The Thieving Magpie, and William Tell.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-c5def35a6016656033a6 · Estimated cost: $0.0127

Overview

Gioachino Rossini (1792–1868) was an Italian composer whose prolific career made him a leading figure in early nineteenth-century opera. Born in Pesaro to musical parents, he studied in Bologna and began his theatrical career in Venice. He wrote both comic operas, including The Barber of Seville and La Cenerentola, and serious works such as Tancredi and William Tell. After working in Italy, Rossini settled in Paris, where William Tell premiered in 1829 as his last opera. He later composed sacred and shorter works, but his best-known achievements remain his operas, notable for spanning comic, serious, and in-between forms.

Sources: https://museonazionalerossini.it/eng/biography/, https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/gioacchino-rossini

Key facts

  1. Rossini was born in Pesaro on February 29, 1792; his father played horn and trumpet, and his mother was a singer.Sources: https://museonazionalerossini.it/eng/biography/
  2. He studied music in Bologna and made his theatrical debut in Venice in 1810 with the comic opera La cambiale di matrimonio.Sources: https://museonazionalerossini.it/eng/biography/, https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/gioacchino-rossini
  3. The Barber of Seville premiered in 1816 and La Cenerentola in 1817; both are among his celebrated comic operas.Sources: https://museonazionalerossini.it/eng/biography/
  4. Rossini also wrote serious opera, including Tancredi; his final opera, William Tell, premiered in Paris in 1829.Sources: https://museonazionalerossini.it/eng/biography/, https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/gioacchino-rossini
  5. After stepping away from opera, he continued to compose other music, including the sacred Stabat Mater and shorter works.Sources: https://museonazionalerossini.it/eng/biography/, https://www.operadeparis.fr/en/artists/gioacchino-rossini
Source reference details
  • Biography: Gioachino Rossini – Life and Works — Museo Nazionale Rossini
    Birth, musical family and studies; 1810 debut; genres and dates of major operas; later sacred and shorter compositions; death.
  • Gioacchino Rossini — Composer — Opéra national de Paris
    Rossini’s early career and Italian opera successes; his move to Paris; the 1829 premiere of William Tell as his last opera; later compositions.

Burj Khalifa

Needs review

Original description: A skyscraper in Dubai, United Arab Emirates, and the world's tallest building since its completion.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-0f7df610f8b3106356bb · Estimated cost: $0.0247

Overview

Burj Khalifa is a mixed-use skyscraper in Dubai, United Arab Emirates, that opened on January 4, 2010. At 828 meters (2,716.5 feet) to its architectural top, it became the world’s tallest building, a title formally confirmed by the Council on Tall Buildings and Urban Habitat in March 2010. Designed by Skidmore, Owings & Merrill, its three wings surround a central core in a Y-shaped plan that helps reduce wind forces; setbacks spiral upward toward a steel spire. Homes, offices, a hotel, and observation decks share the tower, making it a landmark of modern engineering and a defining feature of Dubai’s skyline.

Sources: https://newsletter.ctbuh.org/eblast/BurjKhalifa/BurjKhalifaHeight.html, https://www.emaar.com/en/other-emaar-businesses/burj-khalifa, https://www.burjkhalifa.ae/the-tower/structures/

Key facts

  1. The tower officially opened on January 4, 2010; CTBUH confirmed its world-tallest status on March 11 that year.Sources: https://newsletter.ctbuh.org/eblast/BurjKhalifa/BurjKhalifaHeight.html
  2. CTBUH records its architectural-top height as 828 meters; its measured height to the tip is 829.84 meters.Sources: https://newsletter.ctbuh.org/eblast/BurjKhalifa/BurjKhalifaHeight.html
  3. It was the first mixed-use building to hold the “World’s Tallest” title, according to CTBUH.Sources: https://newsletter.ctbuh.org/eblast/BurjKhalifa/BurjKhalifaHeight.html
  4. Its Y-shaped plan has three wings supported by a central hexagonal core; this buttressed-core system helps resist wind forces.Sources: https://www.burjkhalifa.ae/the-tower/structures/
  5. CTBUH reported 163 floors when it confirmed the building’s record in 2010.Sources: https://newsletter.ctbuh.org/eblast/BurjKhalifa/BurjKhalifaHeight.html
Source reference details

    Official height and record confirmation; opening and confirmation dates; mixed-use status; floor count; distinction between architectural-top height and tip height.
  • Burj Khalifa: World's Tallest Building & Global Landmark — Emaar Properties
    Location, current tallest-building designation, architect firm, mixed-use functions, and skyline significance.
  • Burj Khalifa Structure: Design and Construction — Burj Khalifa official website
    Three-wing, Y-shaped plan; central hexagonal core; buttressed-core system and wind-resisting purpose.

Everyday Life and Culture

Meat

Needs review

Original description: Animal flesh used as food, including conventional meat and meat produced or emulated through cultivated-meat and plant-based alternatives.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-daa0754982a18d2768b5 · Estimated cost: $0.0363

Overview

Meat is animal flesh eaten as food; in many everyday descriptions, “meat” chiefly means mammals such as cattle, pigs, and sheep, with poultry and seafood often grouped separately. It is a protein-rich food, but the animals people eat and the ways they prepare or avoid meat vary across regions, traditions, religions, and personal values. Plant-based meat alternatives contain plant or fungal ingredients processed to resemble animal meat in qualities such as flavor, texture, or appearance. Cultivated meat is different: it is made by growing animal cells in controlled conditions, then harvesting the resulting material. The key distinction is the source—plants or animal cells—not simply how meat-like a product looks or tastes.

Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/food/, https://apps.fas.usda.gov/newgainapi/api/Report/DownloadReportByFileName?fileName=Market+Overview+of+Plant-Based+Meat+Alternative+Products+in+China_Beijing_China+-+Peoples+Republic+of_01-07-2021, https://www.fda.gov/food/food-ingredients-packaging/human-food-made-cultured-animal-cells

Key facts

  1. In common usage, meat often refers to edible mammal flesh; poultry and seafood are also important animal foods and are often discussed separately.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/food/
  2. Meat is a protein-rich food that also contains other nutrients.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/food/
  3. Food traditions, regional ingredients, religion, and personal values can shape whether and how people eat meat.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/food/
  4. Plant-based meat alternatives can use soy, other legumes, or fungi, with processing intended to imitate qualities such as meat’s texture, flavor, or appearance.Sources: https://apps.fas.usda.gov/newgainapi/api/Report/DownloadReportByFileName?fileName=Market+Overview+of+Plant-Based+Meat+Alternative+Products+in+China_Beijing_China+-+Peoples+Republic+of_01-07-2021
  5. Cultivated meat begins with animal cells grown in a controlled environment; the cells can develop into muscle, fat, or connective-tissue types before the material is harvested for food.Sources: https://www.fda.gov/food/food-ingredients-packaging/human-food-made-cultured-animal-cells
Source reference details
  • Food — National Geographic Society
    Definition and common usage of meat; examples of animal-food categories; protein and other nutrients; how region, tradition, religion, and personal choices influence eating.
  • Market Overview of Plant-Based Meat Alternative Products in China — USDA Foreign Agricultural Service
    Plant-based alternatives’ ingredients, including soy, legumes, and fungi, and their processing to resemble animal meat in flavor, texture, and appearance; distinguishes them from cultivated animal-cell foods.

Sodium chloride

Needs review

Original description: The ionic compound NaCl, commonly encountered as edible salt and naturally occurring as the mineral halite, with major historical importance as a food preservative, seasoning, and traded commodity.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-555d82ed78d64f8ea308 · Estimated cost: $0.0250

Overview

Sodium chloride (NaCl), usually called common or table salt, is a compound made of sodium and chlorine. Its naturally occurring mineral form is halite, an evaporite: it crystallizes as salty water loses water through evaporation. Halite may occur in underground beds of rock salt, and its broken crystals often have a cube-like shape. People value salt for seasoning and have long used it to help dry and preserve foods. Salt also shaped economies: in Western Europe, common salt was an object of international competition and was important for preserving provisions on long-distance voyages. Thus, an everyday kitchen ingredient links mineral formation, food technology, and trade.

Sources: https://geokansas.ku.edu/halite-salt, https://nchfp.uga.edu/resources/entry/historical-origins-of-food-preservation, https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/full/10.1086/684654

Key facts

  1. NaCl is sodium chloride; halite is its naturally occurring mineral form.Sources: https://geokansas.ku.edu/halite-salt
  2. Halite forms as salty water evaporates and can accumulate in underground rock-salt beds.Sources: https://geokansas.ku.edu/halite-salt
  3. Halite fragments often have a cube-like shape, a recognizable feature of the mineral.Sources: https://geokansas.ku.edu/halite-salt
  4. Salt has been used both as a seasoning and to help dry foods during curing.Sources: https://nchfp.uga.edu/resources/entry/historical-origins-of-food-preservation
  5. In Western Europe, common salt was a matter of international competition and helped preserve food for long-distance voyages.Sources: https://www.journals.uchicago.edu/doi/full/10.1086/684654
Source reference details
  • Halite (Salt) — Kansas Geological Survey, University of Kansas
    Identifies halite as sodium chloride (NaCl), explains evaporite formation and underground salt beds, and describes the cube-like shape of broken fragments.
  • Historical Origins of Food Preservation — National Center for Home Food Preservation, University of Georgia
    Describes early curing with salt to help dry foods and notes salting's culinary use.
  • Producing (in) Europe and Asia, 1750–1850 — Isis, University of Chicago Press
    Lissa Roberts's historical essay describes common salt as an object of international competition in Western Europe and its importance for preserving food on long-distance trading voyages.

Geography

Haiti

Needs review

Original description: Haiti is the Caribbean country occupying the western portion of Hispaniola, with Port-au-Prince as its capital.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-b135a71ff894bf92f7cb · Estimated cost: $0.0244

Overview

Haiti is a Caribbean country occupying the western part of Hispaniola, the second-largest island in the Greater Antilles; the Dominican Republic occupies the rest. Its capital is Port-au-Prince. Haiti’s two long peninsulas give its outline a distinctive horseshoe shape, and its territory includes offshore islands such as Gonâve. Much of the land is mountainous, with river valleys and small coastal plains; Haiti is described as the most mountainous nation in the Caribbean. Five mountain ranges shape its landscape, which is commonly divided into northern, central, and southern regions. These contrasts between ridges, valleys, and coastal lowlands define Haiti’s physical geography.

Sources: https://dgff2021.unctad.org/haiti/, https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/master/frd/copr/Haiti.pdf

  2. Port-au-Prince is Haiti’s capital.Sources: https://dgff2021.unctad.org/haiti/, https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/master/frd/copr/Haiti.pdf
  3. Haiti’s outline is often described as horseshoe-shaped, with two prominent peninsulas and a long coastline.Sources: https://dgff2021.unctad.org/haiti/, https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/master/frd/copr/Haiti.pdf
  4. Its terrain is mostly mountainous, with river valleys and small coastal plains; Gonâve is among its offshore islands.Sources: https://dgff2021.unctad.org/haiti/, https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/master/frd/copr/Haiti.pdf

Alps

Needs review

Original description: The Alps are a major mountain range in central and southern Europe, extending from France through Switzerland and Italy to Austria and Slovenia.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-d1107568692f20d7bf68 · Estimated cost: $0.0131

Overview

The Alps are a major mountain system in south-central Europe, curving in a broad arc from the Ligurian Sea and southeastern France eastward toward Slovenia and the Adriatic. The Alpine Convention identifies an Alpine region spanning eight countries: Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Liechtenstein, Monaco, Slovenia, and Switzerland. The range is distinguished by high, rugged folded mountains, including Mont Blanc, and by its importance as a source area for major rivers such as the Rhine, Rhône, and Po. The Alps formed through tectonic compression and uplift associated with the collision of the Adriatic microplate and Eurasian Plate; mountain passes have long connected northern and Mediterranean Europe.

Sources: https://courier.unesco.org/en/articles/alpine-europe, https://www.alpconv.org/en/home, https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/fold-mountain/8th-grade/

Key facts

  1. The Alpine Convention’s Alpine region spans eight countries: Austria, France, Germany, Italy, Liechtenstein, Monaco, Slovenia, and Switzerland.Sources: https://www.alpconv.org/en/home
  2. The Alps form a broad arc more than 1,000 kilometres long, from the Ligurian Sea toward Slovenia and the Adriatic.Sources: https://courier.unesco.org/en/articles/alpine-europe
  3. Mont Blanc, between France and Italy, is the highest point in the Alps.Sources: https://courier.unesco.org/en/articles/alpine-europe
  4. The Rhine, Rhône, and Po rise in the Alps, making the range an important source region for European rivers.Sources: https://courier.unesco.org/en/articles/alpine-europe
  5. The Alps are fold mountains associated with the collision of the Adriatic microplate and the Eurasian Plate.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/fold-mountain/8th-grade/
Source reference details
  • Fold Mountain — National Geographic Society
    Identifies the Alps as active fold mountains, describes their eight-country extent and Alpine rock types, and attributes their tectonic setting to collision of the Adriatic microplate with the Eurasian Plate.
  • The Alpine Convention Who We Are — Alpine Convention
    Lists the eight countries included in the Alpine Convention’s Alpine region.
  • Alpine Europe — The UNESCO Courier
    Describes the Alps’ arc-shaped extent, Mont Blanc as the highest point, the Rhine, Rhône and Po as rivers rising there, and the historic role of passes in connecting northern and Mediterranean Europe.

Connecticut

Needs review

Original description: A northeastern U.S. state in New England, with Hartford as its capital and a history rooted in the former Connecticut Colony.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-ea5fc9cddcdccfbea10c · Estimated cost: $0.0135

Overview

Connecticut is a small, southernmost state of New England, bordered by New York, Rhode Island, Massachusetts, and Long Island Sound. The Connecticut River flows south through the state, roughly dividing it; flatter coastal and central areas contrast with forested hills, especially in the northwest. Its history links colonial New England to the founding United States: chartered as a colony in 1662, Connecticut became the fifth state to ratify the U.S. Constitution, on January 9, 1788. Hartford is now its capital and has been the sole capital city since 1875. The state adopted “The Constitution State” as its official designation in 1959.

Sources: https://portal.ct.gov/about/the-land, https://www.census.gov/geographies/reference-files/2010/geo/state-local-geo-guides-2010/connecticut.html, https://portal.ct.gov/en/about

  1. Connecticut borders New York to the west, Rhode Island to the east, Massachusetts to the north, and Long Island Sound to the south.Sources: https://portal.ct.gov/about/the-land
  2. The Connecticut River runs southward and roughly divides the state; its landscape includes flatter coastal and central areas and forested hills.Sources: https://portal.ct.gov/about/the-land
  3. Connecticut was chartered as a colony in 1662 and became the fifth state to ratify the U.S. Constitution, on January 9, 1788.Sources: https://www.census.gov/geographies/reference-files/2010/geo/state-local-geo-guides-2010/connecticut.html
  4. Hartford has been Connecticut’s sole capital city since 1875.Sources: https://portal.ct.gov/en/about
  5. “The Constitution State” became Connecticut’s official designation through a 1959 legislative act.Sources: https://portal.ct.gov/en/about

Colombia

Needs review

Original description: The modern country in northwestern South America, whose capital is Bogotá.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-a60a4f2fbb54d69997bc · Estimated cost: $0.0246

Overview

Colombia, officially the Republic of Colombia, occupies the northwestern corner of South America; its capital, Bogotá, stands high in the Andes. The country is especially varied in landforms: geographers distinguish six natural regions—Andean, Caribbean, Pacific, Orinoquía, Amazonian, and Insular. In the west, the Andes divide into three cordilleras, with the Cauca and Magdalena river valleys between them. Northern lowlands reach the Caribbean, while the Pacific region lies to the west; broad plains and savannas extend east toward Amazonian rainforest. These sharp contrasts in elevation and landscape help make Colombia’s geography distinctive, from mountain valleys and highlands to tropical coasts and forests.

Sources: https://www2.sgc.gov.co/LibroGeologiaColombia/Paginas/v1ch1.aspx, https://bogota.gov.co/en/node/55

Key facts

  1. Colombia lies in the northwestern corner of South America.Sources: https://www2.sgc.gov.co/LibroGeologiaColombia/Paginas/v1ch1.aspx
  2. Bogotá is the country’s capital, located in the Andes at about 2,600 meters above sea level.Sources: https://bogota.gov.co/en/node/55
  3. Colombia’s six natural regions are Andean, Caribbean, Pacific, Orinoquía, Amazonian, and Insular.Sources: https://www2.sgc.gov.co/LibroGeologiaColombia/Paginas/v1ch1.aspx
  4. In Colombia, the Andes form three cordilleras separated by the Cauca and Magdalena river valleys.Sources: https://www2.sgc.gov.co/LibroGeologiaColombia/Paginas/v1ch1.aspx
  5. The eastern Orinoquía is characterized by plains and savannas, while the Amazonian region includes rainforest.Sources: https://www2.sgc.gov.co/LibroGeologiaColombia/Paginas/v1ch1.aspx

Central Park

Needs review

Original description: A large urban park in Manhattan, New York City, designed primarily by Frederick Law Olmsted and Calvert Vaux.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-a6ac76ee34dd0cba8f47 · Estimated cost: $0.0128

Overview

Central Park is a major public park in Manhattan, New York City, created to provide recreation and a countryside-like retreat for residents of a rapidly growing city. Its winning Greensward Plan, designed by landscape architects Frederick Law Olmsted and Calvert Vaux, blended open lawns, woodlands, lakes, and paths with bridges and roads arranged to let traffic pass through without spoiling the park experience. The park’s 843 acres include distinct landscapes such as the Ramble, a deliberately designed woodland with winding trails, a stream, and rocky outcrops. Its influential design helped shape urban parks nationwide. The site also has a human history: creating the park displaced residents, including people from Seneca Village, a predominantly African American community.

Sources: https://www.centralparknyc.org/park-history, https://www.nps.gov/places/central-park.htm, https://www.centralparknyc.org/locations/the-ramble

Key facts

  1. Central Park covers about 843 acres in Manhattan.Sources: https://www.centralparknyc.org/park-history, https://www.nps.gov/places/central-park.htm
  2. Frederick Law Olmsted and Calvert Vaux’s Greensward Plan won a design competition; it combined naturalistic scenery with formal features and bridges.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/central-park.htm
  3. The design used transverse roads to let vehicles cross the park while preserving the park experience.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/central-park.htm
  4. The Ramble is a designed woodland with winding paths, a stream, rocky outcrops, and rustic bridges.Sources: https://www.centralparknyc.org/locations/the-ramble
  5. Building the park displaced residents, including members of Seneca Village, a predominantly African American community.Sources: https://www.centralparknyc.org/park-history
Source reference details

    Park purpose, designers, acreage, landscape features, influence on urban parks, and the displacement of residents including Seneca Village.
  • Central Park — National Park Service
    The Greensward Plan competition, its combination of natural and formal settings, bridges, transverse roads, acreage, and influence.
  • The Ramble — Central Park Conservancy
    The Ramble’s designed woodland character and its paths, stream, rocky outcrops, and bridges.

Nome, Alaska

Needs review

Original description: A city on Alaska’s Seward Peninsula and the western terminus of the Iditarod Trail Sled Dog Race.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-ff4cf48ec961a7524b22 · Estimated cost: $0.0243

Overview

Nome is a city at the southern edge of Alaska’s Seward Peninsula; its local Iñupiaq name is Sitnasuaq. Its modern growth began with a gold discovery near Anvil Creek in 1898, followed by gold found in beach sands the next year, helping turn it into a boomtown. Archaeological evidence also documents human use of the area long before gold seekers arrived. Nome is a regional hub and the finish city of the Iditarod Trail Sled Dog Race: the first race to Nome took place in 1973. The historic trail served as a route for mail and supplies, and part of it was used in the 1925 sled-dog serum relay to Nome.

Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/aps-20-2-3.htm, https://dnr.alaska.gov/parks/oha/clg/AlaskaCLGs/Nome.htm, https://iditarod.com/about__trashed/the-beginning/

Key facts

  1. Nome lies at the southern edge of the Seward Peninsula; the National Park Service gives Sitnasuaq as its local Iñupiaq name.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/aps-20-2-3.htm

  3. Archaeological evidence indicates that people used the area before the gold rush, although the site was not a permanent settlement when gold was discovered.Sources: https://dnr.alaska.gov/parks/oha/clg/AlaskaCLGs/Nome.htm

  5. The historic Iditarod Trail carried mail and supplies, and the 1925 serum relay to Nome used part of the trail.Sources: https://dnr.alaska.gov/parks/oha/clg/AlaskaCLGs/Nome.htm, https://iditarod.com/about__trashed/the-beginning/

Tigris River

Needs review

Original description: A major river of Mesopotamia flowing through or along Turkey, Syria, and Iraq, including Baghdad, and forming one of the region's defining rivers alongside the Euphrates.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-ecff1d3bcd878b55a3e7 · Estimated cost: $0.0246

Overview

The Tigris is one of the two great rivers associated with Mesopotamia, alongside the Euphrates. It rises in eastern Turkey’s Taurus Mountains, receives much of its water from snowmelt, and flows south through Turkey into Iraq. In Iraq, it passes Baghdad, a major city on its banks. Farther south, the Tigris joins the Euphrates in the Shatt al-Arab waterway, which empties into the Persian Gulf. The river has long mattered because its water supports irrigation, farming, travel, and settlement in a region where water is especially valuable.

Sources: https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/tigris-river/, https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/tigris-river/, https://www.usgs.gov/media/audio/eyes-earth-episode-7-training-iraqi-scientists

Key facts

  1. Four major tributaries named in a scholarly overview are the Greater Zab, Lesser Zab, Al-Adhaim, and Diyala.Sources: https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/tigris-river/
  2. The Tigris and Euphrates form a river system associated with Mesopotamia and the Fertile Crescent.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/tigris-river/
  3. The river’s water has supported irrigation, farming, and travel in the region since the earliest known civilizations.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/tigris-river/
  4. The river flows from Turkey into Iraq, making Iraq a downstream country that depends on water from the wider basin.Sources: https://www.usgs.gov/media/audio/eyes-earth-episode-7-training-iraqi-scientists
  5. The Tigris has a stronger seasonal flow: a scholarly overview describes high water in March and April and lower levels in summer.Sources: https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/tigris-river/
Source reference details
  • TIGRIS RIVER — Encyclopædia Iranica
    Eastern Turkey and Taurus Mountains headwaters, snowmelt, Baghdad and navigation, tributaries, seasonal high water, and historical importance.
  • Tigris River — National Geographic Society
    Tigris-Euphrates relationship, Shatt al-Arab and Persian Gulf, and the river’s importance for irrigation, farming, and travel.
  • Eyes on Earth Episode 7 – Training Iraqi Scientists — U.S. Geological Survey
    USGS interview transcript describing the Tigris flowing from Turkey to Iraq and Iraq’s downstream dependence.

Red Sea

Needs review

Original description: A seawater inlet between northeastern Africa and the Arabian Peninsula, connecting the Mediterranean region through the Gulf of Suez and the Gulf of Aqaba to the Gulf of Aden.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-8103c6933bf6b005f91d · Estimated cost: $0.0255

Overview

The Red Sea is a long, narrow tropical sea between northeastern Africa and the Arabian Peninsula. At its northern end, the Sinai Peninsula separates it into the Gulf of Suez and the Gulf of Aqaba; the Gulf of Suez connects to the Mediterranean through Egypt’s Suez Canal. In the south, the Bab el-Mandeb Strait leads to the Gulf of Aden and the Indian Ocean. This position makes the Red Sea part of a major shipping route between Europe and Asia. It is also a rift basin shaped by the movement of the African and Arabian tectonic plates, and its warm waters support extensive coral reefs and distinctive marine life.

Sources: https://www.emecs.or.jp/en/wp-content/uploads/2019/10/2015_guidebook-16_redsea.pdf, https://www.suezcanal.gov.eg/English/About/SuezCanal/Pages/AboutSuezCanal.aspx, https://whc.unesco.org/document/169155

Key facts

  1. The Red Sea is a semi-enclosed tropical sea; its southern opening is through the Bab el-Mandeb Strait to the Gulf of Aden.Sources: https://www.emecs.or.jp/en/wp-content/uploads/2019/10/2015_guidebook-16_redsea.pdf
  2. At its northern end, the Red Sea branches into the Gulf of Suez and the Gulf of Aqaba.Sources: https://www.emecs.or.jp/en/wp-content/uploads/2019/10/2015_guidebook-16_redsea.pdf
  3. Egypt’s Suez Canal is an artificial waterway connecting the Red Sea and Mediterranean Sea, creating a shorter maritime route between Europe and the Indian Ocean region.Sources: https://www.suezcanal.gov.eg/English/About/SuezCanal/Pages/AboutSuezCanal.aspx
  4. The Red Sea basin is associated with the African and Arabian tectonic plates gradually moving apart.Sources: https://whc.unesco.org/document/169155
  5. The Red Sea is noted for warm, highly saline waters, extensive coral reefs, and marine species found nowhere else.Sources: https://whc.unesco.org/document/169155
Source reference details
  • 16 Red Sea (Guidebook chapter) — Environmental Management of Enclosed Coastal Seas (EMECS)
    Location between the Mediterranean and Indian Ocean; tropical, narrow, semi-enclosed form; northern gulfs; southern connection through Bab el-Mandeb and Gulf of Aden; coral reefs and regional marine habitats.
  • About Suez Canal — Suez Canal Authority
    The canal is an artificial waterway connecting the Mediterranean and Red Sea and provides a shorter shipping route between Europe and the Indian Ocean region.
  • Nomination of Sanganeb Marine National Park and Dungonab Bay/Mukkawar Island Marine National Park (Sudan – Red Sea) for Inscription on the World Heritage List — Sudanese National Commission for UNESCO; hosted by the UNESCO World Heritage Centre
    The Red Sea as an embryonic ocean shaped by separation of African and Arabian tectonic plates; warm, highly saline waters; coral reefs and distinctive marine biodiversity.

Literature

Lord Byron

Needs review

Original description: Lord Byron, formally George Gordon Byron, was an English Romantic poet known for works including Don Juan, Childe Harold’s Pilgrimage, and “The Destruction of Sennacherib.”

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-1d0320088cf522549523 · Estimated cost: $0.0245

Overview

George Gordon Byron (1788–1824), known as Lord Byron, was an English poet and leading figure of the Romantic movement. His aristocratic title and public image made him a celebrated, controversial figure, but his reputation rests especially on poems that combine vivid narrative, personal feeling, political interest, and satire. Childe Harold’s Pilgrimage, partly shaped by his travels, brought him wide fame in 1812. In contrast, Don Juan turns the legendary lover’s story into a comic, sharply critical narrative poem, using the playful eight-line ottava rima stanza; Byron left it unfinished. His writings helped shape the influential “Byronic hero,” while his support for Greek independence linked his literary fame to political action; he died in Greece.

Sources: https://www.qmul.ac.uk/library/archives/blogs/items/eight-octagon-poets-lord-byron.html, https://www.britishlibrary.cn/en/works/don-juan/, https://poets.org/poet/lord-george-gordon-byron

Key facts

  1. Byron was born George Gordon Byron and inherited an English peerage; he became known as Lord Byron.Sources: https://www.qmul.ac.uk/library/archives/blogs/items/eight-octagon-poets-lord-byron.html
  2. The first two cantos of Childe Harold’s Pilgrimage appeared in 1812 and quickly made him famous.Sources: https://www.qmul.ac.uk/library/archives/blogs/items/eight-octagon-poets-lord-byron.html
  3. Don Juan is a comic, satirical narrative poem; its eight-line ottava rima stanzas end with a rhyming couplet.Sources: https://www.britishlibrary.cn/en/works/don-juan/
  4. Byron’s Don Juan remained unfinished when he died in Greece in 1824, where he had supported the independence movement.Sources: https://www.qmul.ac.uk/library/archives/blogs/items/eight-octagon-poets-lord-byron.html
  5. The rebellious, passionate “Byronic hero” associated with his writing influenced later Romantic poets.Sources: https://poets.org/poet/lord-george-gordon-byron
Source reference details
  • EIGHT OCTAGON POETS: LORD BYRON — Queen Mary University of London, Library Services
    Identity, Romantic movement, the 1812 publication and fame of Childe Harold’s Pilgrimage, unfinished Don Juan, Greek independence support, and Byron’s death in Greece.
  • Lord Byron's Don Juan — The British Library
    Don Juan’s satirical and comic character, its narrative form, and its ottava rima stanza structure.
  • About Lord George Gordon Byron — Academy of American Poets
    Byron’s identity and the Byronic hero’s influence on later Romantic poets.

To a Mouse, on Turning Her Up in Her Nest with the Plough

Needs review

Original description: A poem by Robert Burns in which a plough-disturbed mouse's nest prompts reflections on human disruption of nature and the uncertainty of future plans.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-7470061336abc3b80fd2 · Estimated cost: $0.0226

Overview

In “To a Mouse,” Robert Burns addresses a field mouse whose nest has been overturned by a plough, expressing regret that human activity has disrupted nature. The poem follows the mouse’s ruined shelter as winter approaches, then widens into a reflection on how plans can fail for both animals and people. Its closing contrast suggests that the mouse lives in the present, while the speaker worries about the past and future. Burns’s Scots wording—such as “wee” and “gang aft agley”—gives the poem a distinctive voice. First published in 1786, it is known for its sympathy toward the mouse and inspired the title of John Steinbeck’s *Of Mice and Men*.

Sources: https://www.nts.org.uk/stories/to-a-mouse, https://poetryarchive.org/poem/mouse-turning-her-up-her-nest-plough-november-1785/

Key facts

  1. The full subtitle identifies the occasion as turning the mouse up in her nest with a plough in November 1785.Sources: https://www.nts.org.uk/stories/to-a-mouse, https://poetryarchive.org/poem/mouse-turning-her-up-her-nest-plough-november-1785/
  2. The poem appeared in 1786 in *Poems, Chiefly in the Scottish Dialect*.Sources: https://www.nts.org.uk/stories/to-a-mouse
  3. Burns’s speaker calls the mouse an “earth-born companion” and “fellow-mortal,” emphasizing kinship between humans and animals.Sources: https://www.nts.org.uk/stories/to-a-mouse, https://poetryarchive.org/poem/mouse-turning-her-up-her-nest-plough-november-1785/
  4. The phrase “the best-laid schemes o’ mice an’ men / Gang aft agley” expresses the poem’s idea that plans often go wrong.Sources: https://www.nts.org.uk/stories/to-a-mouse, https://poetryarchive.org/poem/mouse-turning-her-up-her-nest-plough-november-1785/
  5. The poem inspired the title of John Steinbeck’s *Of Mice and Men*.Sources: https://www.nts.org.uk/stories/to-a-mouse
Source reference details
  • To a Mouse — National Trust for Scotland
    Provides the poem, its 1785 setting and 1786 publication history, a Scots-word glossary, commentary on its treatment of nature, and the connection to Steinbeck’s title.
  • To A Mouse, On Turning Her Up in Her Nest with the Plough, November, 1785 — The Poetry Archive
    Provides the poem text, including the speaker’s apology, the mouse’s disrupted shelter, the plans-gone-wrong passage, and the closing contrast between the mouse’s present and the speaker’s fears.

Song of Myself

Needs review

Original description: Walt Whitman's landmark long poem in Leaves of Grass, opening with “I celebrate myself, and sing myself” and including the phrase “barbaric yawp.”

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-66fdde095836fdc688ee · Estimated cost: $0.0258

Overview

“Song of Myself” is Walt Whitman’s long poem, first published in 1855 as one of twelve untitled poems in Leaves of Grass; it later became known by its familiar title. Its 52 sections move through scenes of nature, bodily experience, work, death, and encounters with other people. The speaker begins by celebrating himself but repeatedly connects his own life with the reader’s and with a broad human community, making the self both individual and shared. Whitman’s experimental free verse and sweeping, energetic voice helped establish a distinctly American poetic style. The poem’s final section ends with its defiant image of a “barbaric yawp,” a phrase that captures its bold, public voice.

Sources: https://www.nps.gov/people/walt-whitman.htm, https://poets.org/text/guide-walt-whitmans-leaves-grass, https://poets.org/anthology/song-myself

Key facts

  1. The poem began as an untitled work in the 1855 edition of Leaves of Grass and later became famous as “Song of Myself.”Sources: https://www.nps.gov/people/walt-whitman.htm, https://www.loc.gov/exhibits/whitman/leavesofgrass.html
  2. The poem is long and divided into 52 sections.Sources: https://poets.org/text/guide-walt-whitmans-leaves-grass
  3. Its opening links the speaker’s self-celebration to the reader, presenting what belongs to one as belonging to others too.Sources: https://poets.org/anthology/song-myself
  4. The poem brings together nature, bodily experience, everyday work, death, and encounters with other people.Sources: https://poets.org/anthology/song-myself
  5. Whitman’s experimental free verse helped make him one of the most influential poets in American literature.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/people/walt-whitman.htm
  6. In the final section, the speaker declares, “I sound my barbaric yawp over the roofs of the world.”Sources: https://poets.org/anthology/song-myself
Source reference details
  • Walt Whitman — National Park Service
    The 1855 Leaves of Grass contained twelve untitled poems, one later famous as “Song of Myself”; Whitman’s experimental free verse and influence in American literature.
  • A Guide to Walt Whitman’s Leaves of Grass — Academy of American Poets
    The poem’s place in the 1855 collection and its length of 52 sections.
  • Song of Myself — Academy of American Poets
    Primary text supporting the opening’s connection between self and reader, the poem’s range of subjects, and the closing “barbaric yawp.”
  • Walt Whitman and Leaves of Grass: Revising Himself — Leaves of Grass — Library of Congress
    The collection’s changing editions and Whitman’s practice of naming or renaming poems.

Invisible Man (Ralph Ellison novel)

Needs review

Original description: Ralph Ellison's 1952 novel follows an unnamed Black narrator whose social invisibility reflects racism, political manipulation, and the racial realities of the United States.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-7539291f43a2a3bc33ec · Estimated cost: $0.0129

Overview

Ralph Ellison’s novel *Invisible Man* (1952) is a first-person coming-of-age story narrated by an unnamed Black man who describes himself as invisible because society refuses to recognize him. His journey from the South to New York and Harlem exposes racism and the pressures of institutions and political movements that try to define him. Encounters with a paint factory and the Brotherhood, an organization claiming to help oppressed people, mix social critique with surreal and sometimes comic episodes. His underground refuge frames an ongoing search for identity and self-determination. Recognized as an important work of American literature, the novel explores African American life amid unequal treatment; Ellison became the first Black author to win the National Book Award, in 1953.

Sources: https://www.neh.gov/article/invisible-man-seventy, https://loc.gov/exhibits/books-that-shaped-america/1950-to-2000.html

Key facts

  1. The novel was published by Random House in 1952.Sources: https://loc.gov/exhibits/books-that-shaped-america/1950-to-2000.html
  2. Its unnamed narrator’s invisibility is social and metaphorical: people fail to recognize or pay attention to him.Sources: https://www.neh.gov/article/invisible-man-seventy, https://loc.gov/exhibits/books-that-shaped-america/1950-to-2000.html
  3. The narrator’s journey takes him from the South to New York City and Harlem.Sources: https://www.neh.gov/article/invisible-man-seventy
  4. He lives in an underground room illuminated by 1,369 stolen lightbulbs.Sources: https://www.neh.gov/article/invisible-man-seventy
  5. Ellison won the 1953 National Book Award for the novel, becoming the first Black author to receive the award.Sources: https://www.neh.gov/article/invisible-man-seventy
Source reference details
  • Invisible Man at Seventy — National Endowment for the Humanities, HUMANITIES magazine
    Supports the narrator’s identity and journey, the novel’s coming-of-age form and surreal episodes, its treatment of racism and identity, underground-room detail, and 1953 National Book Award recognition.
  • 1950 to 2000 - Books That Shaped America — Library of Congress
    Supports publication details, the unnamed narrator’s metaphorical invisibility, and the novel’s significance as a work about African American life and minority rights.

Amy Tan

Needs review

Original description: American novelist known for works including The Joy Luck Club and The Kitchen God's Wife, often depicting Chinese-American family relationships.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-16f6228ca40d3c3dbc16 · Estimated cost: $0.0129

Overview

Amy Tan is an American novelist whose fiction explores family, memory, immigration, and cultural heritage. Her first novel, The Joy Luck Club (1989), tells the stories of four Chinese immigrant mothers and their American daughters. Its connected stories, with linked perspectives and family histories, combine into a novel-like whole. By focusing on relationships across generations and cultures, the book presents immigrant experience through individual lives. Tan also wrote The Kitchen God's Wife and other novels. The Joy Luck Club was a 1989 National Book Award finalist, and Tan received the 2021 National Humanities Medal; its citation recognized her contribution to expanding the American literary canon.

Sources: https://www.neh.gov/award/amy-tan, https://www.arts.gov/initiatives/nea-big-read/joy-luck-club, https://www.nationalbook.org/books/the-joy-luck-club/, https://www.loc.gov/events/2020-national-book-festival/authors/item/n88021941/amy-tan/

  2. The novel links stories about four pairs of mothers and daughters; each of its four sections opens with a Chinese fable.Sources: https://www.arts.gov/initiatives/nea-big-read/joy-luck-club
  3. The book's Joy Luck Club was inspired by an investment club formed by Tan's parents and their friends, who met to play mahjong and share their lives.Sources: https://www.neh.gov/award/amy-tan
  4. The Joy Luck Club was a 1989 National Book Award finalist in fiction.Sources: https://www.nationalbook.org/books/the-joy-luck-club/
  5. Tan also wrote The Kitchen God's Wife, The Bonesetter's Daughter, and other novels.Sources: https://www.loc.gov/events/2020-national-book-festival/authors/item/n88021941/amy-tan

Alexandre Dumas père

Needs review

Original description: Alexandre Dumas père was a French novelist known for adventure works including The Count of Monte Cristo and The Three Musketeers.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-27b78cd1efd8fc0cb7cb · Estimated cost: $0.0253

Overview

Alexandre Dumas père (1802–1870)—“père” distinguishes him from his son, Alexandre Dumas fils—was a French novelist and playwright born in Villers-Cotterêts. A leading storyteller of historical adventure, he brought history to popular readers through energetic plots, theatrical scenes, and sharply drawn characters. His best-known novels include The Three Musketeers (1844) and The Count of Monte Cristo (1845–46). In The Three Musketeers, Dumas reshaped earlier memoir-like material into a lively fictional world featuring d’Artagnan, Athos, Porthos, and Aramis; the Académie française notes that collaborator Auguste Maquet drafted an early version, which Dumas revised and reworked. Historical settings, suspense, humor, and memorable companions helped make these stories lasting landmarks of popular literature.

Sources: https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb119010630.public, https://www.musee-orsay.fr/fr/ressources/repertoire-artistes-personnalites/alexandre-dumas-11550, https://expositions.bnf.fr/les-nadar/grand/nad_064.php, https://www.academie-francaise.fr/genese-des-trois-mousquetaires

Key facts

  1. The Bibliothèque nationale de France identifies Dumas as a French novelist and dramatist and lists Alexandre Dumas père as an alternate form of his name.Sources: https://catalogue.bnf.fr/ark:/12148/cb119010630.public
  2. The Three Musketeers appeared in 1844; The Count of Monte Cristo followed in 1845–1846.Sources: https://expositions.bnf.fr/les-nadar/grand/nad_064.php
  3. Dumas drew on earlier d’Artagnan memoirs for The Three Musketeers, but the Académie française notes that the memoirs were apocryphal, not written by the historical d’Artagnan.Sources: https://www.academie-francaise.fr/genese-des-trois-mousquetaires
  4. Auguste Maquet collaborated on The Three Musketeers: he prepared an early draft, which Dumas substantially reworked.Sources: https://www.academie-francaise.fr/genese-des-trois-mousquetaires
  5. The novel’s serialized publication drew eager readers; the Académie française recounts that people in villages went out to meet the postman for the next installment.Sources: https://www.academie-francaise.fr/genese-des-trois-mousquetaires

Model-reported concerns

  • The BnF Nadar exhibition caption lists Dumas’s birth year as 1803, in conflict with the BnF authority record and Musée d’Orsay, which give 1802. The overview uses the concordant 1802 records.

  • Alexandre Dumas (11550) — Musée d’Orsay

Saki

Needs review

Original description: Saki was the pen name of English short-story writer Hector Hugh Munro, known for satirical and macabre fiction.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-f28928063b24a0ca0f2e · Estimated cost: $0.0130

Overview

Saki was the pen name of Hector Hugh Munro (1870–1916), an English writer whose short stories made him known for their sharp wit and satire. Many take aim at upper-class English life, mixing clever humor with a sometimes cruel edge. Munro also wrote journalism, political sketches, a history of Russia, and a novel, but short fiction became his best-known work. Collections such as Reginald, The Chronicles of Clovis, and Beasts and Super-Beasts showcase his unconventional characters, fantastic situations, and knack for an unexpected phrase. His literary signature is the combination of polished social comedy with a distinctly biting, unpredictable tone.

Sources: https://www.oxfordlearnersdictionaries.com/us/definition/english/saki, https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography,_1927_supplement/Munro,_Hector_Hugh, https://orlando.cambridge.org/people/35a25024-886b-4a2d-b1ba-bc01cc110f81

Key facts

  1. Saki was born Hector Hugh Munro in Akyab, Burma, in 1870, and died in France in 1916.Sources: https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography,_1927_supplement/Munro,_Hector_Hugh, https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/authors/55640/saki/
  2. He first worked as a political satirist and journalist, and later reported from abroad for the Morning Post.Sources: https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography,_1927_supplement/Munro,_Hector_Hugh, https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/authors/55640/saki/
  3. His stories are often set among upper-class English people and use clever, sometimes cruel humor.Sources: https://www.oxfordlearnersdictionaries.com/us/definition/english/saki
  4. Notable story collections include Reginald (1904), The Chronicles of Clovis (1911), and Beasts and Super-Beasts (1914).Sources: https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography,_1927_supplement/Munro,_Hector_Hugh, https://orlando.cambridge.org/people/35a25024-886b-4a2d-b1ba-bc01cc110f81
  5. His pseudonym, “Saki,” was identified in a 1927 biography as the name of the cupbearer in the Rubáiyát of Omar Khayyám.Sources: https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Dictionary_of_National_Biography,_1927_supplement/Munro,_Hector_Hugh

Harry Potter

Needs review

Original description: The protagonist of J. K. Rowling's Harry Potter series, a young wizard who survives Voldemort's attack and ultimately defeats him at the Battle of Hogwarts.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-98de011b05ebec07eceb · Estimated cost: $0.0356

Overview

Harry James Potter is the fictional young wizard at the center of J.K. Rowling’s seven-book fantasy series, which follows one year of his life at Hogwarts in each book. Orphaned when Lord Voldemort murdered his parents, Harry becomes known as “the Boy Who Lived” after surviving Voldemort’s attack as a baby—the only known survivor of the Killing Curse. Raised by Muggle relatives unaware of magic, he learns he is a wizard on his eleventh birthday and joins Gryffindor at Hogwarts, where he forms a close friendship with Ron Weasley and Hermione Granger. His survival marks him as Voldemort’s rival, and their conflict culminates in the Battle of Hogwarts and Voldemort’s downfall.

Epic of Gilgamesh

Needs review

Original description: An ancient Mesopotamian epic centered on King Gilgamesh of Uruk, including his adventures with Enkidu and quest for immortality.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-bdb0f6d837c20442a281 · Estimated cost: $0.0248

Overview

The Epic of Gilgamesh is an ancient Mesopotamian epic centered on Gilgamesh, a legendary king of Uruk. If he was a historical person, he may have lived around 2700 BCE, but no contemporary evidence of him is known. The work took shape through Sumerian and Akkadian stories preserved on clay tablets. In the best-known Babylonian narrative, Gilgamesh and Enkidu become friends, battle Humbaba and the Bull of Heaven, and Enkidu’s death drives Gilgamesh to seek eternal life. His meeting with Utnapishtim introduces a flood-survivor story, while his failed quest explores mortality, friendship, leadership, and the limits of heroic power.

Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/it/essays/gilgamesh, https://collections.penn.museum/collections/object.php?irn=347490, https://yalebooks.yale.edu/2020/04/30/the-epic-of-gilgamesh/

Key facts

  1. Gilgamesh is presented as king of Uruk; the Met cautions that his historical existence is unconfirmed.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/it/essays/gilgamesh
  2. The work draws on Sumerian and Akkadian traditions; surviving versions are not all identical.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/it/essays/gilgamesh, https://www.metmuseum.org/ko/essays/the-isin-larsa-and-old-babylonian-periods-2004-1595-b-c
  3. A Penn Museum clay tablet from Nippur, dated 1900–1600 BCE, preserves a Sumerian literary episode about Gilgamesh and Huwawa.Sources: https://collections.penn.museum/collections/object.php?irn=347490
  4. After Enkidu dies, Gilgamesh seeks immortality; the story frames the quest as a struggle with human mortality.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/it/essays/gilgamesh, https://yalebooks.yale.edu/2020/04/30/the-epic-of-gilgamesh/
  5. In the Babylonian narrative, Utnapishtim recounts surviving a great flood, an episode in the epic’s exploration of life and death.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/it/essays/gilgamesh, https://yalebooks.yale.edu/2020/04/30/the-epic-of-gilgamesh/
Source reference details
  • Gilgamesh — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    Gilgamesh’s uncertain historicity; the Sumerian and Akkadian story traditions; the Enkidu friendship, heroic adventures, immortality quest, and flood-survivor episode.
  • Tablet - 29-13-209 — Penn Museum
    A physical clay tablet from Nippur, its Old Babylonian date and Sumerian language, and its literary text about Gilgamesh and Huwawa.
  • The Epic of Gilgamesh — Yale University Press
    The epic’s Mesopotamian setting, clay-tablet preservation, major plot events, flood narrative, and themes of friendship and mortality.
  • The Isin-Larsa and Old Babylonian Periods (2004–1595 B.C.) — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
