The Lottery (short story)

Needs review

Original description: Shirley Jackson's 1948 short story about a village's annual ritualistic stoning of one of its residents.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-87ebcbce5c50c814b3a7 · Estimated cost: $0.0138

Overview

“The Lottery” is Shirley Jackson’s short story, first published in The New Yorker in 1948. It begins in an ordinary-looking village on a bright summer morning, where neighbors gather for an annual drawing organized around a shabby black box and folded slips of paper. Jackson gradually reveals that the “winner” is selected for death: the black-spotted slip identifies Tessie Hutchinson, whom the villagers stone. The calm, matter-of-fact narration and the children’s early collection of stones make the turn especially chilling. The story remains significant for its unsettling portrait of communal violence and unexamined tradition. Its meaning has prompted debate, so it is best approached as an open-ended story rather than a fixed allegory.

Sources: https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1948/06/26/the-lottery, https://www.neh.gov/humanities/2018/winter/feature/the-voice-shirley-jackson-was-unnerving-and-full-foreboding

Key facts

  1. The New Yorker identifies the story’s print publication as June 26, 1948.Sources: https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1948/06/26/the-lottery, https://www.neh.gov/humanities/2018/winter/feature/the-voice-shirley-jackson-was-unnerving-and-full-foreboding
  2. In the story, villagers gather for an annual drawing led by Mr. Summers; the black box and paper slips are remnants of a ritual that has changed over time.Sources: https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1948/06/26/the-lottery
  3. The black-spotted slip marks Tessie Hutchinson, and the villagers stone her.Sources: https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1948/06/26/the-lottery, https://www.neh.gov/humanities/2018/winter/feature/the-voice-shirley-jackson-was-unnerving-and-full-foreboding
  4. Jackson contrasts a sunny, everyday village scene with ominous details—including children gathering stones—so the violence emerges gradually.Sources: https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1948/06/26/the-lottery, https://www.neh.gov/humanities/2018/winter/feature/the-voice-shirley-jackson-was-unnerving-and-full-foreboding
  5. Jackson recalled that early readers’ letters expressed bewilderment, speculation, and anger, reflecting the story’s capacity to unsettle and invite interpretation.Sources: https://www.neh.gov/humanities/2018/winter/feature/the-voice-shirley-jackson-was-unnerving-and-full-foreboding

  • The Voice of Shirley Jackson Was Unnerving and Full of Foreboding — National Endowment for the Humanities
    Publication context, gradual revelation and understated style, the story’s subject and reception, and the range of responses and interpretations.

Rashōmon (short story)

Needs review

Original description: A short story by Ryūnosuke Akutagawa about a starving servant who steals clothing from a woman taking hair from corpses to make wigs.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-23a733cbb88464569674 · Estimated cost: $0.0246

Overview

Ryūnosuke Akutagawa’s “Rashōmon,” first published in 1915, is a Japanese short story set in a decaying Kyoto during the late twelfth century. Its unnamed, recently dismissed servant takes shelter from rain at the ruined city gate, then climbs into an upper chamber where an old woman pulls hair from corpses to make wigs. When she defends her act as necessary to avoid starvation, the servant uses her reasoning to justify stripping her of her kimono. By linking the woman’s explanation to the servant’s sudden choice, the story probes how desperation can reshape moral judgment. Its bleak setting and compact confrontation make survival and ethical compromise its defining concerns.

Sources: https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/rashomon-ryunosuke-akutagawa, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1982/pg1982-images.html, https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/nihonbungaku/35/1/35_KJ00009902123/_article/-char/en

Key facts

  1. “Rashōmon” was first published in 1915; its story is set in late-twelfth-century Kyoto.Sources: https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/rashomon-ryunosuke-akutagawa
  2. The dismissed servant initially weighs theft as a way to survive, then encounters the old woman among corpses in the gate’s upper chamber.Sources: https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/rashomon-ryunosuke-akutagawa, https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1982/pg1982-images.html
  3. The old woman says she plans to make wigs from hair she plucks from a corpse.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1982/pg1982-images.html, https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/rashomon-ryunosuke-akutagawa
  4. To defend herself, she describes the dead woman’s past practice of selling dried snake as dried fish and argues that both women acted to avoid starvation.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1982/pg1982-images.html, https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/literature-and-writing/rashomon-ryunosuke-akutagawa
  5. The servant turns the old woman’s survival-based defense into a justification for taking her kimono; one scholarly reading frames their conflict through idealism and realism, and order and disorder.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1982/pg1982-images.html, https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/nihonbungaku/35/1/35_KJ00009902123/_article/-char/en

Aladdin (character)

Needs review

Original description: The folkloric young hero who discovers a magical lamp and uses the wishes granted by its genie to overcome a sorcerer’s schemes.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-6f0c102460466dd0f90e · Estimated cost: $0.0254

Overview

Aladdin—also rendered Ala al-Din—is the young hero of “Aladdin and the Wonderful Lamp,” a tale now strongly associated with *One Thousand and One Nights*. In a widely circulated version, he is the son of a poor tailor; a sorcerer tricks him into entering a cave to retrieve a lamp, and a magic ring helps him escape. The lamp’s powerful jinni brings him wealth and helps him marry a princess, but the sorcerer later steals the lamp and threatens his new life. The tale was not present in established Arabic editions of the Nights: Antoine Galland included it in his French version after Syrian storyteller Hanna Diyab told it to him in 1709. Galland’s version helped make it famous internationally.

Sources: https://www.colorado.edu/projects/fairy-tales/aladdin-or-the-wonderful-lamp, https://dlcl.stanford.edu/events/path-when-hanna-met-antoine-syrian-storyteller-18th-century-paris-elias-muhanna, https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/alf-layla-wa-layla/

Key facts

  1. The name is also written Ala al-Din or Ala ad-Din.Sources: https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/alf-layla-wa-layla/
  2. In a well-known version, Aladdin is the son of a poor tailor, and a sorcerer sends him to retrieve a lamp from a cave.Sources: https://www.colorado.edu/projects/fairy-tales/aladdin-or-the-wonderful-lamp
  3. The story features two magical helpers: one summoned by the lamp and another by a ring given to Aladdin.Sources: https://www.colorado.edu/projects/fairy-tales/aladdin-or-the-wonderful-lamp
  4. The sorcerer later steals the lamp and transports Aladdin’s palace; Aladdin uses the ring to find it and defeat the sorcerer.Sources: https://www.colorado.edu/projects/fairy-tales/aladdin-or-the-wonderful-lamp
  5. Syrian storyteller Hanna Diyab told the tale to Antoine Galland in 1709; the tale’s inclusion and popularity in Europe are associated with Galland’s French version, rather than established Arabic editions of the Nights.Sources: https://dlcl.stanford.edu/events/path-when-hanna-met-antoine-syrian-storyteller-18th-century-paris-elias-muhanna, https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/alf-layla-wa-layla/

Satan (Paradise Lost)

Needs review

Original description: The fallen angel and central antagonist of John Milton’s epic poem Paradise Lost, who leads the rebellion against God and tempts Adam and Eve.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-bbc03a22cb406d2be545 · Estimated cost: $0.0246

Overview

In John Milton’s Christian epic Paradise Lost, Satan—also called Lucifer—is the fallen angel who leads a revolt against God and becomes the poem’s central antagonist. The story opens with Satan and his followers defeated and cast into Hell, then traces his rebellion and his journey to Eden, where he takes serpent form to tempt Eve. Satan is distinctive for his forceful, self-justifying speeches about freedom and rule. Their appeal makes him a compelling character, but the poem also connects his envy and hunger for power with war, ruin, and separation from God. His role helps shape the poem’s account of both the angels’ fall and humanity’s fall.

Sources: https://www.christs.cam.ac.uk/paradise-lost, https://academic.oup.com/princeton-scholarship-online/book/16275/chapter-abstract/171374307, https://oyc.yale.edu/english/engl-220/lecture-15

Key facts

  1. Milton’s poem opens after Satan and the rebel angels have been defeated and cast into Hell.Sources: https://www.christs.cam.ac.uk/paradise-lost
  2. Satan’s rebellion is linked to envy and the desire for glory and power; the Princeton chapter describes his pursuit as ending in war and destruction.Sources: https://academic.oup.com/princeton-scholarship-online/book/16275/chapter-abstract/171374307
  3. In Book IX, Satan appears as a serpent and persuades Eve to eat the forbidden fruit.Sources: https://www.christs.cam.ac.uk/paradise-lost
  4. Satan’s speeches can sound persuasive, but a Yale lecture cautions that the poem cannot be reduced to those speeches or treated as expressing only one simple viewpoint.Sources: https://oyc.yale.edu/english/engl-220/lecture-15
  5. The poem links Satan’s fall with the Fall of Adam and Eve, giving the two stories contrasting outcomes.Sources: https://academic.oup.com/princeton-scholarship-online/book/16275/chapter-abstract/171374307
Source reference details
  • Paradise Lost — Christ’s College, University of Cambridge
    Supports Satan/Lucifer’s rebellion, the fallen angels’ defeat and casting into Hell, his serpent form and temptation of Eve, and the poem’s two falls.
  • Introduction — Princeton Scholarship Online, Oxford Academic
    David Quint’s chapter abstract describes Satan’s story as a fall driven by envy and the pursuit of glory and temporal power, culminating in war, destruction, and alienation from God; contrasts it with Adam and Eve’s fall.
  • ENGL 220 - Lecture 15 - Paradise Lost, Books V-VI — Open Yale Courses, Yale University
    Lecture transcript discusses Satan’s rebellion, his self-justifying arguments, and why the poem’s ideas cannot be reduced to the speeches of a single character.

Autobiography

Needs review

Original description: A nonfiction literary genre in which an author recounts and interprets their own life, typically in narrative prose.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-6aea9a417b8d390a1feb · Estimated cost: $0.0250

Overview

An autobiography is nonfiction life-writing in which a person retrospectively narrates their own life—or a substantial part of it—rather than someone else’s life, as in biography. It often uses first-person prose, but there is no single required structure: autobiographical works can vary in form and scope. The author does more than list events, selecting and arranging memories to interpret personal identity and development within a social and historical setting. This makes autobiography a literary way to view both an individual’s experience and the world around them, though memory and selection mean it is not a complete, neutral record. “Memoir” is sometimes used interchangeably, but more often focuses on a particular period or theme.

Sources: https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/129.html, https://teachersinstitute.yale.edu/curriculum/units/1985/3/85.03.03/2, https://news.illinois.edu/the-truth-about-memoirs/

Key facts

  1. An autobiography is written by the person whose life it recounts; a biography tells someone else’s life.Sources: https://teachersinstitute.yale.edu/curriculum/units/1985/3/85.03.03/2, https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/129.html
  2. Autobiographies often look back on a person’s life as a whole, while memoirs more often focus on a particular period; writers and critics sometimes use the terms interchangeably.Sources: https://news.illinois.edu/the-truth-about-memoirs/, https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/129.html
  3. First-person retrospective prose is common, but autobiography has no single required structure or form.Sources: https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/129.html, https://teachersinstitute.yale.edu/curriculum/units/1985/3/85.03.03/2
  4. Because autobiographers select, arrange, and interpret memories—and memory can be fallible—their accounts are personal interpretations, not necessarily complete or neutral records.Sources: https://news.illinois.edu/the-truth-about-memoirs/, https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/129.html
  5. Maya Angelou’s I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings is an autobiographical work centered on her life from about age three to sixteen.Sources: https://teachersinstitute.yale.edu/curriculum/units/1985/3/85.03.03/2
Source reference details
  • Autobiography — The Living Handbook of Narratology, University of Hamburg
    Defines autobiography as retrospective narration of the author’s own life or a substantial part; discusses nonfiction claims, first-person conventions, social and historical context, and the shaping role of memory.
  • 85.03.03: Autobiography: Maya Angelou — The Yale-New Haven Teachers Institute
    Discusses autobiography’s flexible form and cites a definition as a retrospective prose account by a real person of their own existence; identifies Angelou’s book as autobiographical and describes its life period.
  • The ‘truth’ about memoirs — University of Illinois News Bureau
    English professor Philip Graham discusses how autobiography often ranges across a life while memoir often treats a particular era, the overlap in usage, and how memory and authorial choices shape life writing.

The Minister's Black Veil

Needs review

Original description: A short story by Nathaniel Hawthorne in which Reverend Hooper wears a black veil symbolizing hidden sin and secrecy.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-cdccdd8ac4a6226fb7d9 · Estimated cost: $0.0272

Overview

“The Minister’s Black Veil: A Parable” is a short story by American writer Nathaniel Hawthorne, included in his 1837 collection Twice-Told Tales. Set in the New England village of Milford, it follows Reverend Mr. Hooper after he appears at church wearing a black crape veil that hides most of his face. The congregation’s fear and his fiancée Elizabeth’s questions make the veil a sign of separation and secrecy; Hooper connects it to hidden sin shared by humanity. Yet Hawthorne never reveals a private offense as the veil’s definite cause. That uncertainty is central: the veil works as a disturbing symbol of what people conceal from one another and themselves, rather than a simple clue to a single secret.

Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/longfellow-and-hawthorne.htm, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/508/508-h/508-h.htm, https://scholarworks.umb.edu/masters_theses/56/

Key facts

  1. The story appeared in Hawthorne’s collection Twice-Told Tales, published in 1837.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/longfellow-and-hawthorne.htm, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/508/508-h/508-h.htm
  2. Hooper’s veil is made of black crape and conceals most of his face, leaving his mouth and chin visible.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/508/508-h/508-h.htm
  3. Hooper refuses to remove the veil even when Elizabeth, his fiancée, asks him to explain it.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/508/508-h/508-h.htm
  4. At the story’s end, Hooper argues that the veil points beyond himself to the hidden sin and secrets people keep from one another.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/508/508-h/508-h.htm
  5. The story leaves Hooper’s personal reason unresolved; a University of Massachusetts Boston thesis also identifies concealment and the attention it attracts as a key feature of the tale.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/508/508-h/508-h.htm, https://scholarworks.umb.edu/masters_theses/56/
Source reference details

Mercutio

Needs review

Original description: A witty friend of Romeo in William Shakespeare’s Romeo and Juliet who delivers the Queen Mab speech and is killed by Tybalt.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-f54253088cd8f3df2aeb · Estimated cost: $0.0135

Overview

Mercutio is a witty, quick-tempered friend of Romeo in William Shakespeare’s tragedy Romeo and Juliet, and a relative of Verona’s Prince. His jokes and wordplay provide comic relief, but his readiness to fight also pulls him into the families’ feud. In Act 1, scene 4, he answers Romeo’s anxiety about dreams with the imaginative Queen Mab speech, portraying a tiny fairy whose visits shape people’s dreams. Later, when Tybalt challenges Romeo and Romeo refuses to fight, Mercutio takes up the challenge. Tybalt fatally wounds him as Romeo tries to intervene; his death drives Romeo to kill Tybalt and marks a turning point toward further tragedy.

Sources: https://www.shakespeare.org.uk/explore-shakespeare/shakespedia/shakespeares-characters/romeo-and-juliet/, https://www.folger.edu/explore/shakespeares-works/romeo-and-juliet/read/

After Romeo declines Tybalt’s challenge, Mercutio fights Tybalt and is fatally wounded while Romeo tries to stop them.Sources: https://www.shakespeare.org.uk/explore-shakespeare/shakespedia/shakespeares-characters/romeo-and-juliet/, https://www.folger.edu/explore/shakespeares-works/romeo-and-juliet/read/

Nobel Prize in Literature

Needs review

Original description: An annual international literary award established in 1901 from Alfred Nobel's estate and awarded by the Swedish Academy.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-c95baa01b0da74cbe4de · Estimated cost: $0.0474

Overview

The Nobel Prize in Literature is one of the prizes Alfred Nobel provided for in his 1895 will. He entrusted the Swedish Academy with choosing the literature laureate, and the first award was presented in 1901. Nobel’s instruction called for outstanding literary work with an ideal purpose—a broad standard, not a single genre. Laureates show the prize’s range: it has recognized poetry, novels, short stories, plays and historical writing, and in 2016 the Academy honored Bob Dylan for poetic expression in songs. This breadth distinguishes the award: literary achievement can take many forms, while the final selection rests with the Swedish Academy.

  3. Nobel’s criterion called for the most outstanding literary work in an ideal direction; the Academy applies that wording when assessing candidates.Sources: https://www.svenskaakademien.se/en/the-nobel-prize-in-literature

Mathematics

Basel Problem

Needs review

Original description: The problem of determining the exact value of the infinite series of reciprocals of the positive integer squares, equal to π²/6.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-a1caf482b2602fb67b31 · Estimated cost: $0.0248

Overview

The Basel problem asks for the exact sum of the infinite series 1/1² + 1/2² + 1/3² + …, whose terms are reciprocals of the positive integers’ squares. Its unexpected answer is π²/6: a question about whole-number squares turns out to involve π, the circle constant. Mathematicians including members of the Bernoulli family had worked on the sum; the name reflects Basel and that family’s association with the city. Leonhard Euler solved the problem in 1735, a celebrated early achievement that helped establish his reputation. The result remains a subject of mathematical study, with researchers continuing to publish different proofs.

Sources: https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk/Biographies/Euler/, https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Applied_Mathematics/The_Essence_of_Mathematics_Through_Elementary_Problems_(Borovik_and_Gardiner)/06:_Infinity_-_Recursion_Induction_Infinite_Descent/6.05:_Some_classical_inequalities, https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/euleriana/vol6/iss1/6/

Key facts

  1. The series adds the reciprocals of the squares of 1, 2, 3, and every subsequent positive integer.Sources: https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Applied_Mathematics/The_Essence_of_Mathematics_Through_Elementary_Problems_(Borovik_and_Gardiner)/06:_Infinity_-_Recursion_Induction_Infinite_Descent/6.05:_Some_classical_inequalities, https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/euleriana/vol6/iss1/6/
  2. Its exact sum is π²/6, linking an infinite series to the circle constant π.Sources: https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk/Biographies/Euler/, https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Applied_Mathematics/The_Essence_of_Mathematics_Through_Elementary_Problems_(Borovik_and_Gardiner)/06:_Infinity_-_Recursion_Induction_Infinite_Descent/6.05:_Some_classical_inequalities
  3. The challenge was to find an exact value, rather than merely estimate the sum with a decimal approximation.Sources: https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Applied_Mathematics/The_Essence_of_Mathematics_Through_Elementary_Problems_(Borovik_and_Gardiner)/06:_Infinity_-_Recursion_Induction_Infinite_Descent/6.05:_Some_classical_inequalities
  4. Euler’s solution, dated to 1735 by the cited histories, followed unsuccessful efforts by mathematicians including Jacob, Johann, and Daniel Bernoulli.Sources: https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk/Biographies/Euler/, https://plus.maths.org/basel-problem
  5. The name refers to Basel and the Bernoulli family’s association with the city; Jakob Bernoulli helped popularize the problem.Sources: https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Applied_Mathematics/The_Essence_of_Mathematics_Through_Elementary_Problems_(Borovik_and_Gardiner)/06:_Infinity_-_Recursion_Induction_Infinite_Descent/6.05:_Some_classical_inequalities
  6. A 2026 historical study documents the continuing publication of peer-reviewed papers devoted to proofs of Euler’s formula.Sources: https://scholarlycommons.pacific.edu/euleriana/vol6/iss1/6/

    Defines the Basel problem’s series, gives Euler’s value and 1735 solution, and describes the earlier attempts and the result’s importance to his early reputation.

    Explains the series, why an exact value was sought, the Basel naming connection to Jakob Bernoulli and the city, and Euler’s solution.

    Defines the problem and formula and describes the continuing scholarly study of proofs.

Limit (mathematics)

Needs review

Original description: A mathematical limit is the value that a function or sequence approaches as its input or index approaches a specified value or infinity.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-965731e7fb8bd35a18c8 · Estimated cost: $0.0345

Overview

In mathematics, a limit describes the value that a function’s outputs approach as its input nears a chosen point, or that a sequence’s terms approach as its index grows. The key is nearby behavior, not necessarily the value at the point: a function may be undefined there, or its defined value may differ from its limit. For a two-sided function limit, outputs from both sides must approach the same finite value; otherwise, no such finite limit exists. Limits are foundational to calculus: continuity at a point requires the limit to equal the function’s value there, and the derivative is defined using a limit of slopes of nearby secant lines.

Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/2-2-the-limit-of-a-function, https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-2/pages/5-1-sequences, https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/2-4-continuity, https://math.hmc.edu/calculus/hmc-mathematics-calculus-online-tutorials/single-variable-calculus/limit-definition-of-the-derivative/

Key facts

  1. The notation lim indicates a limit; for a function, x→a means examining outputs as inputs approach a, not necessarily at a.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/2-2-the-limit-of-a-function
  2. A function’s limit can differ from its value at the point, and the function need not be defined at that point for the limit to exist.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/2-2-the-limit-of-a-function
  3. A sequence is a function on the positive integers; its limit describes how its terms behave as the index n grows.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-2/pages/5-1-sequences
  4. Continuity at a point requires that the function be defined there, that its limit exist, and that the limit equal the function’s value.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/2-4-continuity
  5. A derivative uses a limit: secant-line slopes approach the tangent-line slope as the second point moves toward the first.Sources: https://math.hmc.edu/calculus/hmc-mathematics-calculus-online-tutorials/single-variable-calculus/limit-definition-of-the-derivative/
Source reference details
  • 2.2 The Limit of a Function - Calculus Volume 1 — OpenStax
    Intuitive function-limit definition, behavior from either side, and the distinction between a function’s limit and its value at the point.
  • 5.1 Sequences - Calculus Volume 2 — OpenStax
    Sequences as functions on positive integers and convergence as the index grows.
  • 2.4 Continuity - Calculus Volume 1 — OpenStax
    The three conditions for continuity at a point, including equality of the limit and function value.
  • Limit Definition of the Derivative – HMC Calculus Tutorial — Harvey Mudd College, HMC Calculus Tutorial
    Secant-line slopes, their limiting tangent-line slope, and the derivative’s dependence on a limit.

Geometric congruence

Needs review

Original description: The geometric relationship in which figures have the same shape and size, with corresponding parts congruent.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-947d1cc5a9e0a20921fc · Estimated cost: $0.0243

Overview

Geometric congruence describes figures that match exactly in shape and size: one can be placed onto the other with no stretching or resizing. A translation (slide), rotation (turn), or reflection (flip) may change a figure’s position or orientation without changing its measurements, so corresponding side lengths and angle measures remain equal. A mirror image can therefore still be congruent. This differs from similarity, which allows figures to have the same shape but different sizes. Congruence helps identify exact matches in patterns and is useful in proving triangles match: rules such as SSS and SAS let certain corresponding measurements establish triangle congruence without measuring every part.

Sources: https://cms-assets.illustrativemathematics.org/bmh5wekf6f7x9hju8obyyymduy62, https://openstax.org/books/contemporary-mathematics/pages/10-3-triangles, https://openstax.org/books/elementary-algebra-2e/pages/8-7-solve-proportion-and-similar-figure-applications, https://openstax.org/books/contemporary-mathematics/pages/10-5-tessellations

Key facts

  1. Figures are congruent when translations, rotations, and reflections can map one exactly onto the other; these rigid motions preserve measurements.Sources: https://cms-assets.illustrativemathematics.org/bmh5wekf6f7x9hju8obyyymduy62
  2. Corresponding sides of congruent figures have equal lengths, and corresponding angles have equal measures.Sources: https://cms-assets.illustrativemathematics.org/bmh5wekf6f7x9hju8obyyymduy62, https://openstax.org/books/contemporary-mathematics/pages/10-3-triangles
  3. A reflection can make one figure a mirror image of another without preventing the figures from being congruent.Sources: https://cms-assets.illustrativemathematics.org/xqex4y12u54d63aif72z25pd6x1k
  4. Similar figures can have the same shape but different sizes; congruent figures also have matching size measurements.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/elementary-algebra-2e/pages/8-7-solve-proportion-and-similar-figure-applications, https://openstax.org/books/contemporary-mathematics/pages/10-3-triangles
  5. For triangles, SSS, SAS, ASA, and AAS are criteria that can establish congruence from selected corresponding side and angle measurements.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/contemporary-mathematics/pages/10-3-triangles
Source reference details

    Defines congruence using translations, rotations, and reflections, and explains that rigid transformations preserve measurements, including corresponding side lengths and angle measures.
  • 10.3 Triangles - Contemporary Mathematics — OpenStax
    Explains triangle congruence, the SSS, SAS, ASA, and AAS criteria, and contrasts congruent and similar triangles.
  • 8.7 Solve Proportion and Similar Figure Applications - Elementary Algebra 2e — OpenStax
    Distinguishes similar figures, which may differ in size, by their corresponding equal angles and proportional side lengths.
  • 10.5 Tessellations - Contemporary Mathematics — OpenStax

Expected Value

Needs review

Original description: The probability-weighted average of a random variable’s possible outcomes, also called its expectation or first moment.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-6128973c5658c2797eac · Estimated cost: $0.0130

Overview

Expected value, also called expectation or the mean, is a number that summarizes a random variable by weighting its possible values according to their probabilities. For a discrete variable, multiply each possible value by its probability and add; for a continuous variable with density f(x), the corresponding calculation is the integral of x f(x) over its range. It is commonly written E[X] or E(X), and is the first moment. Expected value can describe an average over many repetitions, but it need not be an outcome of any single trial: a fair six-sided die has expected value 3.5, although no roll shows 3.5.

Sources: https://openstax.org/books/statistics/pages/4-2-mean-or-expected-value-and-standard-deviation, https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-042j-mathematics-for-computer-science-fall-2010/93cad640cf3ed0b23ef70688f452d4d5_MIT6_042JF10_notes.pdf, https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-02-multivariable-calculus-spring-2006/37ab9973a0787cd9de2492347b180034_lecnotes_prob.pdf

Key facts

  1. For a discrete random variable, calculate E(X) by adding each possible value multiplied by its probability.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/statistics/pages/4-2-mean-or-expected-value-and-standard-deviation
  2. For a continuous random variable with density f(x), its expected value is the integral of x f(x) over the range.Sources: https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-02-multivariable-calculus-spring-2006/37ab9973a0787cd9de2492347b180034_lecnotes_prob.pdf
  3. A fair six-sided die has expected value 3.5, even though 3.5 cannot appear on an individual roll.Sources: https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/6-042j-mathematics-for-computer-science-fall-2010/93cad640cf3ed0b23ef70688f452d4d5_MIT6_042JF10_notes.pdf
  4. For a discrete random variable, expected value is also called its mean or long-term average.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/statistics/pages/4-2-mean-or-expected-value-and-standard-deviation
  5. The expectation of X is its first moment; more generally, moments describe expectations of powers or other functions of X.Sources: https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-02-multivariable-calculus-spring-2006/37ab9973a0787cd9de2492347b180034_lecnotes_prob.pdf
Source reference details

    Defines expected value for discrete random variables as the probability-weighted sum, identifies it with the mean or long-term average, and explains the long-run interpretation.
  • 6.042J Complete course notes — MIT OpenCourseWare
    Defines expectation as a probability-weighted average and gives the fair-die example, including that its expectation need not be an attainable outcome.

Natural logarithm

Checks passed

Original description: The logarithm to base e, commonly denoted ln, and the antiderivative of 1/x on intervals not containing zero.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-03998868a6b7ad74a4fa · Estimated cost: $0.0129

Overview

The natural logarithm, written ln x, is the logarithm to base e: it answers the question “to what power must e be raised to produce x?” For positive x, it can also be defined by the integral ln x = ∫₁ˣ (1/t) dt, making its value the signed area under y = 1/t between 1 and x. This definition explains its key calculus role: its derivative is 1/x, so an antiderivative of 1/x is ln|x| + C on any interval that does not cross zero. The number e is characterized by ln e = 1, and ln is the inverse of the exponential function eˣ.

Sources: https://mathworld.wolfram.com/NaturalLogarithm.html, https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-7-integrals-exponential-functions-and-logarithms

Key facts

  1. ln x is the logarithm of x to base e, where e is approximately 2.71828.Sources: https://mathworld.wolfram.com/NaturalLogarithm.html
  2. For x > 0, ln x = ∫₁ˣ (1/t) dt; when 0 < x < 1, this integral is negative.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-7-integrals-exponential-functions-and-logarithms
  3. For x > 0, the derivative of ln x is 1/x.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-7-integrals-exponential-functions-and-logarithms
  4. For x ≠ 0, an antiderivative of 1/x is ln|x| + C; the absolute value allows the formula on negative intervals too.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-7-integrals-exponential-functions-and-logarithms
  5. The number e is characterized by ln e = 1, and the natural logarithm and exponential function are inverses.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-7-integrals-exponential-functions-and-logarithms
Source reference details
  • Natural Logarithm — Wolfram MathWorld
    Identifies ln as the logarithm to base e, gives the approximate value of e, the integral definition for positive x, and the derivative 1/x.
  • 6.7 Integrals, Exponential Functions, and Logarithms - Calculus Volume 1 — OpenStax
    Supports the integral definition, signed-area interpretation, derivative, antiderivative ln|x| + C, characterization ln e = 1, and inverse relationship with the exponential function.

Popular Media and Entertainment

ByteDance

Needs review

Original description: A Chinese technology company that owns TikTok and operates other internet-content and social-media services.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-f15148f4c6221d78c4f3 · Estimated cost: $0.0248

Overview

ByteDance is a Beijing-based Chinese internet technology company founded in 2012 by a team led by Zhang Yiming and Liang Rubo. It became known first for Toutiao, a news and information app that recommends material to readers, then for video services: Douyin launched in China in 2016, and TikTok followed for international audiences in 2017. TikTok’s 2018 merger with Musical.ly helped it expand into short-form video and popular culture worldwide. ByteDance is more than TikTok’s parent: its portfolio also includes tools such as CapCut and Lark, and China-focused services such as Toutiao and Douyin. Its significance lies in building a diverse media-and-content business that reaches audiences through multiple apps.

Daft Punk

Needs review

Original description: A French electronic music duo consisting of Thomas Bangalter and Guy-Manuel de Homem-Christo, known for helmeted robot imagery and songs such as “One More Time,” “Harder, Better, Faster, Stronger,” and “Get Lucky.”

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-5bce6f33e80d2ffeb2de · Estimated cost: $0.0243

Overview

Daft Punk was a French electronic music duo formed in Paris in 1993 by Thomas Bangalter and Guy-Manuel de Homem-Christo; the pair announced its end in 2021. Their music helped bring French house to a wider audience: their 1997 debut Homework established them as influential house innovators, while 2001’s Discovery expanded their sound toward pop and helped make them international stars. They became recognizable through their robot helmets, a visual identity introduced around Discovery, and audiovisual live shows, including a famous pyramid stage design. Songs such as “One More Time,” “Harder, Better, Faster, Stronger,” and “Get Lucky” are among their best-known work; their 2013 album Random Access Memories won the 2014 Grammy for Album of the Year.

Mickey Mouse

Needs review

Original description: An iconic Disney cartoon mouse character who debuted in the 1928 animated short Steamboat Willie.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-aa7e112ffc612dd57751 · Estimated cost: $0.0240

Overview

Mickey Mouse is a fictional cartoon character created for Walt Disney’s animation studio, with Ub Iwerks designing and animating the early films and Disney directing. Mickey became known to movie audiences through *Steamboat Willie*, which opened in New York on November 18, 1928. Although two Mickey shorts had been made earlier, *Steamboat Willie* was released first. In it, the lively, mischievous mouse works as a steamboat deckhand. The short’s carefully synchronized music and sound effects helped make it a landmark in animation, while Mickey’s playful screen presence launched a long-running career as Disney’s signature character and an internationally recognized cartoon figure.

Sources: https://www.moma.org/collection/works/302797?art_term_slug=film&sov_referrer=art_term, https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/steamboat_willie.pdf?loclr=blogflt, https://d23.com/a-to-z/mickey-mouse/

Key facts

  1. Walt Disney and his staff developed Mickey after Disney lost control of his earlier character, Oswald the Lucky Rabbit; Ub Iwerks was assigned to design Mickey with input from Disney.Sources: https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/steamboat_willie.pdf?loclr=blogflt
  2. *Plane Crazy* and *The Gallopin’ Gaucho* were made before *Steamboat Willie*, but the earlier shorts were silent and *Steamboat Willie* was released first.Sources: https://www.moma.org/collection/works/302797?art_term_slug=film&sov_referrer=art_term, https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/steamboat_willie.pdf?loclr=blogflt
  3. *Steamboat Willie* opened at New York’s Colony Theater on November 18, 1928, the date commonly used as Mickey’s screen debut.Sources: https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/steamboat_willie.pdf?loclr=blogflt, https://d23.com/a-to-z/mickey-mouse/
  4. The film was made with synchronized sound in mind: its music and effects were planned to match the action on screen.Sources: https://www.moma.org/collection/works/302797?art_term_slug=film&sov_referrer=art_term, https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/steamboat_willie.pdf?loclr=blogflt
  5. In the short, Mickey is a mischievous deckhand who turns objects and animals aboard the boat into makeshift musical instruments.Sources: https://www.moma.org/collection/works/302797?art_term_slug=film&sov_referrer=art_term

Walt Disney

Needs review

Original description: American animator, filmmaker, producer, and entrepreneur who co-founded the Walt Disney Company and helped create or develop Mickey Mouse and other animated characters.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-fe16029b079a308483a1 · Estimated cost: $0.0134

Overview

Walter Elias Disney (1901–1966) was an American animator, filmmaker, and producer who started an animation business with his brother Roy after moving to Hollywood in 1923. He became widely associated with Mickey Mouse: Walt conceived the character, while artist Ub Iwerks further developed it and animated the early cartoons. Mickey’s public debut in Steamboat Willie (1928), one of the first cartoons to use synchronized sound successfully, helped establish the studio. Disney’s influence also came from treating animation as feature-length storytelling. Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs (1937) was his studio’s first feature—not the first animated feature ever—and combined expressive character animation, music, color, and a carefully developed story. Disney later helped create Disneyland, which opened in 1955.

Sources: https://www.waltdisney.org/sites/default/files/walt-disney/walt-disney.html, https://www.waltdisney.org/blog/birth-mouse, https://d23.com/about-walt-disney/, https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/snow_white_dwarfs.rev.pdf

  1. In 1923, Walt and Roy Disney began a production operation in Hollywood, making Alice Comedies shorts.Sources: https://d23.com/about-walt-disney/
  2. Walt conceived Mickey Mouse, and Ub Iwerks further developed the character; Steamboat Willie gave Mickey his first public appearance on November 18, 1928.Sources: https://www.waltdisney.org/blog/birth-mouse, https://d23.com/about-walt-disney/
  3. Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs was the Disney studio’s first feature-length film, but it was not the first animated feature. Its blend of animation techniques, color, music, and story helped make it a landmark.Sources: https://www.loc.gov/static/programs/national-film-preservation-board/documents/snow_white_dwarfs.rev.pdf
  4. Disney’s 1930s Silly Symphonies shorts served as a testing ground for animation advances, including color and techniques that created an illusion of depth.Sources: https://www.waltdisney.org/sites/default/files/walt-disney/walt-disney.html
  5. Disneyland, the theme park Disney pursued with his team, opened on July 17, 1955.Sources: https://www.waltdisney.org/sites/default/files/walt-disney/walt-disney.html

Religion and Mythology

Satyr

Needs review

Original description: A satyr is a masculine woodland or nature spirit in Greek mythology, typically depicted with human features and animal attributes such as goat ears, legs, horns, or a tail, and associated with music and revelry.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-81ae316e745db69bdefc · Estimated cost: $0.0354

Overview

Satyrs are male nature spirits in Greek mythology, imagined as humanlike beings with animal features. Their appearance varies across art: some Greek depictions give them horse-like ears and tails, while other portrayals show goatlike legs, ears, or horns. They were linked with woodland settings and often accompanied Dionysus (also called Bacchus), the god of wine and theater. Artists commonly show them with music, wine, dancing, and celebration. Satyrs also became figures in Greek theater: satyr plays used a chorus of satyrs and brought comic, earthy contrast to tragic myths. Vase paintings and sculpture show how artists reimagined these spirits over time.

Sources: https://www.getty.edu/news/why-are-ancient-greek-phalluses-funny/, https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/203912

Key facts

  1. Satyr imagery was not uniform: some Greek representations show horse-like ears and tails, while other art gives satyrs goatlike features.Sources: https://www.getty.edu/news/why-are-ancient-greek-phalluses-funny/, https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/203912
  2. Satyrs often accompanied Dionysus, the wine god; art shows them with pipes, wine vessels, or dancing in celebration.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/203912
  3. Satyr plays featured a satyr chorus and were performed after a trio of tragedies, adding comedy to mythic subjects.Sources: https://www.getty.edu/news/why-are-ancient-greek-phalluses-funny/
  4. A mid-fifth-century BCE Attic red-figure cup fragment at the Met depicts satyrs with a maenad and drinking or Dionysian attributes.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/719014
  5. A Greek terracotta statuette at the Met, dated to the late fourth–early third century BCE, shows a satyr wearing a wreath and holding a cornucopia.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/248614
Source reference details

    Satyrs as Dionysus’s followers, their horse-like ears and tails, their appearance in satyr plays, and their presence on symposium vases.

    Satyrs’ woodland setting, association with Bacchus, goatlike features, and musical instruments.
  • Attributed to the Veii Painter - Terracotta fragment of a kylix (drinking cup) — The Metropolitan Museum of Art

  • Terracotta statuette of a satyr — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    The date and attributes of a Greek terracotta satyr statuette: wreath and cornucopia.

Kashrut (Jewish dietary laws)

Needs review

Original description: The body of Jewish dietary laws governing which foods may be eaten and how they are prepared.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-1f2ab86b91553d6733ed · Estimated cost: $0.0248

Overview

Kashrut, also spelled kashruth, is the Jewish system of dietary laws and practices; “kosher” means fit or permitted under that system. Its scriptural foundations include the Torah’s classifications of animals, while rabbinic interpretation developed further rules for preparing, slaughtering, and serving food—so kosher status concerns more than ingredients alone. Distinctive rules include permitting land animals that chew the cud and have split hooves, allowing fish with fins and scales, and separating meat and dairy, often with separate utensils. Foods that are neither meat nor dairy are called pareve. Kashrut connects everyday eating with Jewish religious tradition, though observance and interpretation differ among Jewish communities.

Sources: https://reformjudaism.org/learning/torah-study/torah-commentary/you-are-what-you-eat-new-world-kosher-food, https://reformjudaism.org/glossary/kosher, https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/6735256/jewish/Kosher-FAQ.htm

Key facts

  1. “Kosher” means “fit” or “proper”; it describes food permitted under kashrut.Sources: https://reformjudaism.org/glossary/kosher
  2. The Torah provides kashrut’s scriptural foundations; rabbinic interpretation added rules about food preparation, slaughter, and separating meat and dairy.Sources: https://reformjudaism.org/learning/torah-study/torah-commentary/you-are-what-you-eat-new-world-kosher-food
  3. Among the permitted foods, land animals must chew the cud and have split hooves; kosher fish have fins and scales.Sources: https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/6735256/jewish/Kosher-FAQ.htm
  4. Meat and dairy are kept separate, often using separate dishes and utensils; foods that are neither are commonly called pareve.Sources: https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/6735256/jewish/Kosher-FAQ.htm
  5. For meat to be kosher, the FAQ describes ritual slaughter called shechitah and removal of blood; it also notes that observance and discussion of kashrut have developed differently across Jewish movements.Sources: https://www.chabad.org/library/article_cdo/aid/6735256/jewish/Kosher-FAQ.htm, https://reformjudaism.org/learning/torah-study/torah-commentary/you-are-what-you-eat-new-world-kosher-food
Source reference details

  • kosher — Union for Reform Judaism (Reform Judaism)
    Definition of kosher as ritually fit or proper and permitted under kashrut.
  • Kosher FAQ — Chabad.org, a division of the Chabad-Lubavitch Media Center
    Examples of permitted animals and fish, shechitah and blood removal, meat-dairy separation, separate utensils, and the pareve category.

The Hero with a Thousand Faces

Needs review

Original description: Joseph Campbell’s 1949 comparative mythology study presenting the monomyth, or hero’s journey, as a recurring narrative pattern.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-a82c2659ca447212e2f2 · Estimated cost: $0.0363

Overview

Joseph Campbell’s The Hero with a Thousand Faces is a comparative mythology book first published by Pantheon Books in 1949. Drawing on myths, folklore, religion, and modern psychology, Campbell proposed a “monomyth”: a recurring hero-story pattern that he believed appeared across cultures. Its central movement is departure from familiar life, initiation through trials and transformation, and return; the pattern can include crossing into a strange or supernatural realm, gaining a boon, and bringing something back. The book also examines myths of creation and cosmic dissolution. Its lasting significance is that it gave readers and storytellers an influential lens for comparing narratives, including modern films. Campbell’s model is an interpretation, not a rule that every culture’s stories must follow.

Sources: https://www.jcf.org/learn/joseph-campbell-heros-journey, https://www.jcf.org/learn/joseph-campbell-collected-works, https://books.google.com/books/about/The_Hero_with_a_Thousand_Faces.html?id=PlvXAAAAMAAJ, https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/147470490500300108

Key facts

  1. The first edition was published in 1949 by Pantheon Books as part of the Bollingen Series.Sources: https://books.google.com/books/about/The_Hero_with_a_Thousand_Faces.html?id=PlvXAAAAMAAJ
  2. Campbell called his pattern the “monomyth,” a term he borrowed from writer James Joyce.Sources: https://www.jcf.org/learn/joseph-campbell-heros-journey
  3. Campbell described its basic arc as departure, initiation, and return, while allowing for variations in the stages.Sources: https://www.jcf.org/learn/joseph-campbell-heros-journey
  4. The book also presents the Cosmogonic Cycle, a pattern about the creation and dissolution of worlds.Sources: https://www.jcf.org/learn/joseph-campbell-collected-works
  5. Campbell’s claim of a recurring pattern across cultures is a comparative theory, not a fixed formula every story must follow; scholars have debated claims about literary universals.Sources: https://www.jcf.org/learn/joseph-campbell-heros-journey, https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/147470490500300108
Source reference details
  • Joseph Campbell and the Hero’s Journey — Joseph Campbell Foundation
    Book’s 1949 origin, monomyth terminology and Joyce attribution, departure-initiation-return structure, variation, and the Foundation’s account of Campbell’s cross-cultural thesis.
  • The Collected Works of Joseph Campbell — Joseph Campbell Foundation
    The book’s connection of psychology and comparative mythology, its Cosmogonic Cycle, and its influence among creative artists.
  • The Hero with a Thousand Faces — Google Books
    Bibliographic record for the 1949 Pantheon Books edition and the book’s comparative examples from folklore and religion.
  • The Heroine with a Thousand Faces: Universal Trends in the Characterization of Female Folk Tale Protagonists — SAGE Publications, Evolutionary Psychology
    Scholarly discussion of Campbell’s cross-cultural universality thesis and broader debate about literary universals.

Excalibur

Needs review

Original description: The legendary magical sword associated with King Arthur in Arthurian tradition.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-5ba4084c561fb1c42cd4 · Estimated cost: $0.0257

Overview

Excalibur is the legendary sword associated with King Arthur, a ruler whose stories became a major part of medieval literature. Its significance varies by version, but it is often tied to rightful kingship and supernatural aid. In Thomas Malory’s *Le Morte d’Arthur*, Arthur first draws a sword from a stone; after that sword breaks, the Lady of the Lake gives him Excalibur. Thus, in Malory, the two swords are distinct, though other tellings connect or combine the motifs. Malory also gives Excalibur a magical scabbard that prevents its wearer from bleeding. The sword’s shifting origins show how Arthurian storytellers reshaped the legend over time.

Sources: https://www.bl.uk/stories/blogs/posts/king-arthur-fable-fact-and-fiction, https://fantasy.bnf.fr/en/grand/fan_108.php, https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1251/1251-h/1251-h.htm

Key facts

  1. In Malory’s story, Arthur pulls a sword from a stone to demonstrate his right to rule, then later receives Excalibur from the Lady of the Lake.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1251/1251-h/1251-h.htm
  2. Other medieval tellings connect the sword-in-the-stone episode with Excalibur; a 13th-century French manuscript depicts Arthur drawing Excalibur from an anvil or stone.Sources: https://fantasy.bnf.fr/en/grand/fan_108.php
  3. Malory says Excalibur’s scabbard keeps its wearer from losing blood, even when badly wounded.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1251/1251-h/1251-h.htm
  4. Arthurian stories developed across medieval texts: the British Library describes later writers adding episodes about Merlin, the Round Table, and Arthur’s knights.Sources: https://www.bl.uk/stories/blogs/posts/king-arthur-fable-fact-and-fiction
  5. In Malory’s account, Arthur takes Excalibur and its scabbard from a barge beside a lake after Merlin directs him to the Lady of the Lake.Sources: https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1251/1251-h/1251-h.htm
Source reference details
  • King Arthur: fable, fact and fiction — The British Library
    Arthur’s place in medieval literature, the development of Arthurian stories across later writers, and the sword-in-the-stone motif in medieval manuscript tradition.
  • Excalibur: Arthur Drawing the Sword from the Anvil — Bibliothèque nationale de France
    A medieval version identifying the sword Arthur draws from stone/anvil as Excalibur and its connection to rightful kingship.
  • The Project Gutenberg eBook of Le Morte D’Arthur, Volume I (of II), by Thomas Malory — Project Gutenberg
    Primary-text evidence for Arthur’s sword in the stone, his later receipt of Excalibur from the Lady of the Lake, and the protective scabbard.

Calydonian Boar Hunt

Needs review

Original description: A Greek mythic hunt in which heroes including Meleager and Atalanta pursued and killed the monstrous boar that ravaged Calydon.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-398e418edb09cbebb713 · Estimated cost: $0.0340

Overview

The Calydonian Boar Hunt is a Greek myth about a dangerous beast sent to ravage the countryside around Calydon. In the version summarized by the Metropolitan Museum of Art, Artemis sends the boar after King Oeneus fails to include her in his offerings; Oeneus’s son Meleager gathers famous Greek hunters to stop it. The hunt is especially remembered for Atalanta, a skilled huntress who wounds the boar first, and Meleager, who delivers the fatal blow. Meleager then awards her the animal’s pelt, a decision that leads to a quarrel over the prize. The story’s dramatic hunt and trophy-giving scene inspired later artworks.

Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/748525, https://ogcma.byu.edu/MeleagerCalydonianBoar1.0000_Reid.htm

Key facts

  1. The myth places the hunt in the countryside of Calydon; Artemis sends the boar after King Oeneus omits her from his offerings.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/748525
  2. Meleager assembles a band of celebrated Greek heroes to hunt the beast.Sources: https://ogcma.byu.edu/MeleagerCalydonianBoar1.0000_Reid.htm, https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/748525
  3. Atalanta is the huntress who wounds the boar first; Meleager kills it.Sources: https://ogcma.byu.edu/MeleagerCalydonianBoar1.0000_Reid.htm
  4. Meleager gives Atalanta the boar’s pelt, provoking a dispute with his uncles over the trophy.Sources: https://ogcma.byu.edu/MeleagerCalydonianBoar1.0000_Reid.htm
  5. Later artists depicted both the pursuit and the aftermath, including Meleager presenting the boar’s head to Atalanta.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/714888, https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/748525
Source reference details
  • Etienne Delaune - Atalanta and Meleager chasing the Calydonian Boars — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    The story’s Artemis-related cause, the gathering of hunters, and the scene of Atalanta wounding the boar while Meleager joins the hunt.
  • Meleager: Calydonian Boar Hunt — Brigham Young University, Online Guide to Classical Mythology in the Arts
    Meleager’s leadership, Atalanta’s first wound, Meleager’s killing of the boar, and the ensuing dispute over the pelt.
  • Guillaume Boichot - Vase depicting the Calydonian boar hunt (one of a pair) — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    Later artistic depictions of the hunt and of Meleager presenting the boar’s head to Atalanta.

Xibalba

Needs review

Original description: The underworld in K’iche’ Maya mythology, ruled by death gods and featured prominently in the Popol Vuh.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-98f7b847bdfe7e3ac384 · Estimated cost: $0.0139

Overview

Xibalba (also spelled Xibalbá and sometimes called “Place of Fear”) is the underworld in Maya myth, especially the K’iche’ Maya tradition recorded in the Popol Vuh. The Getty describes it as a realm below earth associated with death and powerful rulers. In this story, underworld lords challenge the Hero Twins, who ultimately defeat them through cunning rather than strength. Xibalba therefore forms the setting for a major encounter between the twins and death-associated powers. The myth’s influence is also explored through Maya ceramics: museum specialists connect some Classic-period vessels with underworld figures and possible Popol Vuh episodes, though identifying exact scenes can remain uncertain.

Sources: https://www.getty.edu/cona/CONAIconographyRecord.aspx?iconid=901000045, https://artmuseum.princeton.edu/art/collections/objects/32221, https://umaine.edu/hudsonmuseum/worldviews/the-underworld/

  1. The Getty lists Xibalba, Xibalbá, and “Place of Fear” as names for this Maya legendary place.Sources: https://www.getty.edu/cona/CONAIconographyRecord.aspx?iconid=901000045
  2. In the Popol Vuh, Xibalba is described as a court below the earth associated with death and powerful rulers.Sources: https://www.getty.edu/cona/CONAIconographyRecord.aspx?iconid=901000045
  3. The Popol Vuh’s Hero Twins defeat the underworld lords through trickery; Princeton notes that identifying a related scene on its Maya vase is not certain.Sources: https://artmuseum.princeton.edu/art/collections/objects/32221
  4. The University of Maine Hudson Museum calls the Popol Vuh the “Book of Counsel” and connects it to K’iche’ Maya tradition.Sources: https://umaine.edu/hudsonmuseum/maya/ballgame/
  5. The Hudson Museum notes that some Classic-period ceramics have been interpreted as related to the Popol Vuh, while describing those connections as interpretations.Sources: https://umaine.edu/hudsonmuseum/worldviews/the-underworld/

Lord's Prayer

Needs review

Original description: The central Christian prayer taught by Jesus, beginning with an address to God as “Our Father in heaven.”

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-ab5c94551934034f9f35 · Estimated cost: $0.0251

Overview

The Lord’s Prayer, also called the Our Father or Pater Noster, is a central Christian prayer that Christian scripture presents as taught by Jesus. Matthew places it within the Sermon on the Mount, while Luke gives a shorter version after a disciple asks Jesus how to pray. The prayer addresses God as “Our Father” and moves through requests concerning God’s name and kingdom, daily needs, forgiveness, and protection from evil. Its use of “our” makes it a shared, communal prayer. The two Gospel versions differ in wording and length, and Christian traditions vary in how they conclude it.

Sources: https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Matthew+6%3A9-13&version=NRSVUE, https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Luke+11%3A1-4&version=NRSVUE, https://www.vatican.va/content/catechism/en/part_four/section_two.html, https://www.posenlibrary.com/entry/jesus-prayer-jewish-models

Key facts

  1. The prayer appears in Matthew 6:9–13 and Luke 11:2–4; the Vatican Catechism describes Matthew’s form as seven petitions and Luke’s as five.Sources: https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Matthew+6%3A9-13&version=NRSVUE, https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Luke+11%3A1-4&version=NRSVUE, https://www.vatican.va/content/catechism/en/part_four/section_two.html
  2. In Matthew, it follows Jesus’ teaching against showy prayer and begins with an instruction to pray in this manner.Sources: https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Matthew+6%3A9-13&version=NRSVUE
  3. Its petitions include God’s name and kingdom, daily bread, forgiveness, and deliverance from evil or testing; wording differs among versions.Sources: https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Matthew+6%3A9-13&version=NRSVUE, https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Luke+11%3A1-4&version=NRSVUE, https://www.vatican.va/content/catechism/en/part_four/section_two.html
  4. The early Christian Didache also records a version and instructs its readers to pray it three times daily; this is evidence of what that text prescribed, not proof of how widely the practice was followed.Sources: https://www.posenlibrary.com/entry/jesus-prayer-jewish-models
  5. The Didache’s version shares phrases and themes with Jewish prayers, while its wording differs from the versions in Matthew and Luke.Sources: https://www.posenlibrary.com/entry/jesus-prayer-jewish-models

Parvati

Needs review

Original description: A major Hindu goddess, consort of Shiva and mother of Ganesha and Karthikeya, regarded as the rebirth or form of Sati.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-b847a78411d725788fae · Estimated cost: $0.0136

Overview

Parvati is a major Hindu goddess, known especially as Shiva’s consort and as the mother of Ganesha and Skanda (also called Kartikeya). Hindu stories and images present her as both a partner in Shiva’s divine life and a nurturing figure at the heart of their family; some accounts describe her as Sati reborn. She also appears beside Shiva in Ardhanari, a shared form that represents the divine as both female and male. South Indian Somaskanda sculptures make these family relationships visible, placing Parvati and Shiva on either side of their son Skanda. Such images are devotional art as well as depictions of mythological figures.

Sources: https://asia-archive.si.edu/exhibition/devi-the-great-goddess/, https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/parvati/jQGXv_H3uumtSA, https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/39327

Key facts

  1. A Museum of Fine Arts, Houston account describes Parvati as Sati, Shiva’s first consort, reborn; this is a mythological tradition, not a historical claim.Sources: https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/parvati/jQGXv_H3uumtSA
  2. One legend says Ganesha was formed from dirt Parvati rubbed from her body, which Shiva then brought to life.Sources: https://asia-archive.si.edu/exhibition/devi-the-great-goddess/
  3. In Ardhanari, Parvati and Shiva share one form, representing the divine as both female and male.Sources: https://asia-archive.si.edu/exhibition/devi-the-great-goddess/
  4. A South Indian Somaskanda sculpture shows Parvati and Shiva with Skanda between them; The Met describes this as a popular religious image in South India.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/art/collection/search/39327
  5. A c. 11th-century bronze Parvati figure was made for temple worship during the Chola dynasty, according to the Museum of Fine Arts, Houston’s collection entry.Sources: https://artsandculture.google.com/asset/parvati/jQGXv_H3uumtSA

  • Parvati — Google Arts & Culture; collection entry from the Museum of Fine Arts, Houston

Guru (Sikhism)

Needs review

Original description: A title for spiritual leaders in Sikhism, culminating in the eternal authority of the Guru Granth Sahib after the human Gurus.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-81b0adf6d0aa8d643124 · Estimated cost: $0.0128

Overview

In Sikhism, Guru denotes an authoritative spiritual guide who conveys divine wisdom; the title has a specific meaning in the tradition beyond its general use for a teacher. Sikh history recognizes ten human Gurus, beginning with Guru Nanak, who founded the faith in Punjab in the late fifteenth century. The human succession ended with Guru Gobind Singh in 1708. Sikhs recognize the Guru Granth Sahib—their sacred scripture—as the eternal Guru and continuing spiritual authority. This transition distinguishes Sikh Guruship: spiritual guidance continues through the scripture rather than a successor in human form. The Gurus’ teachings are central to Sikhism and are enshrined in the Guru Granth Sahib.

Sources: https://sgpc.net/ten-guru-sahibs/, https://new.sgpc.net/ten-guru-sahibs/guru-gobind-singh-sahib/, https://digital.gndu.ac.in/SikhStudiesChair.aspx

Key facts

  1. The ten human Gurus begin with Guru Nanak and end with Guru Gobind Singh.Sources: https://sgpc.net/ten-guru-sahibs/
  2. Guru Nanak founded Sikhism in Punjab in the late fifteenth century; nine successors followed him.Sources: https://digital.gndu.ac.in/SikhStudiesChair.aspx
  3. Guru Gobind Singh established the Khalsa in 1699.Sources: https://new.sgpc.net/ten-guru-sahibs/guru-gobind-singh-sahib/
  4. In 1708, Guru Gobind Singh passed Guruship to the Guru Granth Sahib, ending the line of personal Gurus.Sources: https://new.sgpc.net/ten-guru-sahibs/guru-gobind-singh-sahib/
  5. Sikhs regard the Guru Granth Sahib as the eternal Guru; Sikh teachings are enshrined in it.Sources: https://digital.gndu.ac.in/SikhStudiesChair.aspx
Source reference details
  • Ten Guru Sahibs – SGPC official , Amritsar — Shiromani Gurdwara Parbandhak Committee (SGPC)
    Defines the specific Sikh use of Guru and identifies the ten human Gurus, from Nanak to Gobind Singh.
  • Sri Guru Gobind Singh Ji – SGPC official , Amritsar — Shiromani Gurdwara Parbandhak Committee (SGPC)
    Describes the Khalsa’s establishment in 1699 and the transfer of Guruship to the Guru Granth Sahib in 1708.
  • Sikh Studies Chair — Guru Nanak Dev University
    Places Sikhism’s origin in late-fifteenth-century Punjab, notes nine successors to Guru Nanak, and identifies the Guru Granth Sahib as the eternal Guru containing Sikh teachings.

Ahimsa

Needs review

Original description: The religious and ethical principle of nonviolence toward living beings, especially central to Jainism.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-4dd8b84186ca45414e89 · Estimated cost: $0.0358

Overview

Ahimsa (also spelled ahinsa) means nonviolence or non-harming, and while recognized in several South Asian traditions, it is especially central in Jainism. Jain teaching links this ethic to the belief that living beings, including plants and animals, have souls; practitioners therefore seek to reduce harm rather than assume it can always be eliminated. Ahimsa is more than avoiding physical injury: Jain vows encompass thought, speech, and action, and prohibit causing or approving harm as well as doing it oneself. Its everyday expressions include vegetarian choices and careful movement to avoid injuring small creatures. It is the first of five vows that shape both monastic commitments and lay ethics.

Sources: https://pluralism.org/ahimsa-reverence-for-life, https://pluralism.org/an-ethic-for-living, https://cswr.hds.harvard.edu/news/2026/08/03/nonviolence-ahimsa, https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/jain-sculpture

Key facts

  1. Ahimsa means nonviolence and is also important in Hindu and Buddhist traditions; Jain teaching makes it a foundation of religious life.Sources: https://cswr.hds.harvard.edu/news/2026/08/03/nonviolence-ahimsa
  2. Jain teaching regards humans, animals, and plants as ensouled beings, extending the ethic of non-harm across forms of life.Sources: https://pluralism.org/ahimsa-reverence-for-life
  3. Ahimsa is the first of five Jain vows, alongside truthfulness, non-stealing, chastity, and non-possession.Sources: https://pluralism.org/an-ethic-for-living
  4. The vow concerns harm in thought, speech, and action, including causing harm or consenting to it.Sources: https://pluralism.org/an-ethic-for-living, https://cswr.hds.harvard.edu/news/2026/08/03/nonviolence-ahimsa
  5. Vegetarian choices and precautions against injuring insects are examples of ahimsa in daily life; Jain renouncers may take especially careful steps.Sources: https://pluralism.org/ahimsa-reverence-for-life
  6. The Metropolitan Museum describes the motionless standing meditation posture kayotsarga as an expression of ahimsa: stillness is intended to prevent harm even to tiny creatures.Sources: https://www.metmuseum.org/essays/jain-sculpture
Source reference details
  • Ahimsa: Reverence for Life — The Pluralism Project, Harvard University
    Jain belief that humans, animals, and plants have souls; ahimsa’s central role; vegetarian practice and precautions to avoid injuring insects.
  • An Ethic for Living — The Pluralism Project, Harvard University
    Ahimsa as the first of five vows; vows’ application through thought, deed, and speech; minimizing harm and differences in lay and monastic practice.
  • Nonviolence (Ahiṃsā) — Center for the Study of World Religions, Harvard Divinity School
    Meaning of ahimsa, its presence in Hindu, Jain, and Buddhist traditions, and the Jain vow’s reach into thought, speech, and action, including causing or consenting to harm.
  • Jain Sculpture — The Metropolitan Museum of Art
    Kayotsarga standing meditation and its connection to ahimsa and avoiding harm to even small creatures.

Gungnir

Needs review

Original description: Gungnir is Odin’s enchanted spear in Norse mythology, famed for never missing its target and associated with Odin’s ordeal on the World Tree.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-ec59ceaa558f436bb8c9 · Estimated cost: $0.0262

Overview

Gungnir is Odin’s named spear in Norse mythology. In Snorri Sturluson’s Prose Edda, dwarves called Ívaldi’s Sons make the spear, which becomes Odin’s possession. The Hávamál describes Odin hanging on a tree for nine nights, wounded by a spear, and gaining runes; that passage does not name either the spear or the tree. The Swedish History Museum identifies them as Gungnir and Yggdrasil in its account of Odin’s ordeal. Gungnir’s importance therefore lies not only in its connection to a god of war, but also in the mythic story of Odin’s self-sacrifice in pursuit of wisdom.

Sources: https://www2.hf.uio.no/polyglotta/index.php?mid=1545933&page=record, https://honors.libraries.psu.edu/files/final_submissions/7942, https://historiska.se/en/explore-history/history-hub/odin-leader-of-the-gods/

Key facts

  1. Gungnir is identified as Odin’s spear in Snorri Sturluson’s Prose Edda.Sources: https://www2.hf.uio.no/polyglotta/index.php?mid=1545933&page=record
  2. The Prose Edda says the dwarves known as Ívaldi’s Sons made Gungnir.Sources: https://www2.hf.uio.no/polyglotta/index.php?mid=1545933&page=record
  3. In the Hávamál, Odin recounts hanging for nine nights, spear-wounded, and acquiring runes.Sources: https://honors.libraries.psu.edu/files/final_submissions/7942
  4. The Hávamál passage describes a spear and a tree but does not name them Gungnir and Yggdrasil; the Swedish History Museum makes those identifications in its account.Sources: https://honors.libraries.psu.edu/files/final_submissions/7942, https://historiska.se/en/explore-history/history-hub/odin-leader-of-the-gods/
  5. A Penn State honors thesis cautions that much literary evidence about Viking society was written after the Viking Age and in a Christian context.Sources: https://honors.libraries.psu.edu/files/final_submissions/7942

Model-reported concerns

  • The Hávamál passage itself does not name Gungnir or Yggdrasil; the identification is supplied by the Swedish History Museum’s synthesis.

Science and Technology

Earth's mantle

Needs review

Original description: The solid rocky layer of Earth between the crust and the core, whose convection and internal structure influence tectonic activity and hotspot formation.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-03c6e9c536cf00c54544 · Estimated cost: $0.0135

Overview

Earth’s mantle is the mostly solid, silicate-rich layer between the thin crust and the core. About 2,900 kilometers thick, it makes up most of Earth’s interior. It is not a global ocean of molten rock: under the intense heat and pressure underground, solid mantle rock can deform and creep extremely slowly. Heat-driven movement within the mantle transfers energy toward the surface and helps drive plate tectonics, including seafloor spreading. In some hotspot models, heat rising through the mantle contributes to volcanic activity, though magma forms at shallower depths. Scientists continue to investigate the details of mantle circulation and the origins of hotspots.

Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/mantle/, https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/seafloor-spreading/, https://www.usgs.gov/news/volcano-watch-heat-deep-and-magma-shallow-a-hot-spot-system

Key facts

  1. The mantle lies between Earth’s crust and core; the Moho marks its upper boundary, and the core–mantle boundary is roughly 2,900 kilometers below the surface.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/mantle/
  2. Mantle rocks are mostly silicates, including minerals such as olivine, garnet, and pyroxene.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/mantle/
  3. The mantle is mostly solid, but its rock can deform and flow very slowly under deep-Earth conditions; the asthenosphere is a relatively weak, ductile region in the upper mantle.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/mantle/, https://www.usgs.gov/news/volcano-watch-heat-is-deep-and-magma-shallow-a-hot-spot-system
  4. Mantle convection transfers heat and material; it is associated with plate tectonics and processes such as seafloor spreading.Sources: https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/mantle/, https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/seafloor-spreading/
  5. A USGS explanation of the Hawaiian hotspot describes solid mantle rising as heat moves upward, with magma forming at shallower depths; the proposed origin of hotspots is a scientific model, not a settled explanation for every hotspot.Sources: https://www.usgs.gov/news/volcano-watch-heat-is-deep-and-magma-shallow-a-hot-spot-system, https://education.nationalgeographic.org/resource/mantle/

Model-reported concerns

  • The USGS hotspot explanation is an older educational account focused on Hawaii; it is used only for the stated distinction between deep heat transfer and shallower magma formation, not as a comprehensive current consensus on all hotspot origins.
Source reference details
  • Mantle — National Geographic Society
    Layer position, approximate thickness, silicate composition, mostly solid character, asthenosphere, convection, and scientific uncertainty about mantle circulation and plumes.
  • Seafloor Spreading — National Geographic Society
    Connection between mantle convection, plate tectonics, and seafloor spreading.
  • Volcano Watch — Heat is deep and magma is shallow in a hot-spot system — U.S. Geological Survey, Hawaiian Volcano Observatory
    Solid mantle material can convect slowly; the USGS account distinguishes deep heat transfer from shallower magma formation and presents hotspot origins as a theory.

Soybean

Needs review

Original description: A leguminous crop plant cultivated for its protein- and oil-rich seeds, which are used in foods such as tofu, edamame, and textured vegetable protein.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-91d17e935e87b9e5be09 · Estimated cost: $0.0239

Overview

Soybean (Glycine max), also called soya, is an annual plant in the legume family, cultivated for its protein- and oil-rich seeds. Its leaves usually have three leaflets, and its roots can form nodules with bacteria that convert nitrogen from the air into a form plants can use. People eat soybeans in many ways: young harvested beans are edamame, while processed beans are used for tofu, tempeh, soy drinks and other foods. Soybean oil is used in cooking and processed foods, and the protein-rich meal left after oil extraction is widely used in animal feed. Evidence suggests soybean was domesticated in China, though the precise history remains under study.

Sources: https://inspection.canada.ca/en/plant-varieties/plants-novel-traits/applicants/directive-94-08/biology-documents/glycine-max-merr, https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/pub/21-004-x/2017001/article/14779-eng.htm, https://extension.umn.edu/about/our-stories/news/source-magazine/from-field-to-tofu

Key facts

  1. Soybean is the cultivated species Glycine max in the Fabaceae, or legume, family; it is an annual crop.Sources: https://inspection.canada.ca/en/plant-varieties/plants-novel-traits/applicants/directive-94-08/biology-documents/glycine-max-merr
  2. Its foliage leaves are usually made up of three leaflets, and fine hairs can occur on the leaves, stems and pods.Sources: https://inspection.canada.ca/en/plant-varieties/plants-novel-traits/applicants/directive-94-08/biology-documents/glycine-max-merr
  3. Root nodules house bacteria that turn atmospheric nitrogen into a form available to plants; this helps explain why soybean is useful in crop rotations.Sources: https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/pub/21-004-x/2017001/article/14779-eng.htm
  4. Edamame is soybean harvested as a vegetable while the pods are still green; mature soybeans are harvested as dry seeds.Sources: https://extension.umn.edu/about/our-stories/news/source-magazine/from-field-to-tofu
  5. Soybeans are processed into foods including tofu, tempeh, soy sauce and soy drinks; soybean oil and protein-rich meal also have major food and feed uses.Sources: https://extension.umn.edu/about/our-stories/news/source-magazine/from-field-to-tofu, https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/pub/21-004-x/2017001/article/14779-eng.htm
Source reference details

    Scientific identity and family, annual growth, leaf and root-nodule traits, and evidence and uncertainty about domestication history.
  • Soy Story: A Short History of Glycine max in Canada — Statistics Canada
    Nitrogen conversion by root-nodule bacteria, crop-rotation relevance, edible oil uses and protein-rich soybean meal.
  • From field to tofu — University of Minnesota Extension
    Food-grade soybean products, including tofu, tempeh, edamame, soy sauce and soy drinks; distinguishes food-grade uses from common feed and oil uses.
