Malaria

Needs review

Original description: A mosquito-borne infectious disease caused by parasites of the genus Plasmodium and characterized by recurring fevers.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-420fcf21456769545c8f · Estimated cost: $0.0128

Overview

Malaria is a potentially life-threatening infection caused by Plasmodium parasites and usually transmitted when an infected female Anopheles mosquito bites a person. The parasite’s unusual life cycle passes between two hosts: after entering a human, it first develops in liver cells and then multiplies in red blood cells; another mosquito can pick it up while feeding. Fever, chills, and headache are common early symptoms, but illness can become severe, especially with P. falciparum. Five Plasmodium species are known to cause human malaria. Unlike colds or flu, malaria is not ordinarily spread directly from person to person, and prompt diagnosis and treatment can prevent severe outcomes.

Sources: https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/malaria, https://www.cdc.gov/dpdx/malaria/, https://cdc.gov/malaria/about/index.html

Key facts

  1. Only female Anopheles mosquitoes transmit human malaria, and only some Anopheles species carry the parasites.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/dpdx/malaria/
  2. In humans, the parasite develops first in liver cells, then multiplies in red blood cells; blood-stage parasites cause the illness’s symptoms.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/dpdx/malaria/
  3. Early symptoms commonly include fever, chills, and headache, often beginning about 10–15 days after an infectious bite.Sources: https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/malaria
  4. P. falciparum is the deadliest malaria parasite species; untreated infection can progress to severe illness and death.Sources: https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/malaria, https://www.cdc.gov/dpdx/malaria/
  5. Malaria can be diagnosed with a blood test and treated with prescription medicines; prompt care can help prevent severe illness.Sources: https://cdc.gov/malaria/about/index.html, https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/malaria
Source reference details

    Cause, mosquito transmission, five human-infecting Plasmodium species, common symptoms and timing, severity of P. falciparum, and diagnosis and treatment.
  • CDC - DPDx - Malaria — Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
    Two-host life cycle, liver and red-blood-cell stages, symptoms caused by blood-stage parasites, female Anopheles transmission, and severe disease.
  • About Malaria — Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
    Malaria is not ordinarily spread directly between people; blood testing and prescription treatment.

Refraction

Needs review

Original description: The change in direction of a wave as it passes between media in which it travels at different speeds, commonly illustrated by light bending between air and water.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-ea8faaa212cc3c1ea0a4 · Estimated cost: $0.0129

Overview

Refraction is the change in direction of a wave as it crosses between materials where it travels at different speeds; light is a familiar example. At an angled boundary, a speed change makes the ray turn, while the direction of bending depends on whether it slows or speeds up. For light, the refractive index n = c/v compares its speed in a vacuum with its speed in a material, and Snell’s law (n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂) predicts the angles, measured from the perpendicular (normal) to the boundary. Refraction explains why a pencil partly submerged in water can look bent and enables lenses in eyeglasses and telescopes to redirect light.

Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/16-2-refraction, https://science.nasa.gov/learn/basics-of-space-flight/chapter6-6/

Key facts

  1. The refractive index of a material is the speed of light in a vacuum divided by its speed in that material: n = c/v.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/16-2-refraction, https://science.nasa.gov/learn/basics-of-space-flight/chapter6-6/
  2. For light entering a slower medium at an angle, the ray bends toward the perpendicular; entering a faster medium bends it away.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/16-2-refraction
  3. Snell’s law, n₁ sin θ₁ = n₂ sin θ₂, relates the indices of refraction to the ray’s angles in each medium; those angles are measured from the perpendicular to the boundary.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/16-2-refraction, https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-3/pages/1-6-huygenss-principle
  4. Different refractive indices in air and water make a pencil partly submerged in water appear discontinuous or bent.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/learn/basics-of-space-flight/chapter6-6/, https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/16-2-refraction
  5. Refraction is used by lenses in eyeglasses and refracting telescopes to redirect light.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/learn/basics-of-space-flight/chapter6-6/
Source reference details
  • 16.2 Refraction - Physics — OpenStax, Rice University
    Defines refractive index and its relation to light speed; explains bending as light changes speed, the direction of bending, Snell’s law and angle convention, the bent-pencil observation, and optical applications.
  • Chapter 6: Electromagnetics — NASA Science
    Defines refraction and refractive index; describes the air-water pencil effect and uses of refraction in eyeglasses and telescope lenses.
  • 1.6 Huygens’s Principle - University Physics Volume 3 — OpenStax, Rice University
    Explains refraction through wave fronts changing direction when wave speed changes, and derives Snell’s law.

Rickets

Needs review

Original description: A childhood disorder of defective bone mineralization, commonly caused by vitamin D deficiency or related calcium and phosphate deficiencies, resulting in weak or bowed bones.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-b49f4aba0e445ae1a991 · Estimated cost: $0.0130

Overview

Rickets is a disorder of growing bones in which bone tissue fails to mineralize normally, leaving bones soft, weak, and sometimes deformed. It most commonly follows too little vitamin D, which helps the body absorb calcium, but too little calcium or phosphate can also cause it. Some cases are inherited or linked to conditions affecting mineral balance, so rickets is not always simply a dietary deficiency. Children may have bone pain, poor growth, or bowed legs and other skeletal changes. Rickets matters because disrupted mineralization during growth can alter a developing skeleton; treatment aims to correct the underlying nutrient shortage or other cause.

Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/rickets.html, https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000344.htm, https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/pediatrics/bone-disorders-in-children/introduction-to-metabolic-bone-disorders-in-children

Key facts

  1. Vitamin D helps the body absorb calcium; low calcium or phosphate can also contribute to rickets.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/rickets.html, https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000344.htm, https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND-Consumer/
  2. Possible signs include bone pain, impaired growth, bowed legs, and other skeletal changes.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/rickets.html, https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000344.htm
  3. Not all rickets is nutritional: inherited forms and conditions affecting mineral balance can also cause it.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/rickets.html, https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000344.htm, https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/pediatrics/bone-disorders-in-children/introduction-to-metabolic-bone-disorders-in-children
  4. Rickets affects children whose bones are still growing; vitamin D deficiency in teens and adults is associated instead with osteomalacia.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000344.htm, https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND-Consumer/
  5. Treatment is directed at correcting the cause, such as replacing deficient vitamin D, calcium, or phosphorus.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000344.htm, https://medlineplus.gov/rickets.html
Source reference details

  • Rickets: MedlinePlus Medical Encyclopedia — MedlinePlus, U.S. National Library of Medicine; health content provided by A.D.A.M., Inc.

Sickle Cell Disease

Needs review

Original description: An inherited disorder caused by abnormal hemoglobin that distorts red blood cells and impairs oxygen transport, while heterozygous carriers have increased resistance to malaria.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-59f265663165d5fea259 · Estimated cost: $0.0350

Overview

Sickle cell disease (SCD) is a group of inherited red-blood-cell disorders caused by gene variants that produce abnormal hemoglobin. Hemoglobin normally carries oxygen; in SCD, hemoglobin can make red cells rigid and sickle- or crescent-shaped. These cells may break down early and stick in small blood vessels, reducing blood flow and contributing to anemia, pain, and organ complications. SCD includes different inherited forms: HbSS and combinations of HbS with another abnormal hemoglobin can cause disease, with severity that varies. By contrast, sickle cell trait usually means one HbS gene and one HbA gene; it is not SCD. Trait is associated with partial protection against severe malaria caused by Plasmodium falciparum, but it does not guarantee immunity.

Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/sickle-cell/about/index.html, https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/sickle-cell-disease/causes, https://www.cdc.gov/MMWR/preview/mmwrhtml/mm5523a1.htm

Key facts

  1. SCD is inherited; a child receives genes for abnormal hemoglobin from their parents.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/sickle-cell/about/index.html, https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/sickle-cell-disease/causes
  2. SCD includes several forms, such as HbSS and HbSC; their severity can differ.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/sickle-cell/about/index.html
  3. Sickled red cells can break down early and block small blood vessels, contributing to anemia, pain, and other complications.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/sickle-cell/about/index.html, https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/sickle-cell-disease/causes
  4. Sickle cell trait usually means inheriting one HbS gene and one HbA gene; it is distinct from SCD.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/sickle-cell/about/index.html, https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/sickle-cell-disease/causes
  5. Sickle cell trait is associated with partial protection against severe malaria, but people with the trait can still get malaria.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/MMWR/preview/mmwrhtml/mm5523a1.htm, https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/sickle-cell-disease/causes
Source reference details
  • About Sickle Cell Disease — Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
    Defines SCD as a group of inherited red-cell disorders; describes abnormal hemoglobin, sickled cells, impaired blood flow, complications, major disease forms, and the distinction between SCD and trait.
  • Sickle Cell Disease Causes and Risk Factors — National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute, National Institutes of Health
    Explains inheritance, hemoglobin S, trait versus disease, sickled-cell effects, and the association between hemoglobin S and protection against serious malaria.
  • Malaria in Multiple Family Members — Chicago, Illinois, 2006 — Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report
    Reports that sickle cell trait provides a selective advantage through resistance to severe malaria, while documenting that carriers can still experience complicated malaria.

Schrödinger's cat thought experiment

Needs review

Original description: Erwin Schrödinger's thought experiment involving a cat treated as simultaneously alive and dead in a quantum superposition until observation.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-84157f7a98bfd3a97eb1 · Estimated cost: $0.0140

Overview

Schrödinger’s cat is a 1935 thought experiment by physicist Erwin Schrödinger, devised to expose a puzzle in applying quantum mechanics to everyday-sized things. In the imagined setup, a cat shares a sealed chamber with a radioactive atom, a Geiger counter, and a poison-release mechanism: a decay triggers the counter and releases poison, while no decay leaves the cat alive. Quantum theory assigns probabilities to the atom’s decay; when its uncertain outcome is linked to the cat, the mathematical description includes both alive and dead possibilities before a result is determined. Schrödinger used this deliberately unsettling case to question how quantum descriptions relate to observable reality. It is an illustration, not a real experiment or a claim that ordinary cats literally occupy both conditions.

Sources: https://www2.unicamp.br/~chibeni/textosdidaticos/schrodinger-1935-cat.pdf, https://www.nist.gov/quantum-information-science/quantum-computing-explained

Key facts

  1. In Schrödinger’s imagined mechanism, a radioactive decay sets off a Geiger counter and hammer that breaks a flask of poison; without a decay, the cat lives.Sources: https://www2.unicamp.br/~chibeni/textosdidaticos/schrodinger-1935-cat.pdf
  2. The thought experiment links an uncertain event at the atomic scale to two possible outcomes for a much larger system: the cat alive or dead.Sources: https://www2.unicamp.br/~chibeni/textosdidaticos/schrodinger-1935-cat.pdf, https://www.nist.gov/quantum-information-science/quantum-computing-explained
  3. Schrödinger described the example as a deliberately ‘ridiculous’ case to highlight concerns about extending quantum descriptions to visible, everyday objects.Sources: https://www2.unicamp.br/~chibeni/textosdidaticos/schrodinger-1935-cat.pdf
  4. The example raises questions about measurement: a detector can record the atomic event, while the interpretation of what happens to the combined system is part of the puzzle.Sources: https://www.open.edu/openlearn/mod/oucontent/view.php?id=135655&section=3.3, https://www2.unicamp.br/~chibeni/textosdidaticos/schrodinger-1935-cat.pdf
  5. The cat is imaginary; the thought experiment is used to explain quantum ideas, not to suggest that real cats are literally both alive and dead.Sources: https://www.nist.gov/quantum-information-science/quantum-computing-explained
Source reference details
  • The Present Situation in Quantum Mechanics: A Translation of Schrödinger's "Cat Paradox" Paper — Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society (hosted by the University of Campinas)
    Trimmer’s translation of Schrödinger’s 1935 paper supports the date, the cat-and-radioactive-source setup, its alive/dead description, and Schrödinger’s stated concern about extending quantum descriptions to macroscopic objects.
  • Quantum Computing Explained — National Institute of Standards and Technology
    Explains superposition in accessible terms, summarizes the cat-and-poison-trap scenario, and clarifies that the imaginary cat is a metaphor rather than a real cat being both alive and dead.
  • 3.3 Schrödinger’s cat — OpenLearn, The Open University
    Explains the radioactive-decay setup, superposition, and how the thought experiment raises questions about measurement and the connection between microscopic events and macroscopic records.

Osteoporosis

Needs review

Original description: A disease characterized by reduced bone density and weakened, brittle bones, increasing the risk of fractures.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-1ca2e81f477b9c66bf5f · Estimated cost: $0.0130

Overview

Osteoporosis is a disease in which bone mineral density or mass falls, or bone structure changes, making bones weaker and more likely to fracture. Bone is living tissue that is continually renewed; when breakdown outpaces replacement, its inner honeycomb-like structure thins and the spaces widen. Often there are no warning symptoms until a break occurs, sometimes after a minor fall or even a cough; hip, spine, and wrist are frequent fracture sites. It can affect people of any sex or background, but risk rises with age and is higher in women after menopause. A low-dose DXA/DEXA scan measures bone minerals and helps identify osteoporosis and estimate fracture risk; osteopenia is low bone density less severe than osteoporosis.

Sources: https://www.niams.nih.gov/health-topics/osteoporosis, https://www.nia.nih.gov/health/osteoporosis/osteoporosis, https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bone-density-scan/

Key facts

  1. When bone breakdown exceeds replacement, the inner bone structure thins, its spaces grow, and the outer shell becomes thinner—weakening the bone.Sources: https://www.nia.nih.gov/health/osteoporosis/osteoporosis
  2. Osteoporosis is often symptomless until a fracture; in weakened bones, a minor fall or ordinary stress such as coughing can sometimes cause one.Sources: https://www.niams.nih.gov/health-topics/osteoporosis, https://www.nia.nih.gov/health/osteoporosis/osteoporosis
  3. Fractures commonly affect the hip, spine, or wrist. Spinal fractures may cause severe back pain, height loss, or a hunched posture.Sources: https://www.nia.nih.gov/health/osteoporosis/osteoporosis, https://www.niams.nih.gov/health-topics/osteoporosis
  4. Risk rises with age; other risk factors include low estrogen after menopause, family history, too little calcium or vitamin D, inactivity, and long-term use of some medicines.Sources: https://www.niams.nih.gov/health-topics/osteoporosis, https://www.nia.nih.gov/health/osteoporosis/osteoporosis
  5. A DXA/DEXA scan uses low-dose X-rays to measure bone minerals. Results can help diagnose osteoporosis and assess future fracture risk.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bone-density-scan/
Source reference details

    Definition involving decreased bone mineral density or mass and altered bone structure; fracture risk, symptoms, common fracture effects, and risk factors.
  • Osteoporosis — National Institute on Aging (NIA), National Institutes of Health
    Bone renewal and honeycomb-like structure, silent progression, common fracture sites, age and menopause-related risk, and osteopenia.
  • Bone Density Scan — MedlinePlus, National Library of Medicine
    DXA/DEXA scan method, low-dose X-rays, bone mineral measurement, and use of results to diagnose low bone density and estimate fracture risk.

Ohm's law

Needs review

Original description: The electrical law relating voltage, current, and resistance, commonly expressed as V = IR.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-22e9cdea36934c5fd18a · Estimated cost: $0.0236

Overview

Ohm’s law relates the voltage drop across an electrical component, the current through it, and its resistance: V = IR. It is useful for calculating any one of these quantities when the other two are known, helping describe and analyze simple circuits. The relationship reflects experiments by German physicist Georg Simon Ohm in the 19th century. It is an empirical rule, not a pattern every material or component follows: when a component’s current is proportional to voltage over a range of conditions, it is called ohmic. For an ohmic component, doubling voltage while resistance stays constant doubles current.

Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/19-1-ohms-law

Key facts

  1. In V = IR, V is voltage, I is current, and R is resistance; the same relationship can be rearranged to find any one of them.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/19-1-ohms-law
  2. Resistance is measured in ohms (Ω); one ohm equals one volt per ampere (1 Ω = 1 V/A).Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/19-1-ohms-law, https://physics.nist.gov/cuu/pdf/sp330.pdf
  3. Ohm’s law describes an experimentally observed relationship; it does not apply to every material or component in all conditions.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/19-1-ohms-law
  4. A material or component that follows the proportional voltage-current relationship is called ohmic.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/19-1-ohms-law
  5. Georg Simon Ohm, a German physicist, experimentally established the relationship in the 19th century.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/physics/pages/19-1-ohms-law
Source reference details
  • 19.1 Ohm's law - Physics — OpenStax
    Equation and variable meanings, Ohm’s experimental work, the empirical nature and limits of the relationship, ohmic materials, and the ohm as V/A.
  • The International System of Units (SI), NIST Special Publication 330, 2001 Edition — National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST), U.S. Department of Commerce
    SI definitions relating the volt, ampere, and ohm.

Archaea

Needs review

Original description: Archaea are a distinct domain of single-celled prokaryotic organisms, including methanogens, separated from Bacteria by differences in evolutionary history and cellular chemistry.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-88b747db1ab853364883 · Estimated cost: $0.0248

Overview

Archaea are a domain of single-celled prokaryotes—organisms whose cells lack a nucleus—and one of three major branches of life, alongside Bacteria and Eukarya. Though they can look like bacteria, they are distinct: their cell membranes have ether-linked, branched lipids, and their cell walls lack bacterial peptidoglycan. Comparisons of ribosomal RNA helped Carl Woese and colleagues identify Archaea as a separate evolutionary lineage, changing how scientists organized the tree of life. Archaea live in ordinary as well as extreme environments. Some, called methanogens, make methane as they obtain energy in oxygen-free conditions, including in some animal digestive systems.

Sources: https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/1-3-types-of-microorganisms, https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/4-6-archaea, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10965645/

Key facts

  1. Archaea are unicellular prokaryotes, meaning their cells lack a nucleus; they are classified separately from Bacteria and Eukarya.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/1-3-types-of-microorganisms
  2. Their membranes contain ether-linked branched lipids, and their cell walls lack peptidoglycan, a key component of bacterial cell walls.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/4-6-archaea
  3. Ribosomal RNA comparisons helped Carl Woese and colleagues distinguish Archaea as a separate evolutionary lineage.Sources: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10965645/
  4. Archaea occur in many habitats; extreme heat, cold, or salinity is characteristic of some, not all, members of the domain.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/1-3-types-of-microorganisms, https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/4-6-archaea
  5. Methanogens are archaea that produce methane in oxygen-free environments; some live in the digestive systems of humans and other animals.Sources: https://openstax.org/books/microbiology/pages/4-6-archaea, https://astrobiology.nasa.gov/nai/annual-reports/2014/uiuc/project-6-mining-archaeal-genomes-for-signatures-of-early-life-comparison-of-metabolic-genes-in-methanogens/index.html

    Archaea are unicellular prokaryotes distinct from Bacteria, differ in evolutionary history and cell chemistry, and occur in diverse habitats.

    Archaeal membrane and cell-wall distinctions, broad habitat range, and methanogens’ methane production and presence in animal digestive systems.
  • The discovery of archaea: from observed anomaly to consequential restructuring of the phylogenetic tree — History and Philosophy of the Life Sciences; Springer Nature, hosted by PubMed Central
    Historical account of Woese’s ribosomal-RNA research and the recognition of Archaea as a distinct domain.

Hydrochloric acid

Needs review

Original description: A strong inorganic acid consisting of hydrogen chloride dissolved in water, found in gastric juice and widely used in laboratory, industrial, and chemical-processing applications.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-269961a52d6e0e302e70 · Estimated cost: $0.0363

Overview

Hydrochloric acid (HCl) is an aqueous solution of hydrogen chloride, a gas; it is an inorganic, strong acid. In water, HCl dissociates essentially completely into hydronium and chloride ions, which explains its “strong acid” classification—strength refers to ionization, not simply how concentrated a sample is. In the body, gastric glands secrete hydrochloric acid as part of stomach acid, which helps digestion alongside enzymes. Beyond biology, it is an important industrial chemical: uses include cleaning oxide from steel (“pickling”), processing ores, producing other chemicals, and adjusting water pH. Hydrochloric acid is corrosive, so laboratory and industrial use requires appropriate controls.

Sources: https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPURL.cgi?Dockey=P1017409.txt, https://chem.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/General_Chemistry/Chem1_(Lower)/13:_Acid-Base_Equilibria/13.07:_Exact_Calculations_and_Approximations, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/mesh/68010295, https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/digestive-system-how-it-works

Key facts

  1. Hydrochloric acid is hydrogen chloride dissolved in water; hydrogen chloride itself is a gas.Sources: https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPURL.cgi?Dockey=P1017409.txt
  2. In aqueous solution, HCl is a strong acid because it dissociates essentially completely, forming hydronium and chloride ions.Sources: https://chem.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/General_Chemistry/Chem1_(Lower)/13:_Acid-Base_Equilibria/13.07:_Exact_Calculations_and_Approximations
  3. Gastric parietal cells secrete hydrochloric acid; stomach acid and enzymes help break down food, including proteins.Sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/mesh/68010295, https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/digestive-system-how-it-works
  4. Industrial uses include steel pickling, ore processing, chemical production, and pH adjustment in water treatment.Sources: https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPURL.cgi?Dockey=P1017409.txt
  5. Hydrochloric acid is corrosive and can damage tissues, so it must be handled with appropriate safety precautions.Sources: https://nepis.epa.gov/Exe/ZyPURL.cgi?Dockey=P1017409.txt
Source reference details

    Defines hydrochloric acid as a strong inorganic acid; distinguishes hydrogen chloride gas from its aqueous solution; documents industrial applications and corrosive hazards.

    Explains strong-acid dissociation in water and the ions present in hydrochloric acid solutions.

Halley's Comet

Needs review

Original description: A short-period comet that returns to visibility from Earth approximately every 75–76 years and was depicted in the Bayeux Tapestry.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-049b71b1ea7199dfed50 · Estimated cost: $0.0239

Overview

1P/Halley, commonly called Halley’s Comet, is an icy, dusty object that orbits the Sun on a long, stretched path. As it nears the Sun, warming releases gas and dust that form a hazy cloud and tails. Its average orbit takes about 76 years, though the interval varies. English astronomer Edmond Halley compared observations from 1531, 1607, and 1682 and argued in 1705 that they showed one returning comet; he correctly predicted its 1758 return. The comet appeared in 1066 and is depicted on the Bayeux Tapestry, which tells the story of the Norman conquest of England. In 1986, ESA’s Giotto spacecraft captured the first close-up images of a comet nucleus—Halley’s.

Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/comets/1p-halley/, https://spaceplace.nasa.gov/comets/en/, https://www.bayeuxmuseum.com/en/the-bayeux-tapestry/discover-the-bayeux-tapestry/the_battle_of_hastings/, https://www.esa.int/Science_Exploration/Space_Science/Giotto_overview

Key facts

  1. NASA classifies Halley as a short-period comet: its orbit takes less than 200 years.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/comets/1p-halley/
  2. Its orbital period averages about 76 years, but NASA reports past intervals ranging from 74.42 to 79.25 years.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/comets/1p-halley/
  3. Halley’s 1705 prediction was based on similarities among comet observations from 1531, 1607, and 1682; the comet returned in 1758.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/comets/1p-halley/
  4. Dust and rocky material shed by Halley contributes to the Eta Aquariid and Orionid meteor showers.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/solar-system/comets/1p-halley/
  5. During its 1986 encounter, ESA’s Giotto mission obtained the first close-up images of a comet nucleus and revealed Halley’s shape and active jets.Sources: https://www.esa.int/Science_Exploration/Space_Science/Giotto_overview
Source reference details
  • 1P/Halley — NASA Science
    Halley’s orbit and its variation, Halley’s historical identification and predicted return, its depiction on the Bayeux Tapestry, and its debris’ connection to two meteor showers.
  • What Is a Comet? — NASA Space Place
    Comet composition and the formation of a coma and tails near the Sun; identifies Halley’s orbit as about 76 years.
  • The Battle of Hastings — Bayeux Museum
    The Bayeux Tapestry’s account of the 1066 Battle of Hastings and the Norman conquest of England.
  • Giotto overview — European Space Agency
    Giotto’s 1986 encounter with Halley and its first images of a comet nucleus.

Triton (moon)

Needs review

Original description: Triton is Neptune’s largest natural satellite, a retrograde moon likely captured from the Kuiper Belt and known for its distinctive cantaloupe terrain.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-02876526eb40fe673d8a · Estimated cost: $0.0260

Overview

Triton is Neptune’s largest moon, discovered by British astronomer William Lassell in 1846. Its defining oddity is a retrograde orbit: it circles Neptune opposite the planet’s rotation, unlike other large moons in the solar system. This unusual path, together with similarities to Pluto, supports the idea that Triton formed in the Kuiper Belt and was later captured by Neptune; capture is an inference, not something observed directly. Voyager 2’s 1989 flyby revealed an icy, nitrogen-rich world with active geysers and striking “cantaloupe terrain,” patterned with mounds and pits. Scientists think overturning ice in the crust may have produced the terrain, but its precise origin remains uncertain.

Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/neptune/moons/triton/, https://www.jpl.nasa.gov/images/pia12186-tritons-cantaloupe-terrain/, https://academic.oup.com/mnras/article/460/2/1542/2608991

Key facts

  1. William Lassell discovered Triton on October 10, 1846, just 17 days after Neptune was discovered.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/neptune/moons/triton/
  2. Triton has a retrograde orbit, moving around Neptune opposite the direction of the planet’s rotation.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/neptune/moons/triton/
  3. Its unusual orbit supports the hypothesis that Triton was captured by Neptune from the Kuiper Belt; the exact origin scenario remains an inference.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/neptune/moons/triton/, https://academic.oup.com/mnras/article/460/2/1542/2608991
  4. Voyager 2 found active geysers on Triton during its 1989 flyby; its thin atmosphere is mostly nitrogen.Sources: https://science.nasa.gov/neptune/moons/triton/
  5. The unique cantaloupe terrain has irregular mounds; crustal overturn and rising blobs of ice are a proposed explanation for its formation.Sources: https://www.jpl.nasa.gov/images/pia12186-tritons-cantaloupe-terrain/
Source reference details
  • Triton — NASA Science
    Identity as Neptune’s largest moon; discovery date and discoverer; retrograde orbit; likely Kuiper Belt capture; nitrogen-rich surface and atmosphere; Voyager 2 flyby and geysers.
  • Triton's "Cantaloupe" Terrain — NASA Jet Propulsion Laboratory
    Voyager-derived view of the cantaloupe terrain; irregular mounds; proposed crustal overturn and rising ice diapirs, described as a likely rather than certain explanation.
  • Interpreting the densities of the Kuiper belt's dwarf planets — Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society (Oxford Academic)
    Scientific context for treating Triton as a likely captured Kuiper Belt object, while describing capture as a hypothesis.

Red blood cells

Needs review

Original description: Oxygen-carrying biconcave blood cells whose hemoglobin transports oxygen through the circulatory system.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-6715e91ea12073df84d9 · Estimated cost: $0.0247

Overview

Red blood cells are specialized cells in the blood whose main job is to deliver oxygen from the lungs to tissues throughout the body. They also help carry carbon dioxide back to the lungs to be breathed out. Inside each cell, hemoglobin binds oxygen; its iron-containing heme helps give blood its red color. Red blood cells have a distinctive disk shape with a shallow indentation in the center. Bone marrow makes new blood cells to replace old ones, and a red blood cell typically lives for about 120 days before the spleen removes it. Their oxygen-delivery role makes them essential to the body’s tissues.

Sources: https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/sites/default/files/publications/11-7629.pdf, https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/gene/hbb/, https://medlineplus.gov/anemia.html

Key facts

  1. Red blood cells carry oxygen from the lungs to the body’s tissues.Sources: https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/sites/default/files/publications/11-7629.pdf, https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/gene/hbb/
  2. Hemoglobin inside red blood cells binds oxygen; its heme contains iron.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/gene/hbb/
  3. Their disk shape has a shallow indentation in the center.Sources: https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/sites/default/files/publications/11-7629.pdf
  4. Hemoglobin helps give blood its red color.Sources: https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/sites/default/files/publications/11-7629.pdf, https://medlineplus.gov/anemia.html
  5. Red blood cells live about 120 days; the spleen removes them at the end of their lifespan, while bone marrow makes replacements.Sources: https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/sites/default/files/publications/11-7629.pdf
Source reference details
  • Your Guide to Anemia — National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute (NHLBI), National Institutes of Health
    Describes red blood cells’ shape, oxygen and carbon dioxide transport, hemoglobin’s contribution to blood color, bone-marrow production, approximate lifespan, and spleen removal.
  • HBB gene: Normal Function — MedlinePlus Genetics, U.S. National Library of Medicine
    Explains that hemoglobin is inside red blood cells, that heme contains iron, and that hemoglobin binds oxygen in the lungs for delivery to tissues.
  • Anemia — MedlinePlus, U.S. National Library of Medicine
    Confirms hemoglobin is an iron-rich protein that gives blood its red color and carries oxygen from the lungs to the body.

Ammonia

Needs review

Original description: Ammonia is the nitrogen-hydrogen compound NH3, widely used in fertilizers, chemical manufacturing, cleaning products, and industrial applications.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-824712e6dfc4b3ec7631 · Estimated cost: $0.0249

Overview

Ammonia (NH₃) is a compound of nitrogen and hydrogen, normally a colorless gas with a sharp, pungent odor. In water it acts as a weak base, accepting a hydrogen ion from water to form ammonium and hydroxide ions. This behavior helps make aqueous ammonia useful in cleaning products. Its largest industrial role is supplying nitrogen for fertilizers, supporting plant growth. Manufacturers synthesize it by reacting nitrogen and hydrogen at elevated temperature and pressure with an iron catalyst in the Haber–Bosch process. Ammonia is also used to make nitric acid, refrigerants, and other chemicals; if released or mismanaged, it can harm aquatic ecosystems.

Sources: https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2023-03/Anhydrous%20Ammonia%20Supply%20Chain%20Profile.pdf, https://www.epa.gov/caddis/ammonia, https://chem.libretexts.org/Courses/Portland_Community_College/CH105:_Allied_Health_Chemistry_II/05:_Acids_and_Bases/5.05:_Weak_Bases, https://www.acs.org/green-chemistry-sustainability/education/teaching-modules/ideal-gases.html

Key facts

  1. Ammonia’s formula is NH₃: each molecule contains one nitrogen atom and three hydrogen atoms.Sources: https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2023-03/Anhydrous%20Ammonia%20Supply%20Chain%20Profile.pdf
  2. In water, ammonia is a weak base: only some molecules react to form ammonium and hydroxide ions.Sources: https://chem.libretexts.org/Courses/Portland_Community_College/CH105:_Allied_Health_Chemistry_II/05:_Acids_and_Bases/5.05:_Weak_Bases
  3. The Haber–Bosch reaction combines nitrogen and hydrogen with an iron catalyst under elevated temperature and pressure: N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃.Sources: https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2023-03/Anhydrous%20Ammonia%20Supply%20Chain%20Profile.pdf
  4. Ammonia is used in fertilizers and in making products including nitric acid, household cleaners, and refrigerants.Sources: https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2023-03/Anhydrous%20Ammonia%20Supply%20Chain%20Profile.pdf
  5. Ammonia entering surface waters can act as a nutrient, but high concentrations can harm aquatic life.Sources: https://www.epa.gov/caddis/ammonia, https://www.acs.org/green-chemistry-sustainability/education/teaching-modules/ideal-gases.html

Ludwig Boltzmann

Needs review

Original description: Austrian physicist and pioneer of statistical mechanics whose work connected entropy with microstates and whose namesakes include the Boltzmann constant and Maxwell–Boltzmann distribution.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-36950009faf44f030d15 · Estimated cost: $0.0135

Overview

Ludwig Eduard Boltzmann (1844–1906) was an Austrian physicist and a pioneer of atomic theory and statistical mechanics. He helped explain thermodynamic behavior by applying probability to the motions and possible arrangements of molecules. In his 1877 work, he connected the likelihood of a system’s overall state with how many microscopic arrangements could produce it; equilibrium corresponds to a highly probable state. The relation commonly shown as S = k ln W is engraved on his tombstone, but Boltzmann did not write that exact formula himself. His work on gases, entropy, and irreversibility helped shape the statistical understanding of physical systems.

Sources: https://geschichte.univie.ac.at/en/persons/ludwig-boltzmann, https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/win2021/entries/statphys-Boltzmann/, https://physik.uni-graz.at/en/personalities/historical-figures/

Key facts

  1. Boltzmann was born in Vienna and lived from 1844 to 1906.Sources: https://geschichte.univie.ac.at/en/persons/ludwig-boltzmann
  2. The University of Graz describes his work there as important to statistical heat theory and the interpretation of entropy and irreversibility, including the H-theorem.Sources: https://physik.uni-graz.at/en/personalities/historical-figures/
  3. In his 1877 combinatorial argument, Boltzmann treated equilibrium as the most probable overall state, related to the number of microscopic arrangements that can produce it.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/win2021/entries/statphys-Boltzmann/
  4. The formula S = k log W is engraved on Boltzmann’s tombstone, but the Stanford Encyclopedia notes that he never wrote that exact formula down.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/archives/win2021/entries/statphys-Boltzmann/
  5. Boltzmann held professorships at the University of Graz twice, from 1869–1873 and 1876–1890.Sources: https://physik.uni-graz.at/en/personalities/historical-figures/
Source reference details
  • Boltzmann’s Work in Statistical Physics — Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy
    Boltzmann’s statistical explanation of the second law; the 1877 combinatorial argument relating macrostate probability to microscopic arrangements; the tombstone formula and the qualification that he did not write that exact formula.
  • Ludwig Boltzmann, Univ.-Prof. Dr. — University of Vienna
    Boltzmann’s dates and Vienna birthplace; identifies him as a pioneer of atomic theory and founder of statistical mechanics.
  • Historical figures — University of Graz, Institute of Physics
    Boltzmann’s Graz professorship dates and contributions to statistical heat theory, entropy, irreversibility, and the H-theorem.

Boeing

Needs review

Original description: An American aerospace corporation that manufactures commercial aircraft and develops spacecraft and related aerospace systems.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-7b8c44120ea7b5682463 · Estimated cost: $0.0351

Overview

Boeing (The Boeing Company) is a U.S. aerospace company whose roots trace to William Boeing’s Seattle airplane business, incorporated as Pacific Aero Products in 1916. Today, it works across commercial aviation, defense, space systems, and support services, so its work ranges from passenger jets and freighters to military platforms and space technology. Familiar commercial aircraft include the 737, 777, and 787. Boeing also developed the CST-100 Starliner, a crew spacecraft for NASA’s Commercial Crew Program, designed to transport people and cargo to low Earth orbit and the International Space Station. This combination of airline-scale aircraft, defense systems, and spacecraft distinguishes Boeing from a company focused only on passenger planes.

Cherenkov radiation

Needs review

Original description: Light emitted when a charged particle travels through a material faster than light can travel through that material.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-abd0b565119720da935d · Estimated cost: $0.0250

Overview

Cherenkov radiation is electromagnetic light produced when a charged particle moves through a transparent material faster than light’s phase speed in that material—not faster than light in a vacuum. The particle disturbs the material, and the resulting light waves form a forward-facing cone, somewhat like a sonic boom’s shock front. In water around a reactor, this can appear as a blue or violet glow. Because the cone’s angle depends on the particle’s speed, detectors can use the light to help measure speed and identify particles. Pavel Cherenkov observed the effect in 1934; Ilya Frank and Igor Tamm later explained it theoretically, and all three shared the 1958 Nobel Prize in Physics.

Sources: https://www.energy.gov/ne/articles/cherenkov-radiation-explained, https://physics.aps.org/articles/v2/91, https://home.cern/how-detector-works/

Key facts

  1. The effect occurs when a charged particle travels faster than light does in a particular transparent material; it does not mean the particle exceeds light’s speed in a vacuum.Sources: https://www.energy.gov/ne/articles/cherenkov-radiation-explained, https://www.fnal.gov/pub/science/inquiring/matter/smallest/detection.html
  2. The emitted radiation forms a cone, and its angle depends on the particle’s speed.Sources: https://physics.aps.org/articles/v2/91, https://home.cern/how-detector-works/
  3. The glow around a reactor can look blue or violet; the exact visible appearance is not the definition of the effect.Sources: https://www.energy.gov/ne/articles/cherenkov-radiation-explained, https://physics.aps.org/articles/v2/91
  4. Cherenkov detectors use the emitted light to help measure particle speed and identify particles.Sources: https://home.cern/how-detector-works/, https://www.fnal.gov/pub/science/inquiring/matter/smallest/detection.html
  5. Pavel Cherenkov observed the effect in 1934; Ilya Frank and Igor Tamm later explained it, and the three received the 1958 Nobel Prize in Physics for their work.Sources: https://www.energy.gov/ne/articles/cherenkov-radiation-explained
Source reference details
  • Cherenkov Radiation, Explained — U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Nuclear Energy
    Supports the basic explanation, examples of charged particles and transparent media, blue-violet appearance, and the discovery and Nobel history.
  • Flipping a photonic shock wave — American Physical Society
    Supports the condition involving phase velocity, forward cone in ordinary media, cone angle’s relation to particle speed, and reactor glow.
  • How a detector works — CERN
    Supports the angle’s dependence on velocity and the use of Cherenkov radiation among methods for measuring velocity and identifying particles.
  • How to find the smallest particles - Particle Detectors — Fermi National Accelerator Laboratory
    Supports the distinction between light speed in a vacuum and in a material, and the use of Cherenkov detectors and light-sensitive photomultipliers.

Down syndrome

Needs review

Original description: A genetic condition usually caused by an extra copy of chromosome 21.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-c528005a01c7d84c2d6b · Estimated cost: $0.0130

Overview

Down syndrome (also called trisomy 21) is a genetic condition associated with extra genetic material from chromosome 21, one of the chromosomes that carries genes in the body’s cells. That extra material affects brain and body development, so people may have differences in learning and development and a higher chance of some health conditions; the effects vary from person to person, and many live healthy lives with supportive care. Most often, all cells have three separate copies of chromosome 21. Less commonly, the extra material is attached to another chromosome (translocation), or only some cells have it (mosaic trisomy 21). Most cases begin as a random cell-division event, not an action or fault of parents.

Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/birth-defects/about/down-syndrome.html, https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/condition/down-syndrome/, https://www.nichd.nih.gov/health/topics/down/conditioninfo/causes

Key facts

  1. Trisomy 21 is the pattern in which each cell has three separate copies of chromosome 21; it is the most common form of Down syndrome.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/birth-defects/about/down-syndrome.html
  2. In mosaic Down syndrome, some cells have three copies of chromosome 21 while others have the usual two.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/birth-defects/about/down-syndrome.html, https://www.nichd.nih.gov/health/topics/down/conditioninfo/causes
  3. In translocation Down syndrome, extra chromosome 21 material is attached to another chromosome; in some cases, a parent without Down syndrome can carry a chromosome rearrangement that is passed on.Sources: https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/condition/down-syndrome/, https://www.nichd.nih.gov/health/topics/down/conditioninfo/causes
  4. Most cases result from a random cell-division error called nondisjunction. NICHD says no parental behavior or environmental factor is known to cause Down syndrome.Sources: https://www.nichd.nih.gov/health/topics/down/conditioninfo/causes
  5. Possible associated health conditions include congenital heart defects, hearing loss, and obstructive sleep apnea, but individuals’ traits and health needs vary.Sources: https://www.cdc.gov/birth-defects/about/down-syndrome.html, https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/condition/down-syndrome/
Source reference details
  • Down Syndrome — Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
    Definition, developmental effects, common health conditions, the three forms, and the statement that many people live healthy lives with supportive care.
  • Down syndrome: MedlinePlus Genetics — National Library of Medicine, MedlinePlus Genetics
    Chromosome 21 patterns, translocation and mosaic forms, individual variation, and inheritance information.
  • What causes Down syndrome? — Eunice Kennedy Shriver National Institute of Child Health and Human Development
    Nondisjunction, random cell-division errors, the three chromosomal patterns, and the note that no parental behavior or environmental factor is known to cause the condition.

Apple Inc.

Needs review

Original description: A technology company founded by Steve Jobs, Steve Wozniak, and Ronald Wayne that develops consumer electronics, software, and services including the Macintosh and iPhone.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-bdcbae8ebd1f9d520815 · Estimated cost: $0.0134

Overview

Apple Inc. is a technology company that began as Apple Computer Company, a partnership formed by Steve Jobs, Steve Wozniak, and Ronald Wayne on April 1, 1976; it became incorporated as Apple Computer, Inc. in 1977. Its early Apple II reached beyond computer hobbyists, and the spreadsheet program VisiCalc helped boost that model’s sales. In 1984, Apple introduced the Macintosh, a computer that helped make graphical, mouse-driven personal computing familiar to a wider public. Its later iPhone combined phone, music-player, and internet functions in one touch-controlled product. Apple now sells devices including Mac and iPhone and offers services such as the App Store, iCloud, and Apple Music, linking hardware, software, and online services.

  5. Steve Jobs introduced the iPhone in 2007 as a touch-controlled combination of an iPod, a phone, and an internet communications device.Sources: https://www.apple.com/newsroom/2017/01/iphone-at-ten-the-revolution-continues/

Inertial confinement fusion

Needs review

Original description: A fusion approach that compresses a fuel capsule or pellet so that inertia confines the fuel long enough for thermonuclear fusion to occur.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-6f93028457788c8d72e0 · Estimated cost: $0.0135

Overview

Inertial confinement fusion (ICF) is a method of initiating fusion by rapidly compressing and heating a tiny fuel target. Instead of using magnetic fields to hold plasma, it relies on the imploding fuel’s inertia to keep particles close enough for reactions before the fuel flies apart. In common laser-driven indirect-drive experiments, lasers heat a surrounding hollow metal cylinder, generating X-rays that strip material from the capsule’s surface and drive it inward; direct-drive designs illuminate the capsule directly. ICF matters for studying matter under extreme conditions and nuclear processes, and is investigated as a possible energy source. But producing fusion in experiments is not yet equivalent to a practical power plant.

Sources: https://www.energy.gov/science/doe-explainsplasma-confinement, https://lasers.llnl.gov/education/glossary, https://www.energy.gov/topics/fusion-energy

Key facts

  1. A common fuel is deuterium and tritium, two hydrogen isotopes; their fusion produces a helium nucleus and a neutron.Sources: https://lasers.llnl.gov/education/glossary
  2. In indirect drive, a hohlraum converts laser energy into X-rays that compress the fuel capsule; direct drive focuses laser light on the capsule itself.Sources: https://lasers.llnl.gov/education/glossary
  3. Inertial confinement works on an extremely brief timescale: DOE describes fuel compression to high densities for billionths of a second.Sources: https://www.energy.gov/science/doe-explainsplasma-confinement
  4. Fusion ignition in a laboratory is distinct from commercial power generation; developing a practical fusion energy system still involves scientific and technological challenges.Sources: https://www.energy.gov/topics/fusion-energy, https://lasers.llnl.gov/education/glossary
  5. ICF research serves nuclear-stockpile stewardship as well as research into fusion’s potential role in energy production.Sources: https://www.energy.gov/science/doe-explainsplasma-confinement, https://www.energy.gov/topics/fusion-energy
Source reference details
  • DOE Explains...Plasma Confinement — U.S. Department of Energy
    Defines inertial confinement as compression by imploding matter, contrasts it with magnetic confinement, and describes its brief timescale and research roles.
  • Glossary — National Ignition Facility & Photon Science, Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory
    Defines ICF, direct and indirect drive, hohlraum, fuel capsule, D-T fuel, and ignition.
  • Fusion Energy — U.S. Department of Energy
    Explains the experimental implosion sequence at NIF, ICF’s stockpile-stewardship role, and the remaining challenges to practical commercial fusion energy.

Apoptosis

Needs review

Original description: Apoptosis is a regulated form of programmed cell death involving cellular self-destruction, caspase activity, and characteristic cellular fragmentation.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-b05fd1b6d8003fb7b787 · Estimated cost: $0.0237

Overview

Apoptosis (uh-POP-toh-sis) is a regulated form of cell death—often called programmed cell death—that helps a multicellular organism remove unneeded, damaged, or abnormal cells. It is important in development and tissue maintenance: for example, it helps remove tissue between developing digits. Caspases, enzymes activated in a controlled chain reaction, carry out much of the cell’s dismantling. Signals can start the process through cell-surface receptors or from within the cell; one internal pathway involves mitochondria releasing cytochrome c. As apoptosis proceeds, the cell shrinks, its DNA and nucleus fragment, and its membrane forms blebs and sealed pieces called apoptotic bodies. Nearby cells or macrophages engulf these fragments. Unlike cells that swell and burst after acute injury, apoptotic cells are dismantled in an orderly way.

Sources: https://www.cancer.gov/publications/dictionaries/cancer-terms/def/apoptosis, https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9922/

Key facts

  1. Apoptosis helps balance cell production and removal, maintaining adult tissues.Sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9922/
  2. Caspases activate one another in a cascade and cleave proteins involved in cell breakdown.Sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9922/
  3. In one internal pathway, mitochondrial cytochrome c helps activate caspase-9 with Apaf-1.Sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9922/
  4. In *C. elegans*, 131 of 1,090 somatic cells are eliminated through programmed cell death during development.Sources: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK9922/
  5. Apoptosis may be blocked in cancer cells, allowing abnormal cells to persist.Sources: https://www.cancer.gov/publications/dictionaries/cancer-terms/def/apoptosis
Source reference details
  • apoptosis — Definition of apoptosis — National Cancer Institute, National Institutes of Health
    Defines apoptosis as a series of molecular steps leading to cell death; supports its role in removing unneeded or abnormal cells and the note that apoptosis may be blocked in cancer cells.
  • Regulation of Programmed Cell Death — The Cell: A Molecular Approach — NCBI Bookshelf, National Library of Medicine; textbook originally published by Sinauer Associates
    Supports developmental and tissue-maintenance roles, digit tissue removal, caspase cascades, cytochrome c and Apaf-1 involvement, apoptotic morphology and clearance, distinction from acute injury, and the *C. elegans* cell count.

Fluorine

Needs review

Original description: Fluorine is the chemical element with atomic number 9 and the highest electronegativity, whose fluoride compounds are used in dental-cavity prevention.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-fccfabe44c9c0af8cdd3 · Estimated cost: $0.0248

Overview

Fluorine is a chemical element with symbol F and atomic number 9. On the Pauling scale, it has the highest electronegativity value, meaning its atoms strongly attract shared electrons in chemical bonds. At room temperature, pure fluorine is a very pale yellow-green, highly reactive gas. This hazardous elemental form is different from fluoride, the negatively charged ion found in many compounds. Fluoride compounds are used in toothpaste and some drinking water to help prevent tooth decay. In the mouth, fluoride helps replace minerals lost when acids attack teeth and makes enamel harder to dissolve, showing how fluorine-containing compounds can have useful roles even though elemental fluorine is dangerous.

Sources: https://periodic-table.rsc.org/element/9/, https://chem.libretexts.org/Courses/Northern_Michigan_University/CH_215:_Chemistry_of_the_Elements_Fall_2023/02:_Acid-Base_and_Donor-Acceptor_Chemistry/2.02:_Electronegativity, https://cdc.gov/oral-health/prevention/about-fluoride.html

Key facts

  1. Fluorine is in group 17 and period 2 of the periodic table; its atomic number is 9.Sources: https://periodic-table.rsc.org/element/9/
  2. Fluorine has an electronegativity of 3.98 on the Pauling scale—the highest value on that scale.Sources: https://periodic-table.rsc.org/element/9/, https://chem.libretexts.org/Courses/Northern_Michigan_University/CH_215:_Chemistry_of_the_Elements_Fall_2023/02:_Acid-Base_and_Donor-Acceptor_Chemistry/2.02:_Electronegativity
  3. Elemental fluorine is a dangerously reactive gas; fluoride is the ion present in many compounds used in dental products.Sources: https://periodic-table.rsc.org/element/9/, https://cdc.gov/oral-health/prevention/about-fluoride.html
  4. Fluoride helps replace minerals lost from teeth and makes the outer enamel layer harder to dissolve.Sources: https://cdc.gov/oral-health/prevention/about-fluoride.html
  5. Chemist Henri Moissan isolated fluorine in 1886 by electrolysis of potassium bifluoride dissolved in liquid hydrogen fluoride.Sources: https://periodic-table.rsc.org/element/9/
Source reference details
  • Fluorine - Element information, properties and uses | Periodic Table — Royal Society of Chemistry
    Atomic number, group and period; room-temperature appearance and reactivity; fluoride uses; history of Moissan’s isolation of fluorine.
  • 2.2: Electronegativity — Chemistry LibreTexts; authored by Kathryn A. Newton, Northern Michigan University
    Defines electronegativity as attraction for electrons in a bond and identifies fluorine as the most electronegative element, with a Pauling value of 3.98.
  • About Fluoride — Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, Division of Oral Health
    Explains fluoride’s role in replacing minerals lost from teeth, strengthening enamel against acid, and use in fluoride toothpaste.

Social Science and Philosophy

Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz

Needs review

Original description: German polymath and rationalist philosopher known for the doctrines of monads and pre-established harmony, the claim that this is the best of all possible worlds, and the independent development of calculus.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-4d6dd331abd2f9ce3137 · Estimated cost: $0.0152

Overview

Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716) was a German philosopher, mathematician, and polymath whose work ranged across metaphysics, logic, and mathematics. A leading rationalist, he argued that reality is grounded in simple, immaterial substances called monads. Though they do not directly affect one another, God’s “pre-established harmony” makes their independent activity appear coordinated. In his 1710 Theodicy, Leibniz defended the religious-philosophical claim that God chose the best among possible worlds, addressing how evil could exist in such a world. He also developed calculus independently of Isaac Newton; Leibniz’s differential and integral notation helped shape the symbols still used in mathematics.

Sources: https://iep.utm.edu/leib-ove/, https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk/Biographies/Leibniz/

Key facts

  1. He was born in Leipzig on 1 July 1646 and died in Hannover on 14 November 1716.Sources: https://www.uni-hannover.de/en/universitaet/profil/leibniz/lebenslauf
  2. Leibniz’s monads were simple, immaterial substances that, in his theory, did not directly interact; their coordination was explained by pre-established harmony.Sources: https://iep.utm.edu/leib-ove/
  3. Published in 1710, his Theodicy defended the thesis that this is the best of all possible worlds and addressed the problem of evil.Sources: https://iep.utm.edu/leib-ove/
  4. In 1714, he wrote the Monadology, a compact summary of his philosophical views.Sources: https://iep.utm.edu/leib-ove/
  5. Leibniz developed calculus independently of Newton and published his differential notation in 1684 and integral notation in 1686.Sources: https://iep.utm.edu/leib-ove/, https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk/Biographies/Leibniz/
Source reference details

    Leibniz’s rationalist philosophy; monads and pre-established harmony; the Theodicy, best-of-all-worlds thesis, and problem of evil; Monadology; independent development of calculus.
  • Gottfried Wilhelm von Leibniz — MacTutor History of Mathematics, University of St Andrews
    Biographical and mathematical context, including Leibniz’s calculus notation and its publication.
  • The Life and Work of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz — Leibniz University Hannover
    Leibniz’s birth and death dates and locations.

Arthashastra

Needs review

Original description: An ancient Indian Sanskrit treatise on governance, economics, diplomacy, espionage, and military strategy, traditionally attributed to Kautilya (Chanakya).

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-a611ea39698c5a7fd0ad · Estimated cost: $0.0368

Overview

The Arthaśāstra (also written Arthashastra) is a Sanskrit work on statecraft: how rulers organize and sustain a kingdom. Tradition associates it with Kautilya, also known as Chanakya, but scholars question who composed the surviving text and when. Its fifteen books range across royal administration, law, taxation and economic activity, diplomacy, espionage, and war. Rather than simply describing how a kingdom operated, the work offers practical guidance for governing, building resources, and dealing with rivals. Its wide-ranging treatment of political and economic questions makes it an important text for studying ancient South Asian political thought.

Sources: https://academic.oup.com/book/8486, https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/history-of-the-arthasastra/history-of-the-arthasastra/248B111B1F9F821D1D0C58B35FE23BD1, https://southasia.ucla.edu/history-politics/ancient-india/kautilya-and-arthashastra/

Key facts

  1. The title is associated with material gain or the science of polity; the work focuses on governing a state, not just personal wealth.Sources: https://southasia.ucla.edu/history-politics/ancient-india/kautilya-and-arthashastra/, https://academic.oup.com/book/8486
  2. Kautilya is the traditional author; the surviving text’s authorship and development remain debated.Sources: https://southasia.ucla.edu/history-politics/ancient-india/kautilya-and-arthashastra/, https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/history-of-the-arthasastra/history-of-the-arthasastra/248B111B1F9F821D1D0C58B35FE23BD1
  3. The work is a guide to statecraft, not a direct historical account of Mauryan administration.Sources: https://southasia.ucla.edu/history-politics/ancient-india/kautilya-and-arthashastra/
  4. Its topics include organizing government, raising revenue, maintaining law and order, and settling disputes.Sources: https://academic.oup.com/book/8486
  5. Its outline includes separate books on secret conduct, sixfold strategy, war, and relations with confederacies.Sources: https://academic.oup.com/book/8486

Model-reported concerns

  • Scholarly accounts differ on the text’s composition and redaction history; the overview avoids assigning it a firm date.

Federal Reserve System

Needs review

Original description: The central banking system of the United States responsible for conducting monetary policy, regulating banks, and promoting financial stability and employment.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-5bfa33839c0a3191c912 · Estimated cost: $0.0245

Overview

The Federal Reserve System, usually called the Federal Reserve or “the Fed,” is the central bank of the United States. Congress created it in 1913 to help provide a safer, more flexible, and more stable monetary and financial system. Its distinctive hybrid structure combines a Board of Governors in Washington, 12 regional Federal Reserve Banks, and the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC). The FOMC sets monetary policy, while the broader system also supervises banks, monitors financial-system risks, and supports payment services. The regional network gives the Fed a decentralized operating structure, while the Board provides oversight and the System remains accountable to Congress.

Sources: https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12594.htm, https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12593.htm, https://www.federalreservehistory.org/essays/fed-structure

Key facts

  1. President Woodrow Wilson signed the Federal Reserve Act into law on December 23, 1913, creating the Federal Reserve System.Sources: https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12594.htm
  2. The System’s three key entities are the Board of Governors, 12 regional Federal Reserve Banks, and the FOMC.Sources: https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12593.htm, https://www.federalreservehistory.org/essays/fed-structure
  3. The FOMC is the monetary-policy decision-making body; Congress has directed the Fed to pursue maximum employment and stable prices.Sources: https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12593.htm, https://www.federalreserve.gov/aboutthefed/fedexplained/monetary-policy.htm
  4. The Fed also supervises and regulates certain financial institutions, monitors financial stability, and helps operate payment systems.Sources: https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12594.htm
  5. Federal Reserve Banks provide services to banks and the federal government, but they do not offer banking accounts or services to individuals.Sources: https://www.federalreserve.gov/faqs/about_12593.htm

Charles Sanders Peirce

Needs review

Original description: An American philosopher, logician, scientist, and semiotician who helped establish pragmatism and developed the pragmatic maxim.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-85e349a4074d59ed4c77 · Estimated cost: $0.0245

Overview

Charles Sanders Peirce (1839–1914) was an American philosopher, logician, and working scientist whose career joined abstract reasoning with hands-on measurement: for 32 years he worked for the U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey. He is recognized as the principal originator of pragmatism, a philosophical method he first developed in publications of the 1870s; William James later helped popularize the movement. Peirce’s distinctive pragmatic maxim asks us to clarify an idea by considering the practical, observable consequences we would expect if it were true—not simply by repeating a definition. He applied this approach to scientific inquiry, and also developed influential work on logic and the theory of signs; later, he called his view “pragmaticism.”

Sources: https://plato.sydney.edu.au/entries/peirce/, https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/pragmatism/, https://liberalarts.indianapolis.iu.edu/centers/peirce-edition/index.html

Key facts

  1. Peirce was born in Cambridge, Massachusetts, in 1839 and died in 1914.Sources: https://plato.sydney.edu.au/entries/peirce/
  2. His 1878 essay “How to Make Our Ideas Clear” gives a canonical statement of the pragmatic maxim.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/pragmatism/, https://plato.sydney.edu.au/entries/peirce/
  3. Peirce worked for the U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey for 32 years, conducting geodetic investigations and scientific measurements.Sources: https://plato.sydney.edu.au/entries/peirce/
  4. His work ranged beyond pragmatism and logic to a general theory of signs, often called “semeiotic.”Sources: https://plato.sydney.edu.au/entries/peirce/, https://liberalarts.indianapolis.iu.edu/centers/peirce-edition/index.html
  5. Peirce and William James differed in their versions of pragmatism; Peirce later used “pragmaticism” to distinguish his own view.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/pragmatism/, https://plato.sydney.edu.au/entries/peirce/
Source reference details
  • Charles Sanders Peirce — Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy
    Biography, 32-year Coast and Geodetic Survey career, scientific work, logic, theory of signs, and Peirce’s later use of “pragmaticism.”
  • Pragmatism — Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy
    Pragmatism’s origins, Peirce’s pragmatic maxim and 1878 essay, its application to experience, and James’s role in popularizing pragmatism.
  • Peirce Edition Project — Indiana University Indianapolis, School of Liberal Arts
    Peirce’s role in originating pragmatism and the breadth of his work, including logic, philosophy, semiotics, and the sciences.

The Lucifer Effect

Needs review

Original description: A nonfiction book by Philip Zimbardo analyzing how situational and institutional pressures can lead ordinary people to commit harmful acts.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-1de44b6d1a5104204d80 · Estimated cost: $0.0356

Overview

Philip Zimbardo’s 2007 nonfiction book *The Lucifer Effect: Understanding How Good People Turn Evil* asks how ordinary people can take part in cruelty. It argues that situations, group dynamics, and larger systems—not only individual character—can influence harmful behavior. The book’s central case is Zimbardo’s account of the 1971 Stanford Prison Experiment, which he connects to prisoner abuse at Abu Ghraib, Iraq; it also considers ways people can resist harmful pressures and act heroically. A distinctive feature is this pairing of a prison simulation with a real-world abuse case. Readers should treat the book’s interpretation of the Stanford study as debated: later archival scholarship questioned its scientific validity and described concerns about how it was conducted.

Sources: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/195908/the-lucifer-effect-by-philip-zimbardo/, https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0032885508329773, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31380664/

Key facts

  1. Zimbardo, a social psychologist, wrote the book; the original edition appeared in 2007.Sources: https://books.google.com/books/about/The_Lucifer_Effect.html?id=vjeHCA6i4IAC
  2. The book focuses on the Stanford Prison Experiment and draws parallels with abuse at Abu Ghraib.Sources: https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0032885508329773, https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/195908/the-lucifer-effect-by-philip-zimbardo/
  3. Zimbardo’s central argument emphasizes situational pressures and group dynamics alongside individual character.Sources: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/195908/the-lucifer-effect-by-philip-zimbardo/
  4. The book also discusses resisting harmful influences and the possibility of heroic action.Sources: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/195908/the-lucifer-effect-by-philip-zimbardo/
  5. A 2019 article in *American Psychologist* used archival materials and participant interviews to raise concerns about the Stanford experiment’s scientific validity and conduct.Sources: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/31380664/
  6. The book received the 2008 William James Book Award.Sources: https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/195908/the-lucifer-effect-by-philip-zimbardo/

Meditations on First Philosophy

Needs review

Original description: A philosophical treatise by René Descartes that uses radical doubt to investigate knowledge, the self, God, and the distinction between mind and body.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-c65210e478f9b29e5e4d · Estimated cost: $0.0144

Overview

René Descartes’s Meditations on First Philosophy, first published in Latin in 1641, is a six-part philosophical work about what can be known with certainty. Its distinctive first-person, day-by-day format invites readers to follow a thinker who temporarily suspends belief in anything that could be doubted—not to celebrate doubt, but to seek secure starting points for knowledge. The sequence moves from doubts prompted by the senses and dreaming to the claim that thinking confirms the thinker’s existence, then considers God, truth, material things, and whether mind and body are distinct. Its significance includes making radical doubt a tool for investigating knowledge and rebuilding it from foundations.

Sources: https://pressbooks.library.torontomu.ca/meditationsonfirstphilosophy/, https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/descartes-epistemology/, https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/descartess-meditations/introduction-about-the-meditations/199C21F1082BD838EAD91574A05B71BF

Key facts

  1. The work first appeared in Latin in 1641 and is arranged as six meditations, presented like reflections across six days.Sources: https://pressbooks.library.torontomu.ca/meditationsonfirstphilosophy/
  2. In the First Meditation, dream experiences and the possibility of a deceiver help challenge confidence in sensory beliefs; this doubt is a method for seeking certainty, not an end in itself.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/descartes-epistemology/
  3. In the Second Meditation, the act of thinking supports the meditator’s conclusion that they exist, even while other beliefs are in doubt.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/descartes-epistemology/
  4. The later meditations examine God, truth and error, material things, and the proposed distinction between mind and body; these are arguments Descartes develops, not settled facts the book asks readers simply to accept.Sources: https://pressbooks.library.torontomu.ca/meditationsonfirstphilosophy/, https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/descartess-meditations/introduction-about-the-meditations/199C21F1082BD838EAD91574A05B71BF
  5. Descartes circulated the work for feedback, and objections and replies accompanied its first edition, making debate part of its publication history.Sources: https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/descartes-epistemology/
Source reference details
  • Meditations on First Philosophy – Open Textbook — Ryerson University
    Identifies Descartes as author, gives the 1641 publication date and six-meditation format, and lists the topics and titles of the six meditations.
  • Descartes’ Epistemology — Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy
    Explains the constructive purpose of methodical doubt, the dream and deceiver doubts, the cogito, and the publication of objections and replies with the first edition.
  • Introduction: About the Meditations — Cambridge University Press
    Summarizes the questions and progression of the six meditations, including the arguments about God, material things, and the distinction between mind and body.

  • Cited source has no matching open_page action: https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/descartess-meditations/introduction-about-the-meditations/199C21F1082BD838EAD91574A05B71BF

Sports and Games

Bodybuilding

Needs review

Original description: A physique-focused competitive strength and fitness activity involving muscular development through resistance training, nutrition, and staged posing.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-cf29ff7a2207deab1e17 · Estimated cost: $0.0364

Overview

Bodybuilding is both a way of developing muscles and, competitively, a judged physique sport. Competitors train to build muscular physiques, then present them onstage rather than trying to lift the heaviest weight. Judges compare features such as muscular size and symmetry, while set poses help competitors display different muscle groups; some contests also include individual routines. The sport’s modern history dates to the early twentieth century, although some accounts trace it further back. Its public history is closely associated with Eugen Sandow, a Prussian-born strongman who became famous in late-nineteenth-century Britain, promoted exercise routines and made posing a central attraction.

Sources: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10846881/, https://www.ifbbpro.com/npc-worldwide/rules/, https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/eugen-sandow-a-body-worth-immortalising.html

Key facts

  1. In the IFBB Pro League’s men’s bodybuilding rules, competitors perform a series of mandatory poses, including double-biceps, side-chest and abdominals-and-thighs poses.Sources: https://www.ifbbpro.com/npc-worldwide/rules/
  2. Those rules also provide for an individual posing routine to music in the finals.Sources: https://www.ifbbpro.com/npc-worldwide/rules/
  3. For men’s bodybuilding under those rules, judges compare a competitor’s overall balance of size, symmetry and muscularity.Sources: https://www.ifbbpro.com/npc-worldwide/rules/
  4. Eugen Sandow began as a strongman and later became known for posing and for exercise routines he promoted; the Natural History Museum describes him as a figure many consider the first professional bodybuilder.Sources: https://www.nhm.ac.uk/discover/eugen-sandow-a-body-worth-immortalising.html
  5. A scholarly article dates modern bodybuilding competitions to the early twentieth century, while noting that some accounts trace the idea to ancient Greek male beauty contests.Sources: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10846881/

Model-reported concerns

  • Judging criteria and required poses vary by organization and division; the rule examples here are specific to the IFBB Pro League’s men’s bodybuilding category.

    Men’s bodybuilding rules describe mandatory poses, comparison judging, scoring for size, symmetry and muscularity, and individual posing routines in the finals.
