World Series

Needs review

Original description: The annual best-of-seven Major League Baseball championship series between the American League and National League champions.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-8858e57d8ab69048e2b5 · Estimated cost: $0.0243

Overview

The World Series, often called the Fall Classic, is Major League Baseball’s championship showdown between the American League and National League champions. Its modern form began in 1903, when the Boston Americans played the Pittsburgh Pirates; that first contest was best-of-nine, not today’s format. After a 1904 cancellation, the Series became an annual tradition in 1905, and a best-of-seven format was permanently established in 1922, following earlier best-of-nine experiments. By bringing the leagues’ champions together to decide MLB’s season champion, the World Series has become one of baseball’s defining stages, where high-pressure performances and dramatic finishes can help shape players’ legacies.

Sources: https://www.mlb.com/postseason/history/world-series?ueid=e431d36fbc98fbc276aec4fe1bd8faf5, https://baseballhall.org/discover/shortstops/ticket-to-history, https://baseballhall.org/discover/still-a-classic

Key facts

  1. The first modern World Series was played in 1903 between the National League champion Pittsburgh Pirates and American League champion Boston Americans; Boston won in eight games.Sources: https://baseballhall.org/discover/shortstops/ticket-to-history
  2. The Series did not take place in 1904. It returned in 1905 and has been played annually except for the 1994 players’ strike.Sources: https://www.mlb.com/postseason/history/world-series?ueid=e431d36fbc98fbc276aec4fe1bd8faf5, https://baseballhall.org/discover/still-a-classic
  3. The best-of-seven format was permanently adopted in 1922, after several earlier best-of-nine Series.Sources: https://baseballhall.org/discover/still-a-classic, https://www.mlb.com/postseason/history/world-series?ueid=e431d36fbc98fbc276aec4fe1bd8faf5
  4. The 1903 Series helped build the American League’s standing by showing that its champion could defeat the best team in the National League.Sources: https://baseballhall.org/discover/shortstops/ticket-to-history
  5. “Fall Classic” is a nickname for the World Series.Sources: https://baseballhall.org/discover/still-a-classic, https://www.mlb.com/postseason/history/world-series?ueid=e431d36fbc98fbc276aec4fe1bd8faf5
Source reference details
  • Postseason History: World Series — MLB.com
    Provides the cross-league championship purpose, historical best-of-nine exceptions, the modern best-of-seven format, annual tradition since 1905, the 1994 interruption, and the Fall Classic nickname.
  • #Shortstops: Ticket to History — National Baseball Hall of Fame and Museum
    Documents the first modern Series in 1903, the teams and result, the 1904 hiatus, the return in 1905, and the Series’ significance to the American League.
  • World Series heroes of yesteryear come to life in Hall of Fame’s ‘Autumn Glory’ exhibit — National Baseball Hall of Fame and Museum
    Explains the best-of-seven format’s establishment in 1922, earlier nine-game experiments, the Fall Classic nickname, and the Series’ cultural and historical significance.

U.S. History

Marquis de Lafayette

Needs review

Original description: French aristocrat and military officer who supported the American Revolution, including the victory at Yorktown, and later participated in the French Revolution.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-a7bd2adc6c8144944c26 · Estimated cost: $0.0245

Overview

Marie-Joseph Paul Yves Roch Gilbert du Motier, Marquis de Lafayette, was a French aristocrat and military officer who became a leading French volunteer in the American Revolution. Arriving in America in 1777 at age 19, he served as a major general and formed a close bond with George Washington. In 1781, his forces kept pressure on British troops in Virginia while American and French armies prepared the siege that ended at Yorktown. Back in France, Lafayette took part in the French Revolution: he advocated a declaration of rights and commanded the Paris National Guard. His career connected two revolutions, though he favored constitutional change and later fled France as the Revolution grew violent.

Sources: https://www.mountvernon.org/library/digitalhistory/digital-encyclopedia/article/marquis-de-lafayette, https://www.nps.gov/arho/learn/historyculture/marquis-de-lafayette.htm, https://www.assemblee-nationale.fr/dyn/histoire-et-patrimoine/revolution-francaise/la-declaration-des-droits-de-l-homme-et-du-citoyen, https://www2.assemblee-nationale.fr/sycomore/fiche?num_dept=12264

Key facts

  1. Born in 1757 into a French noble family, Lafayette was 19 when he sailed to America in 1777 to join the Revolutionary cause.Sources: https://www.mountvernon.org/library/digitalhistory/digital-encyclopedia/article/marquis-de-lafayette, https://www.nps.gov/arho/learn/historyculture/marquis-de-lafayette.htm
  2. The Continental Congress appointed him a major general; he became a trusted officer and close friend of George Washington.Sources: https://www.mountvernon.org/library/digitalhistory/digital-encyclopedia/article/marquis-de-lafayette
  3. Before the 1781 siege of Yorktown, Lafayette’s troops harassed Cornwallis’s forces in Virginia as Washington and French general Rochambeau prepared to converge on them.Sources: https://www.mountvernon.org/library/digitalhistory/digital-encyclopedia/article/marquis-de-lafayette, https://www.nps.gov/arho/learn/historyculture/marquis-de-lafayette.htm
  4. During the French Revolution, Lafayette proposed a declaration of rights. The National Assembly developed the text collectively and adopted it in August 1789.Sources: https://www.assemblee-nationale.fr/dyn/histoire-et-patrimoine/revolution-francaise/la-declaration-des-droits-de-l-homme-et-du-citoyen, https://www2.assemblee-nationale.fr/sycomore/fiche?num_dept=12264
  5. He commanded the Paris National Guard during the Revolution; after political conflict and rising violence, he left France and was imprisoned by Austrian authorities.Sources: https://www2.assemblee-nationale.fr/sycomore/fiche?num_dept=12264, https://www.mountvernon.org/library/digitalhistory/digital-encyclopedia/article/marquis-de-lafayette

    Biography, American Revolutionary service, relationship with Washington, Yorktown role, French Revolution, flight and imprisonment.
  • Marquis de Lafayette - Arlington House, The Robert E. Lee Memorial — National Park Service

Ron DeSantis

Needs review

Original description: American Republican politician who served as governor of Florida and became nationally prominent for conservative cultural and public-health policies, including signing Florida's 2022 Stop WOKE Act.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-da1e219b1f0767928830 · Estimated cost: $0.0239

Overview

Ron DeSantis (born September 14, 1978) is a Republican politician from Florida who served in the U.S. House of Representatives from 2013 to 2018 and became Florida’s 46th governor in January 2019. Before entering politics, he served in the U.S. Navy and studied at Yale and Harvard Law School. His governorship drew national attention to conservative policy debates, particularly over education and race. A prominent example was House Bill 7, which he signed on April 22, 2022. Formally named the Individual Freedom Act and commonly called the Stop WOKE Act, it restricted specified concepts in certain school instruction and workplace training. That year, a federal judge blocked enforcement of parts affecting public-university teaching.

  3. He attended Yale and Harvard Law School and served in the U.S. Navy from 2004 to 2010.Sources: https://history.house.gov/People/Listing/D/DESANTIS,-Ron-(D000621)/

In November 2022, a federal judge issued a preliminary injunction blocking enforcement of parts of the law affecting public-university teaching; this describes that ruling at the time, not the law’s current legal status.Sources: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCOURTS-flnd-4_22-cv-00304/pdf/USCOURTS-flnd-4_22-cv-00304-0.pdf

Battle of New Orleans (1815)

Needs review

Original description: An American victory led by Andrew Jackson against British forces in January 1815 during the War of 1812, after the Treaty of Ghent had been signed.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-044566e45cc29073920a · Estimated cost: $0.0241

Overview

The Battle of New Orleans was a major battle in the War of 1812’s final campaign, fought on January 8, 1815, at Chalmette, downriver from New Orleans. British forces attacked an American defensive line commanded by Major General Andrew Jackson, but the Americans repelled them despite being outnumbered. The British aimed to capture New Orleans, a vital port and gateway to the Mississippi River Valley, making its defense strategically important. The victory became a lasting source of American national pride. Its timing is notable: the United States and Britain had signed the Treaty of Ghent on December 24, 1814, but its terms required ratification before hostilities ceased, and news had not reached the armies in time.

Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/chalmette-battlefield.htm, https://home.nps.gov/articles/treaty-of-ghent-1.htm, https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/treaty-of-ghent

Key facts

  1. The main battle took place at Chalmette Plantation, about six miles downriver from New Orleans.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/chalmette-battlefield.htm
  2. On January 8, 1815, Jackson’s American forces repelled a larger British force attacking their defensive line.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/chalmette-battlefield.htm
  3. New Orleans was a vital port and gateway to the Mississippi River Valley, which made it strategically important to both sides.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/chalmette-battlefield.htm
  4. The Treaty of Ghent was signed on December 24, 1814; its text stipulated that hostilities would cease once both sides ratified it.Sources: https://www.archives.gov/milestone-documents/treaty-of-ghent
  5. The victory became a source of national pride, and January 8 was celebrated for decades as a holiday.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/places/chalmette-battlefield.htm
Source reference details
  • Chalmette Battlefield — National Park Service, Jean Lafitte National Historical Park and Preserve
    Battle location, British attack, Jackson’s defense, relative force sizes, New Orleans’s strategic importance, and the victory’s national significance.
  • Summer 1814: Americans and British open peace negotiations at Ghent — National Park Service
    Treaty signing date, the delayed arrival of treaty news, and the battle’s place in the last phase of the war.
  • Treaty of Ghent (1814) — U.S. National Archives and Records Administration
    Treaty signing date and the treaty’s original provision that hostilities would cease after ratification by both parties.

Battles of Saratoga

Needs review

Original description: The 1777 American victories over British forces under John Burgoyne in upstate New York, a decisive turning point in the American Revolution that helped secure French support for the American cause.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-3e565ce68484c63de00f · Estimated cost: $0.0129

Overview

The Battles of Saratoga were two major engagements fought in upstate New York during 1777’s British campaign to capture Albany and control the Hudson corridor. British General John Burgoyne faced American forces led by General Horatio Gates: at Freeman’s Farm on September 19, the British held the field but failed to advance; at Bemis Heights on October 7, American forces drove them back. Surrounded and cut off from retreat, Burgoyne surrendered his army on October 17. The American victory helped persuade France to formalize an alliance, signed February 6, 1778. French aid later included troops and naval support, strengthening the American war effort.

Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/burgoyne-s-campaign-june-october-1777.htm, https://home.nps.gov/sara/learn/historyculture/battle-of-freemans-farm.htm, https://www.nps.gov/sara/learn/historyculture/battle-of-bemus-heights.htm, https://history.state.gov/milestones/1776-1783/french-alliance

Key facts

  1. Burgoyne’s plan called for advancing south from Canada to Albany, where he expected to meet another British force moving north.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/burgoyne-s-campaign-june-october-1777.htm
  2. At Freeman’s Farm, the British kept the battlefield, but their attack did not open the route to Albany.Sources: https://home.nps.gov/sara/learn/historyculture/battle-of-freemans-farm.htm
  3. On October 7, American forces captured Breymann’s fortified camp, weakening Burgoyne’s position; Benedict Arnold was wounded in the left leg during the fighting.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/sara/learn/historyculture/battle-of-bemus-heights.htm
  4. Burgoyne surrendered on October 17, 1777, after both attacks failed and his army was surrounded and unable to retreat.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/burgoyne-s-campaign-june-october-1777.htm
  5. News of the surrender helped bring about a formal French alliance; the two countries signed alliance and commerce treaties on February 6, 1778.Sources: https://history.state.gov/milestones/1776-1783/french-alliance
Source reference details
  • Burgoyne's Campaign: June-October 1777 — National Park Service
    British strategy toward Albany, the two failed assaults, Burgoyne’s surrender, and Saratoga’s role in helping secure alliances.
  • Battle of Freeman's Farm — Saratoga National Historical Park, National Park Service
    The September 19 battle, British control of the field, and the American strategic success in stopping Burgoyne’s advance.
  • Battle of Bemus Heights — Saratoga National Historical Park, National Park Service
    The October 7 fighting, the American capture of Breymann’s camp, Arnold’s leg wound, and the resulting weakening of Burgoyne’s position.
  • French Alliance, French Assistance, and European Diplomacy during the American Revolution, 1778–1782 — Office of the Historian, U.S. Department of State
    The timing and terms of the 1778 French treaties, the effect of news from Saratoga on Vergennes’s decision, and later French military and naval aid.

Davy Crockett

Needs review

Original description: American frontiersman, soldier, and politician who died defending the Alamo during the Texas Revolution in 1836.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-e006ba5369fb57ded2c2 · Estimated cost: $0.0128

Overview

David “Davy” Crockett (1786–1836) was a Tennessee-born frontiersman, soldier, state legislator, and U.S. congressman whose reputation as a hunter and storyteller made him nationally famous. After losing his 1835 congressional race, he traveled to Texas, joined the defenders at the Alamo in San Antonio, and died there on March 6, 1836, during the Texas Revolution. His significance lies both in his role in the Alamo’s defense and in his lasting place as an American folk hero: Crockett helped shape his public image, while later writers and popular entertainment embellished it with tall tales. Accounts disagree about the precise circumstances of his death, so familiar dramatic last-stand stories are not certain history.

Sources: https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/crockett-david, https://tennesseeencyclopedia.net/entries/davy-crockett/, https://www.tsl.texas.gov/treasures/republic/alamo/crockett-01.html

Key facts

  1. Crockett was born in Greene County, Tennessee, on August 17, 1786.Sources: https://tennesseeencyclopedia.net/entries/davy-crockett/
  2. He served in the Tennessee legislature and the U.S. House of Representatives, and opposed Andrew Jackson on issues including land reform and Indian removal.Sources: https://tennesseeencyclopedia.net/entries/davy-crockett/, https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/crockett-david
  3. Crockett helped make himself famous, but later publications—including comic almanacs—added fictional tall tales to his reputation.Sources: https://tennesseeencyclopedia.net/entries/davy-crockett/, https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/crockett-david
  4. He chose to join the Alamo defenders after traveling to Texas; he had not originally set out to fight for Texas independence.Sources: https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/crockett-david
  5. Historians cannot establish exactly how Crockett died at the Alamo; accounts differ over whether he died fighting or was captured.Sources: https://www.tshaonline.org/handbook/entries/crockett-david

Model-reported concerns

  • The precise circumstances of Crockett’s death at the Alamo remain disputed in the historical accounts consulted.
Source reference details

    Biography, political career, journey to Texas, role at the Alamo, death-date, and uncertainty about the circumstances of his death; distinguishes historical Crockett from later legend.
  • David "Davy" Crockett — Tennessee Historical Society
    Birthplace and date, Tennessee and congressional service, disagreement with Jackson, Crockett’s role in developing his public image, and the later growth of the legend.
  • David Crockett — Texas State Library and Archives Commission

Samuel Adams

Needs review

Original description: Massachusetts Patriot, revolutionary leader, and member of the Sons of Liberty who helped organize colonial resistance to British rule and the Boston Tea Party.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-76a0509a743f5333d316 · Estimated cost: $0.0265

Overview

Samuel Adams (1722–1803) was a Boston political organizer and Massachusetts Patriot whose work helped turn colonial opposition to British policies into a coordinated movement. He challenged Parliament’s authority to tax the colonies, wrote for newspapers, promoted boycotts, and helped organize Committees of Correspondence to connect resistance across communities. A leader associated with the Sons of Liberty, he chaired a Boston meeting on December 16, 1773, as colonists protested the Tea Act. Although Adams helped build support for resistance, the National Park Service reports no evidence that he joined the men who dumped tea from the ships. He later served in the Continental Congress, signed the Declaration of Independence, and became governor of Massachusetts.

Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/samuel-adams-boston-revolutionary.htm, https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/boston-tea-party-in-real-time.htm, https://history.house.gov/People/Detail/8317, https://www.archives.gov/founding-docs/signers-factsheet

Key facts

  1. Adams’s opposition to British taxation included organizing boycotts and writing political newspaper articles, sometimes under pen names.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/samuel-adams-boston-revolutionary.htm
  2. He helped establish Committees of Correspondence, which connected communities and supported coordinated resistance.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/samuel-adams-boston-revolutionary.htm
  3. On December 16, 1773, Adams chaired a meeting about the tea crisis; the National Park Service says there is no evidence he took part in the tea’s destruction.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/samuel-adams-boston-revolutionary.htm
  4. He represented Massachusetts in the First and Second Continental Congresses and signed the Declaration of Independence.Sources: https://history.house.gov/People/Detail/8317, https://www.archives.gov/founding-docs/signers-factsheet
  5. After the Revolution, Adams served as lieutenant governor and governor of Massachusetts.Sources: https://history.house.gov/People/Detail/8317
Source reference details
  • Samuel Adams: Boston's Radical Revolutionary — National Park Service, Boston National Historical Park
    Supports Adams’s political organizing, writing, boycotts, Committees of Correspondence, leadership at the December 16, 1773 meeting, absence of evidence that he joined the tea dumping, and later political career.
  • Boston Tea Party Timeline — National Park Service
    Provides context for the December 16, 1773 meeting and the Sons of Liberty’s subsequent destruction of tea.
  • ADAMS, Samuel — U.S. House of Representatives, History, Art & Archives
    Supports Adams’s service in the Continental Congress, signing of the Declaration of Independence, and roles as lieutenant governor and governor of Massachusetts.
  • Signers of the Declaration of Independence — National Archives and Records Administration
    Identifies Adams as a Massachusetts signer of the Declaration of Independence and provides his lifespan.

Trump family

Needs review

Original description: An American political and business family centered on Donald Trump and including Melania Trump, their children, and other prominent relatives.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-c2bc571318264b8b3c6d · Estimated cost: $0.0255

Overview

The Trump family is a U.S. business and political family whose public prominence grew from New York real estate into national politics. Donald Trump, born in Queens in 1946, followed his father, developer Fred Trump, into property development, took over the family firm, and shifted its work toward commercial real estate. After becoming a television personality, he entered politics and served as the 45th president from 2017 to 2021, then returned as the 47th in 2025—a nonconsecutive return to office. His wife, Melania, was born in Slovenia and became a naturalized U.S. citizen; she was first lady during both Trump administrations. Donald has five children: Donald Jr., Ivanka, Eric, Tiffany, and Barron.

Sources: https://www.whitehousehistory.org/donald-j-trump, https://www.aoc.gov/what-we-do/programs-ceremonies/inauguration, https://www.whitehousehistory.org/bios/melania-trump, https://www.whitehouse.gov/administration/donald-j-trump/

Key facts

  1. Donald Trump was born in Queens, New York, in 1946; his father, Fred, was a real-estate developer, and Donald was the second youngest of their five children.Sources: https://www.whitehousehistory.org/donald-j-trump
  2. Donald took over his father’s real-estate firm, renamed it the Trump Organization, and moved its focus from residential toward commercial projects.Sources: https://www.whitehousehistory.org/donald-j-trump
  3. Donald Trump served as the 45th president from 2017 to 2021 and as the 47th president beginning in 2025; the terms are nonconsecutive.Sources: https://www.aoc.gov/what-we-do/programs-ceremonies/inauguration
  4. Melania Trump was born in Slovenia, became a naturalized U.S. citizen in 2006, and is the mother of Barron Trump.Sources: https://www.whitehousehistory.org/bios/melania-trump
  5. Donald Trump’s five children are Donald Jr., Ivanka, Eric, Tiffany, and Barron.Sources: https://www.whitehouse.gov/administration/donald-j-trump/

Cornelius Vanderbilt

Needs review

Original description: An American business magnate who built major steamboat and railroad enterprises and became one of the wealthiest figures of the nineteenth century.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-905449d10e2dba4fef38 · Estimated cost: $0.0241

Overview

Cornelius Vanderbilt (1794–1877), widely known as “the Commodore,” was an American entrepreneur whose fortune grew first from ferry and steamboat businesses and later from railroads. Born on Staten Island, he began working on the waterways around New York Harbor and eventually became a major steamship operator. In the 1860s, he shifted toward railroads, consolidating lines into the New York Central & Hudson River Railroad, a powerful corporation. His transportation businesses made him one of the wealthiest Americans of his era. In 1873, he gave $1 million to establish and endow Vanderbilt University in Nashville; the university was his only major philanthropy.

Sources: https://www.history.com/articles/cornelius-vanderbilt, https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/a-history-of-the-hyde-park-estate.htm, https://www.vanderbilt.edu/about/history

Key facts

  1. Vanderbilt was born on Staten Island on May 27, 1794, and died in Manhattan on January 4, 1877.Sources: https://www.history.com/articles/cornelius-vanderbilt
  2. He worked as a ferry captain for businessman Thomas Gibbons in 1817 before building his own steamship and ferry businesses.Sources: https://www.history.com/articles/cornelius-vanderbilt
  3. “Commodore” was his honorific nickname, earned through his steamboating enterprise; it was not a formal naval rank.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/a-history-of-the-hyde-park-estate.htm, https://www.history.com/articles/cornelius-vanderbilt
  4. In 1867, he formed the New York Central & Hudson River Railroad, joining lines into a major railroad corporation.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/articles/000/a-history-of-the-hyde-park-estate.htm
  5. His 1873 gift of $1 million helped found and endow Vanderbilt University in Nashville.Sources: https://www.vanderbilt.edu/about/history

French and Indian War

Needs review

Original description: The 1754–1763 North American theater of the Seven Years’ War, fought primarily between Britain and its American colonists against France and its Indigenous allies.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-fe49733d0649fd837670 · Estimated cost: $0.0138

Overview

The French and Indian War (1754–1763) was the North American struggle within the wider Seven Years’ War, arising from British and French imperial competition, especially over the Ohio River Valley. France and Britain fought with colonial forces and Indigenous allies, but Native nations pursued distinct interests; they did not form a single side. The fighting widened into a major imperial war, and British forces ultimately took Canada. In the 1763 Treaty of Paris, France ceded its North American lands east of the Mississippi to Britain; Indigenous nations were not parties to the settlement, and the transfer intensified disputes over land. Britain’s victory and war costs also deepened colonial tensions that contributed to the later American Revolution.

Sources: https://history.state.gov/milestones/1750-1775/french-indian-war, https://www.nps.gov/subjects/teachingwithhistoricplaces/upload/Teaching-American-Indian-History-with-Primary-Sources-Compliant-1-2_508.pdf

Key facts

  1. The upper Ohio River Valley was a central flashpoint: France built forts there, and George Washington’s colonial forces tried to expel the French in 1754 but were defeated.Sources: https://history.state.gov/milestones/1750-1775/french-indian-war
  2. Indigenous nations made their own choices and pursued their own interests; the British had Indigenous allies, including the Iroquois Confederacy, while France also had Native allies.Sources: https://history.state.gov/milestones/1750-1775/french-indian-war, https://www.nps.gov/subjects/teachingwithhistoricplaces/upload/Teaching-American-Indian-History-with-Primary-Sources-Compliant-1-2_508.pdf
  3. British forces conquered Canada in 1759, a major turning point in the North American conflict.Sources: https://history.state.gov/milestones/1750-1775/french-indian-war
  4. The Treaty of Paris, signed February 10, 1763, ended the war; France ceded its North American territory east of the Mississippi River to Britain.Sources: https://history.state.gov/milestones/1750-1775/french-indian-war, https://home.nps.gov/teachers/classrooms/the-french-and-indian-war-1754-1763-how-did-the-war-end-unit-5.htm
  5. Indigenous nations were not parties to the peace settlement, though their lands were transferred; later British frontier policies and war expenses contributed to colonial discontent.Sources: https://www.nps.gov/subjects/teachingwithhistoricplaces/upload/Teaching-American-Indian-History-with-Primary-Sources-Compliant-1-2_508.pdf, https://history.state.gov/milestones/1750-1775/french-indian-war
Source reference details

    Conflict dates and global context; competing claims in the Ohio Valley; Indigenous alliances; British conquest of Canada; Treaty of Paris territorial terms; war costs and colonial discontent.
  • The French and Indian War 1754-1763: How Did The War End? - Unit 5 — National Park Service

Josh Hawley

Needs review

Original description: Josh Hawley is a Republican lawyer and U.S. senator representing Missouri, serving in the Senate since 2019.

Status: completed · Topic ID: study-fa360747d12bd43e9caa · Estimated cost: $0.0262

Overview

Josh Hawley is a Republican lawyer and U.S. senator representing Missouri. Raised in Lexington, Missouri, he studied at Stanford University and Yale Law School, then worked as a constitutional lawyer, including in cases before the U.S. Supreme Court. Before entering the Senate, he served as Missouri’s attorney general from 2017 to 2019. He began his Senate service on January 3, 2019, and is listed by the Senate as a current Missouri senator. His public profile is also associated with January 6, 2021: a House committee hearing described him raising a fist toward protesters outside the Capitol before they breached the building, characterizing the gesture as solidarity with them.

Sources: https://www.sos.mo.gov/cmsimages/bluebook/2025-2026/3_Federal.pdf, https://www.senate.gov/states/MO/senators.htm, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-117hhrg49356/pdf/CHRG-117hhrg49356.pdf

Key facts

  1. Hawley studied at Stanford University, graduating in 2002, and earned his law degree from Yale Law School in 2006.Sources: https://www.sos.mo.gov/cmsimages/bluebook/2025-2026/3_Federal.pdf
  2. He served as Missouri’s attorney general from 2017 to 2019 before taking his Senate seat.Sources: https://www.sos.mo.gov/cmsimages/bluebook/2025-2026/2_Executive.pdf, https://www.senate.gov/states/MO/senators.htm
  3. The Senate lists Hawley as a Republican senator for Missouri, with service beginning January 3, 2019.Sources: https://www.senate.gov/states/MO/senators.htm
  4. Missouri’s official 2025–2026 manual says Hawley argued cases before the U.S. Supreme Court and was a lead attorney in Hobby Lobby and Hosanna-Tabor.Sources: https://www.sos.mo.gov/cmsimages/bluebook/2025-2026/3_Federal.pdf
  5. At a July 21, 2022, House committee hearing, Representative Elaine Luria described Hawley raising his fist toward protesters at the Capitol on January 6, 2021; this is the committee’s characterization of the gesture.Sources: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-117hhrg49356/pdf/CHRG-117hhrg49356.pdf
